# PREFACE
### TO
## THE FIRST EDITION.

---

In the lectures printed in this volume, an attempt is made to carry further in some particulars the line of investigation pursued by the author in an earlier work on *Ancient Law*. The fortunes of the legal system which then supplied him with the greatest number of his illustrations <note>The author refers to Roman Law, which was the primary focus of his previous book.</note> have been strikingly unlike those of another body of law from which he has now endeavored to obtain some new materials for legal and social history. 

The Roman Law has never ceased to be spoken of with deep respect, and it is in fact the source of the greatest part of the rules by which civil life is still governed in the Western World. The Ancient Irish Law, the so-called **Brehon Law** <note>The "Brehon Law" refers to the statutes and legal traditions that governed Ireland from the pre-Christian era until the 17th century, administered by a class of professional jurists known as Brehons.</note>, has been for the most part bitterly condemned by the few writers who have noticed it; and, after gradually losing whatever influence it once possessed in the country in—

<summary>The author introduces his comparative study of legal history, contrasting the enduring prestige of Roman Law with the historical condemnation and decline of the Ancient Irish Brehon Law.</summary>
<keywords>Ancient Law, Roman Law, Western World, Ancient Irish Law, Brehon Law, legal history, social history</keywords>

in which it grew up, in the end it was forcibly suppressed. Yet the very causes which have denied a modern history to the <term>Brehon Law</term><note>The ancient native legal system of Ireland, administered by a class of professional jurists known as Brehons.</note> have given it a special interest of its own in our day through the arrest of its development. This interest, the Author hopes, is sufficient to serve as his excuse for making the conclusions it suggests the principal subject of the Lectures now published, with the exception of the last three.

The obligations of the Author to various gentlemen for instruction derived from their published writings or private communications are acknowledged in the body of the work. However, he must express his especial thanks to the <term>Bishop of Limerick</term><note>Charles Graves (1812–1899), a mathematician and antiquarian who studied Ogham inscriptions and Brehon Law.</note>, and to Professor Thaddeus O'Mahony, for providing facilities of access to the still unpublished translations of Brehon manuscripts, as well as for many valuable suggestions.

The Lectures (with the omission of certain portions) have all been delivered at Oxford.

Since the first edition of this work was published, the materials for forming an opinion on the extremely obscure subject of the ancient Irish Family and its divisions (discussed on pages 208 and following) have been increased by the publication of an additional volume (the fourth) of the <term>Brehon Law Tracts</term> <note>The ancient legal system of Ireland, written in Old Irish and practiced by "Brehons" or judges until the 17th century</note>, translated into English. The late, lamented editor, <term>Dr. Alexander George Richey</term>, has printed a very valuable preface in this new volume, in which all the evidence bearing on the difficult questions at issue is considered. It may be consulted with much advantage.

H. S. M. <note>Henry Sumner Maine, the author</note>

# LECTURES

### ON THE

# EARLY HISTORY OF INSTITUTIONS.

---

## LECTURE I.

### NEW MATERIALS FOR THE EARLY HISTORY OF INSTITUTIONS.

The sources of information concerning the early history of institutions which have been opened to us during the last few years are numerous and valuable. On one subject in particular—which may be confidently said to have been almost exclusively investigated until lately by writers who had followed a false path—the additions to our knowledge are of special interest and importance. We at length know something concerning the beginnings of the great institution of property in land. The collective ownership of the soil by groups of men who are either actually united by blood-relationship, or who believe and assume that they are so united, is now entitled to take rank as an established primitive phenomenon. This was once a universal characteristic of those human communities whose civilization has a distinct connection to our own.

<summary>This introductory page establishes that recent discoveries have revealed the origins of property law, specifically highlighting that collective land ownership by kinship groups was a universal starting point for Western civilization.</summary>

<keywords>property in land, collective ownership, kinship, primitive institutions, legal history, land tenure</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the historical development of land ownership and the comparative study of ancient legal systems across different cultures.</meta>

# LANDED PROPERTY IN RUSSIA. LECTURE I.

connection or analogy. The evidence has been found on all sides of us, dimly seen and verifiable with difficulty in countries which have undergone the enormous pressure of the Roman Empire, or which have been strongly affected by its indirect influence. However, it is perfectly plain and unmistakable in the parts of the world peopled by the Aryan race where the Empire made itself felt very slightly or not at all. 

As regards the Slavonic <note>original: "Sclavonic"</note> communities, the emancipation <note>original: "enfranchisement"</note> of the peasantry in the Russian dominions in Europe has stimulated inquiries that formerly attracted only a few curious observers; consequently, the amount of information collected has been very large. We now know much more clearly than we did before that the soil of the older provinces of the Russian Empire has been, from time immemorial, almost exclusively distributed among groups of self-styled kinsmen. These people are collected in cultivating village communities which are self-organized and self-governing. 

Since the great reform of the current reign <note>Referring to the Emancipation Reform of 1861 by Tsar Alexander II, which abolished serfdom.</note>, the collective rights of these communities—and the rights and duties of their members toward one another—are no longer entangled with or limited by the manorial privileges of a chief landowner. There is also fresh evidence that the more underdeveloped outlying Slavonic societies are organized according to essentially the same model. It is one of the facts with which the Western world will some day certainly have to reckon: that the political...

<summary>This page discusses how the emancipation of Russian serfs revealed the ancient, self-governing structure of Slavonic village communities, which preserved communal land-tenure systems outside the influence of Roman law.</summary>
<keywords>Slavonic communities, Russian Empire, village communities, land tenure, Emancipation Reform of 1861, Aryan race, Roman Empire influence</keywords>

# LECTURE I. VESTIGES OF THE VILLAGE COMMUNITY

The ideas of such a large portion of the human race—including its ideas regarding property—are inextricably bound up with the notions of family interdependency, collective ownership, and a natural subjection to patriarchal power. The traces of this ancient social order in Germanic and Scandinavian countries are, I hardly need to say, considerably fainter and tend to become increasingly obscured over time. However, the re-examination of written evidence regarding ancient Teutonic <note>Teutonic: Relating to the ancient Germanic peoples.</note> life and custom proceeds without interruption. Incidentally, much light has been thrown on the early history of property by the remarkable work of Sohm, *The Constitutional and Judicial System of the Frankish Empire* <note>original: "Fränkische Reichs-und Gerichtsverfassung"</note>.

The results obtained by the specialized methods of G. L. Von Maurer have, in the meantime, been verified by comparison with phenomena discovered in the most unexpected places. The researches of M. de Laveleye, in particular, have been conducted over a very wide field. Although I disagree with some of the economic conclusions he has reached, I cannot speak too highly of the value of the materials he has collected and described in his recently published volume entitled *Property and its Primitive Forms* <note>original: "La Propriété et ses Formes Primitives"</note>. 

I have not observed that the vestiges left on the soil and law of England and the Scottish Lowlands by the ancient Village Community have been made the subject of any published work since the monograph...

<summary>The author discusses how ancient social structures, characterized by collective ownership and patriarchal authority, are being rediscovered through the scholarship of figures like Sohm, Von Maurer, and De Laveleye. He notes that while these traces are fading in Europe, they remain vital to understanding the history of property law.</summary>

# LECT I. CELTIC SOCIETIES.

of small Celtic societies, which have taken up more than their share of this country's interest—the clans of the Scottish Highlands—had admittedly retained many characteristics, especially the political characteristics, of a more ancient state of the world, almost until our own day. But the explanation is that all Celtic societies were, until recently, seen by those capable of observing them through a uniquely deceptive lens. A veil spread by lawyers—a veil woven from Roman law and from that relatively modern combination of primitive and Roman law which we call feudalism—hung between the Highland institutions and the keen, investigative spirit of the Scottish Lowlanders. A thick mist of feudal law hid the ancient structure of Irish society from English observation and led to groundless doubts regarding the authenticity of the laws of Wales. The ancient organization of the Celts of Gaul, described by Caesar with the greatest clarity and decisiveness, seemed to have completely vanished from France. This was partly because French society was exclusively examined for many centuries by lawyers trained in either Roman or highly feudalized law, but also partly because the institutions of the Gallic Celts had actually been subjected to the crushing machinery of Roman legislation. Indeed, I do not mean to say that this darkness has not recently shown signs of lifting. It has been re-

# CELTIC INSTITUTIONS. LECT. I.

recognized that the collections of Welsh laws published by the Record Commission, though their origin and date are uncertain, are undoubtedly bodies of genuine legal rules. Additionally, independent of the publications to which I am about to direct your attention, a group of Irish scholars—distinguished by a remarkable sobriety of thought and succeeding a school almost infamous for the unrestrained license of its speculations on history and philology—had pointed out many things in Irish custom which connected it with the archaic practices known to be still followed, or to have been followed, by the Germanic races. 

As early as 1837, Mr. W. F. Skene, in a work of much value called *The Highlanders of Scotland*, had corrected many of the mistakes regarding Highland usage into which writers exclusively familiar with feudal rules had been led. The same eminent antiquary, in an appendix to his edition of the Scottish chronicler, Fordun, published in 1872, confirms evidence which had reached me in considerable quantities from private sources to the effect that village communities with 'shifting severalties' <note>'Shifting severalties' refers to a system where land is held by individuals, but the specific plots assigned to them are periodically redistributed among the community.</note> existed in the Highlands within living memory. Quite recently, also, M. Le Play, Mr. Cliffe Leslie, and others have come upon plain traces of such communities in several parts of France. A close re-examination of the *Custumals*, or manuals of feudal rules plentiful in French legal literature, led further to some highly interesting

<header>LECTURE I. ANCIENT FRENCH COMMUNITIES.</header>

results. It clearly appeared from them that communities of <term>villeins</term> <note>Feudal tenants who were legally tied to a lord's land.</note> were constantly found on the estates of the French territorial nobility. Legal writers have always represented these as voluntary associations, which were favored by the lord because of the greater certainty and regularity with which their members performed suit and service for him. As a rule, when a tenant holding by <term>base tenure</term> <note>A form of landholding where the tenant performed manual labor or services for the lord.</note> died, the lord initially succeeded to his land—a rule of which there are clear traces in our English law of <term>copyhold</term>. But it is expressly stated that, in the case of an association of <term>villeins</term>, the lord did not reclaim their land, as he was supposedly compensated by their better ability to provide his dues. Now that the explanation has once been given, there can be no doubt that these associations were not actually voluntary partnerships, but groups of kinsmen; they were not, however, as often organized on the ordinary model of the <term>Village-Community</term> as on that of the <term>House-Community</term>, which has recently been examined in Dalmatia and Croatia. Each of them was what the Hindus call a <term>Joint Undivided Family</term>—a collection of presumed descendants from a common ancestor, maintaining a common hearth and sharing common meals for several generations. There was no <term>escheat</term> <note>The transfer of property back to the lord when a tenant dies without heirs.</note> of the land to the lord upon a death, because such a corporation never dies, and the succession is perpetual.

But by far the most instructive contribution to

# ANCIENT LAWS OF IRELAND. LECTURE I.

...our knowledge of ancient Celtic societies has been provided by the Irish Government through the translations of the <term>Ancient Laws of Ireland</term>, which have been published at its expense. The first volume of these translations was published in 1865; the second in 1869; and the third, enriched with several valuable prefaces, has only just appeared. Anyone interested in the studies currently occupying us could not fail to recognize the importance of the earlier volumes; however, there was much difficulty in determining their exact relevance to the early history of <term>Celtic institutions</term>. The majority of the law first published consisted of a collection of rules belonging to what we would call the <term>Law of Distress</term> <note>A legal process where a person takes possession of another's property to satisfy a debt or enforce a duty.</note> in our modern legal language. Now, in very ancient systems of rules, the <term>Law of Distress</term>—as I shall attempt to explain later—is undoubtedly entitled to a very different place than what would be given to it in any modern system of jurisprudence; nonetheless, it is a highly specialized branch of law at any stage of development. 

There is, however, another more permanent and more serious cause of complication in drawing conclusions from these laws. Until relatively recently, they were practically unintelligible; they were restored to knowledge by the original translators, Dr. O'Donovan and Dr. O'Curry—two very remarkable men, both of whom are now dead. These translations have been carefully revised by the

<summary>This page discusses the publication of the translations of the Ancient Laws of Ireland and the initial difficulties scholars faced in interpreting them, particularly regarding the specialized nature of the Law of Distress.</summary>

# LECT. I. ANCIENT LAWS OF IRELAND. 9

learned editor of the Irish text; but it is likely that several generations of Celtic scholars will have to exchange critiques regarding the language of the laws before a reader who approaches them without any claim to Celtic scholarship can be quite certain that they have the exact meaning of every passage before them. The laws, too, I hardly need to say, are full of technical expressions; and the greatest scholar who lacks legal training—and, indeed, to a certain extent even when they have had legal training—may fail to grasp the exact scope or limitation of meaning that distinguishes a word in popular use from the same word used technically. Such considerations suggest the greatest possible caution in dealing with this collection of rules. In what follows, I attempt to draw inferences only when the meaning and direction of the text seem reasonably certain, and I have avoided some promising lines of inquiry which would lead us through passages of doubtful meaning.

The value which the <term>Ancient Laws of Ireland</term>, the so-called <term>Brehon laws</term>, will possess when they are completely published and interpreted, may, I think, be illustrated in this way. Let it be remembered that <term>Roman Law</term>, which, next to the Christian religion, is the most abundant source of the rules governing actual conduct throughout Western Europe, is descended from a small body of <term>Aryan customs</term> re-

# ANCIENT ROMAN AND IRISH LAW. LECT. I. 10

reduced to writing in the fifth century before Christ and known as the Twelve Tables of Rome. Let it further be remembered that this law was at first expanded and developed, not at all or only very slightly by legislation, but by a process which we can still see operating in various communities—the juridical interpretation of authoritative texts by successive generations of learned men. Now, the largest collection of Irish legal rules that has come down to us claims to be an ancient Code, with an appendage of later glosses and commentaries; and if its authenticity could be fully established, this ancient Irish Code would correspond historically to the Twelve Tables of Rome and to many similar bodies of written rules that appear in the early history of Aryan societies. There is reason, however, to think that its claims to antiquity cannot be sustained to their full extent, and that the Code itself is an accretion of rules that have clustered around an older nucleus. But that some such kernel, or perhaps several such kernels, of written law existed is highly probable, and it is also probable that the whole of the <term>Brehon law</term> consists of them and of the accumulations formed upon them. It is further probable that the process by which these accumulations were formed was, as in the infancy of the Roman State, juridical interpretation. According to the opinion that I follow, the interesting fact about

### LECT. I. INFLUENCE OF THE ROMAN EMPIRE.

the ancient Irish law is that this process was exclusive, and that none of the later agencies by which law is transformed came into play. The Brehon laws are in no sense a legislative construction, and thus they are not only an authentic monument of a very ancient group of Aryan institutions; they are also a collection of rules which have been gradually developed in a way highly favorable to the preservation of archaic peculiarities. Two causes have done the most to obscure the oldest institutions of the portion of the human race to which we belong: one has been the formation throughout the West of strong centralized governments, concentrating in themselves the public force of the community and enabled to give to that force, upon occasion, the special form of legislative power; the other has been the influence, direct and indirect, of the Roman Empire, bringing with it an activity in legislation unknown to the parts of the world which were never subjected to it. Now, Ireland is universally acknowledged to have never formed part of the Empire; it was very slightly affected from a distance by the Imperial law; and, even if it is admitted that, during certain intervals of its ancient history, it had a central government, assuredly this government was never a strong one. Under these circumstances, it is not surprising that the Brehon law, growing together without legislation upon an original body of Aryan custom, and formed

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>IRISH AND HINDOO LAW. LECT. I.</header>

beyond the limit of that cloud of Roman juridical ideas which for many centuries overspread the entire Continent, and even at its furthest reach extended to England, should present some very strong analogies to another set of derivative Aryan usages—the Hindoo law—which was developed in a similar way. The curious and perplexing problems which such a mode of growth suggests must be grappled with by the student of either system.

The ancient laws of Ireland have come down to us as a collection of law tracts, each treating a single subject or a group of subjects. The volumes officially translated and published contain the two largest of these tracts: the <term>Senchus Mor</term>, or Great Book of the Ancient Law, and the <term>Book of Aicill</term>. While a comparison of the <term>Senchus Mor</term> and the <term>Book of Aicill</term> with other surviving bodies of archaic rules leaves no doubt about the great antiquity of much of their contents, the actual period in which they took their current shape is highly uncertain. Mr. Whitley Stokes, one of the most eminent living Celtic scholars, believes—based on an examination of its verbal forms—that the <term>Senchus Mor</term> was compiled in or perhaps slightly before the eleventh century; and there appears to be internal evidence which, on the whole, allows us to attribute the <term>Book of Aicill</term> to the preceding century. The <term>Senchus Mor</term>, it is true, expressly claims for itself a much earlier

He introduces the primary ancient Irish law tracts, the Senchus Mor and the Book of Aicill, noting their estimated compilation in the tenth and eleventh centuries.</summary>

<header>LECT. I. DATES OF IRISH LAW-TRACTS.</header>
<page-num>13</page-num>

origin. In a remarkable preface, of which I will have much to say later, it gives an account, partly in verse, of the circumstances under which it was drawn up; it claims to have been compiled during the life and under the personal influence of St. Patrick. These claims have been cleverly supported, but I think it is not too bold to refuse to accept the fifth century as the date of the *Senchus Mor*. At the same time, it is far from impossible that the writing down of the ancient Irish laws began soon after the Christianization of Ireland. It was Christianity, a "religion of a book," which for the first time introduced many of the less developed nations outside the Empire to the art of writing. We cannot safely claim for the Celts of Ireland, in the fifth century of the Christian era, exactly the same degree of culture which Caesar attributes to the Celts of the Continent in the first century before Christ; but even if we could do so, Caesar explicitly states that although the Gauls were familiar with writing, they had superstitious objections to using written characters to preserve any part of their sacred literature, which would have included their laws at that time. Such objections would, however, necessarily disappear with the conversion of the Irish people to Christianity. On the whole, there is no inherent improbability in the tradition that, soon after this conversion, the customs of the Irish began to be stated in writing, and

<summary>The author examines the claim that the Senchus Mor was compiled under St. Patrick, concluding that while the 5th-century date is unlikely, Christianity probably introduced the literacy necessary to begin recording Irish laws.</summary>
<keywords>Irish law-tracts, Senchus Mor, St. Patrick, Christianity, Celts, Caesar, Gauls, literacy, conversion, legal history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of Irish law tracts, moving from their dating to their original literary form as verse.</meta>

<header>ANCIENT LAW IN VERSE. LECT. I.</header>
<page-num>14</page-num>

Celtic scholars have found a significant amount of evidence that parts of these more ancient writings are embedded in the text of the <term>Book of Aicill</term> and the <term>Senchus Mor</term>.

It is extremely likely that the most ancient law was preserved in primitive verse or rhythmic prose. In the oldest Irish traditions, it is difficult to distinguish the lawyer from the poet, or poetry from general literature. Both in the <term>Senchus Mor</term> and in the <term>Book of Aicill</term>, the formal statement of the law is described as "casting a thread of poetry" around it, and the traditional authors of the <term>Senchus Mor</term> are said to have displayed "all the judgment and poetry of the men of Erin." Modern Irish scholarship has, in fact, discovered that portions of the <term>Senchus Mor</term> are indeed in verse. This phenomenon is not unusual. Mr. Grote, speaking of the elegiac poems of Solon and the natural precedence of verse over prose, says (*History of Greece*, iii. 119), "the knowledge as well as the expressions of an intellectual man, even in the simplest form, (at that time) adjusted themselves not to the limitations of the period and semicolon, but to those of the hexameter and pentameter." I believe there is no question that this ancient written verse is what is now called a survival, passing down to the first ages of written work from the eras when rhythmic meter was absolutely essential so that the memory could carry the massive burdens placed upon it.

<summary>The author explains that ancient Irish laws were originally composed in verse or rhythmic prose to aid memory before the widespread use of writing. This practice made lawyers and poets nearly indistinguishable in early Irish society, a phenomenon also seen in other ancient cultures like Greece.</summary>
<keywords>Senchus Mor, Book of Aicill, Irish law, Celtic scholars, Mr. Grote, verse, oral tradition, mnemonics, Erin, Solon</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the examination of the literary forms of ancient laws, moving from the use of verse to the physical layout of Brehon Law manuscripts and their similarities to other ancient traditions like those of the Sanskrit literature.</meta>

<header>LECT I. FORM OF BREHON TRACTS.</header>
<page-num>15</page-num>

It is now generally agreed that the voluminous versified Sanskrit literature, which includes not only the poetry of the Hindus, but most of their religion, much of what serves as history for them, and even some of their law, was originally preserved by memory and made public through recitation; and even now, in the Sanskrit schools that remain, the pupil is trained in memory exercises that are little short of miraculous to an Englishman.

The tracts are of very unequal size, and the subjects they cover are of very unequal importance. But all alike consist of an original text, divided into paragraphs. Above or opposite the principal words of the text, glosses or interpretations are written in a smaller hand, and a paragraph is consistently followed by an explanatory commentary, also in a smaller hand, written in the space which separates one paragraph from the next. The scarcity of writing materials may perhaps sufficiently account for the form taken by the manuscripts; but the Celts seem to have had a special habit of glossing, and you may have heard that the glosses written by early Irish monks between the lines or on the margins of manuscripts belonging to religious houses on the Continent played a major role in the remarkable discoveries of Zeuss in Celtic philology. A facsimile of part of two Brehon manuscripts, one in the British Museum, and the other in the Library of Trinity

<summary>The author compares the oral preservation of Sanskrit literature to Irish traditions and describes the physical structure of Brehon manuscripts, which feature a primary text supplemented by extensive glosses and commentaries.</summary>

<header>FORM OF BREHON TRACTS. LECT. I.</header>
<page-num>16</page-num>

College, Dublin, may be seen at the beginning of the second published volume of the translations. It seems probable that each tract was the property of, and that it sets forth the special legal doctrines of, some body of persons who, in modern legal phrase, had perpetual succession—a Family or Law School; there is ample evidence of the existence of such law schools in ancient Ireland, and they are another feature of resemblance to the India of the past and, in some degree, to the India of the present.

The text of each of the published tracts appears to have been put together in one effort, no doubt from pre-existing materials, and it may have been written continuously by some single person; but the additions to it must be an accumulation of explanations and expositions of various dates by subsequent possessors of the document. I quite agree with the observation of the Editors that, while the text is for the most part comparatively consistent and clear, the commentary is often obscure and contradictory. Precisely the same remark is frequently made by Anglo-Indian Judges regarding the Brahminical legal treatises, some of which are similarly divided into a text and a commentary. Regarding the ancient Irish law, the result of the whole process is anything but satisfactory to the modern reader. I do not know that, in any extant body of legal rules, the difficulty of mastering the contents has ever been so seriously aggravated by the

<header>LECT. I. MANUSCRIPTS OF IRISH LAW.</header>
<page-num>17</page-num>

repulsiveness of the form. One of the editors has unkindly, but not unjustly, compared a Brehon tract to the worst kind of English law book, without even the moderate advantage of an alphabetical arrangement.

The exact date at which the existing manuscripts were written cannot be satisfactorily settled until they are all made accessible, which unfortunately they are not at present. But we know one manuscript of the *Senchus Mor* to be at least as old as the fourteenth century, since a touching note has been written on it by a member of the family to which it belonged:
> ‘One thousand three hundred and forty-two years from the birth of Christ until this night; and this is the second year since the coming of the plague into Ireland. I have written this in the 20th year of my age. I am Hugh, son of Conor McEgan, and whoever reads it, let him offer a prayer of mercy for my soul. This is Christmas night, and on this night I place myself under the protection of the King of Heaven and Earth, beseeching that he will bring me and my friends safe through the plague. Hugh wrote this in his own father’s book in the year of the great plague.’

The system of legal rules contained in these law tracts is undoubtedly the same as that repeatedly condemned by Anglo-Irish legislation, and repeatedly noticed by English observers of Ireland down to the early part of the seventeenth century.

<header>ENGLISH CENSURES OF BREHON LAW. LECT. I.</header>
<page-num>18</page-num>

It is the same law which, in 1367, a statute of Kilkenny denounces as 'wicked and damnable.' It is the same law which Edmund Spenser, in his '*View of the State of Ireland*,' describes as 'a rule of right unwritten, but delivered by tradition from one to another, in which oftentimes there appears a great show of equity in determining the right between party and party, but in many things conflicting entirely with both God's law and man's.' It is the same 'vile' and 'unreasonable' custom which Sir John Davis contrasts with the 'just and honorable law of England,' and to which he attributes such desolation and barbarism in Ireland, 'the likes of which was never seen in any country that professed the name of Christ.' It is not our business in this department of study to inquire how far this violent antipathy was politically justifiable. Even if the worst that has been said by Englishmen of the Brehon law down to our own day were true, we might console ourselves by turning our eyes to spheres of inquiry fuller of immediate promise to the world than ours, and observing how much of the wealth of modern thought has been obtained from the refuse which earlier generations had rejected. Meanwhile, happily, it is a distinct property of the Comparative Method of investigation to reduce national prejudices. I myself believe that the government of India by the English has been rendered appreciably easier by the discoveries which have brought home to the educated of both races the common Aryan parent—

<header>LECT. I. HISTORICAL CHARACTER OF BREHON LAW.</header>

Similarly, I am not afraid to anticipate that there will some day be more hesitation in repeating the insults of Spenser and Davis, when it is once clearly understood that the ‘ignorant’ institutions of the Irish were virtually the same institutions as those out of which the ‘just and honorable law’ of England grew. Why these institutions followed such different paths in their development is for History to decide; but, when it gives an impartial decision, I doubt much that it will wholly attribute the difference to native faults of Irish character. We, who are able here to examine coolly the ancient Irish law in an authentic form, can see that it is a very remarkable body of archaic law, unusually pure from its origin. It has some analogies with the Roman law of the earliest times, some with Scandinavian law, some with the law of the Slavic races, so far as it is known, some (and these particularly strong) with the Hindu law, and quite enough with old Germanic law of all kinds, to render worthless, for scientific purposes, the comparison which the English observers so constantly make with the laws of England. It is manifestly the same system in origin and principle as that which has descended to us as the Laws of Wales, but these last have somehow undergone the important modifications which arise from the establishment of a comparatively strong central authority. <header>ADVANCED LEGAL DOCTRINES IN BREHON LAW. LECT. I</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

gether disappoint the expectations of the patriotic Irishmen who, partly trusting to the testimony of Edmund Spenser—the least unkind of the English critics of Ireland, though one of the most ruthless in his practical suggestions—looked forward to it manifesting, when it was published, an equity and reasonableness which would put to shame the barbarous jurisprudence of England. Much of it—I am afraid I must say, most of it—is worthless except for historical purposes, but on some points it really does come close to the most advanced legal doctrines of our day. The explanation—which I will hereafter give at length—I believe lies in the method of its development, which has not been through the decisions of courts, but by the opinions of lawyers on hypothetical states of fact.

I think I may lay down that, wherever we have any knowledge of a body of Aryan custom, either prior to or but slightly affected by the Roman Empire, it will be found to exhibit some strong points of resemblance to the institutions which are the basis of the <term>Brehon law</term>. The depth to which the Empire has stamped itself on the political arrangements of the modern world has been illustrated in recent years with much learning; but I repeat my assertion that the great difference between the Roman Empire and all other sovereignties of the ancient world lay in the activity of its legislation, through the Edicts of the <term>Prætor</term> and the <term>Constitutions</term> of the

<header>LECT. I. ORIGIN OF SENCHUS MOR.</header>

With others, it introduced or greatly stimulated the habit of legislation; and this is one of the ways in which it has influenced the stubborn body of Germanic custom prevailing in Great Britain. But wherever the institutions of any Aryan race have been untouched or only slightly affected by it, the common basis of Aryan usage is perfectly discernible; and thus it is that these <term>Brehon law</term>-tracts enable us to connect the races at the eastern and western ends of a later Aryan world: the Hindus and the Irish.

I trust the lectures that follow will help to show what use the student of <term>comparative jurisprudence</term> may make of this new addition to our knowledge of ancient law. # PREFACE OF SENCHUS MOR. LECT 1.

St. Patrick did not decide the point himself; the narrator, in a truly professional spirit, tells us that he set the precedent according to which a foreigner from across the sea always chooses a legal adviser. He chose ‘to go according to the judgment of the royal poet of the men of Erin, Dubhthach Mac ua Lugair,’ and he ‘blessed the mouth’ of Dubhthach. A poem, undoubtedly of great antiquity and fame, is then put into the mouth of the arbitrator, and by the judgment embodied in it, Nuada is to die; but he ascends straight to heaven through the intercession of St. Patrick. ‘Then King Laeghaire said, “It is necessary for you, O men of Erin, that every other law should be settled and arranged by us as well as this.” “It is better to do so,” said Patrick.’ It was then that all the professors of the sciences in Erin were assembled, and each of them demonstrated his art before Patrick, in the presence of every chief in Erin. It was then Dubhthach was ordered to present all the judgments and all the poetry of Erin, and every law which prevailed among the men of Erin. . . . This is the Cain Patraic, and no human <term>Brehon</term> of the <term>Gaedhil</term> is able to repeal anything that is found in the Senchus Mor.’

<page-num>23</page-num>
### LECT. I. COMPOSITION FOR HOMICIDE.

...which God revealed to Dubhthach is that it was a middle course between forgiveness and retaliation. Retaliation prevailed in Erin before Patrick, and Patrick brought forgiveness with him; that is, Nuada was put to death for his crime, and Patrick obtained heaven for him. Today we maintain a path between forgiveness and retaliation; for as no one at present has the power of granting heaven as Patrick did then, so no one is put to death for his intentional crimes as long as the <term>eric</term> fine is paid. Whenever the <term>eric</term> fine is not obtained, he is put to death for his intentional crimes and set adrift on the sea for his unintentional crimes.”

It is impossible, of course, to accept the claim that this widespread ancient institution—the monetary fine levied on tribes or families for the wrongs done by their members—had its origin in Christian influences. However, it is highly probable that it succeeded simple retaliation. No doubt, in its time, it was at least as beneficial to the communities where it prevailed as was the stern royal administration of criminal justice to which sixteenth-century Englishmen were accustomed and of which they were so singularly proud.

But by the sixteenth century, it may well have outlived its usefulness, and so may have partially justified the attacks of its English critics, who generally had the <term>eric</term> fine for homicide in mind when they denounced the <term>Brehon law</term> as “contrary to God’s law and man’s.”

<header>SENCHUS MOR AND BOOK OF AICILL. LECT II.</header>

# LECTURE II.

## THE ANCIENT IRISH LAW.

The great peculiarity of the ancient laws of Ireland, as far as they are accessible to us, is discussed with much instructive illustration in the General Preface to the Third Volume of the official translations. They are not a legislative structure but the creation of a class of professional lawyers, the <term>Brehons</term>, whose occupation became hereditary and who, on that ground, have been designated—though not with strict accuracy—a caste. This view, which is consistent with all that early English authorities on Ireland have told us of the system they call the <term>Brehon law</term>, is certainly the one that would be suggested by a simple inspection of the law tracts currently translated and published. The <term>Book of Aicill</term> is probably the oldest, and its text is avowedly composed of the sayings of two famous lawyers, <term>Cormac</term> and <term>Cennfaeladh</term>. The <term>Senchus Mor</term> does, indeed, claim to have been produced by a process resembling legislation, but this claim cannot be supported; and, even if it could, the <term>Senchus Mor</term> would nonetheless consist of the opinions of famous <term>Brehons</term>. It describes the legal rules embodied in its

<summary>This page explains that ancient Irish law was not created by a legislature but was instead the work of a hereditary class of professional lawyers known as Brehons. It highlights the Book of Aicill and the Senchus Mor as primary examples of these legal tracts composed of the opinions of famous legal experts.</summary>
<keywords>Brehons, Brehon law, Book of Aicill, Cormac, Cennfaeladh, Senchus Mor, Irish law, legal history, hereditary caste</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Senchus Mor, specifically explaining how its text is composed of both pre-Christian "natural law" and Christian "scriptural law."</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>LECT. II.    INGREDIENTS OF THE SENCHUS MOR.</header>

text as formed of the "law of nature" and of the "law of the letter." The "law of the letter" is the Scriptural law, extended by as much of <term>Canon law</term> as the primitive monastic Church of Ireland can be supposed to have created or adopted. The reference in the misleading phrase "law of nature" is not to the memorable combination of words familiar to the Roman lawyers, but to the text of St. Paul in the Epistle to the Romans:

> "For when the Gentiles, who do not have the law, do by nature the things contained in the law, these, not having the law, are a law unto themselves."

(Rom. ii. 14.) The "law of nature" is, therefore, the ancient pre-Christian ingredient in the system, and the <term>Senchus Mor</term> says of it:

> "The judgments of true nature—while the Holy Ghost had spoken through the mouths of the <term>Brehons</term> and just poets of the men of <term>Erin</term>, from the first occupation of Ireland down to the reception of the faith—were all presented by <term>Dubhthach</term> to <term>Patrick</term>. What did not clash with the Word of God in the written law and the New Testament and the consciences of believers, was confirmed in the laws of the <term>Brehons</term> by <term>Patrick</term> and by the ecclesiastics and chieftains of Ireland; for the law of nature had been quite right except regarding the faith, and its obligations, and the harmony of the Church and people. And this is the '<term>Senchus Mor</term>.'"

<header>LEGISLATION AND ANCIENT IRISH LAW. LECT. II.</header>

forms the first volume of the ‘Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish,’ affirms, based on the evidence of ancient records, that the institutions which in some communities undoubtedly developed into true legislatures had their counterparts in the Ireland to which the laws belonged, and he does not hesitate to designate certain portions of the Irish legal system ‘<term>statute-law</term>.’ In the present state of criticism on Irish documents, it is not possible to strike an exact balance between the writers of the Introduction and of the General Preface; but there is not the inconsistency between their opinions that might appear at first sight. In the infancy of society, many concepts are found blended together which are now distinct, and many associations which are now inseparable from particular processes or institutions are not found coupled with them. There is abundant proof that legislative and judicial power are not distinguished in primitive thought; nor, again, is legislation associated with innovation. In our day, the legislator is always supposed to innovate; the judge never. But in the past, the legislator no more necessarily innovated than the judge; for the most part, he only declared pre-existing law or custom. It is impossible to determine how much new law there was in the <term>Laws of Solon</term>, or in the <term>Twelve Tables of Rome</term>, or in the <term>Laws of Alfred</term> and <term>Canute</term>, or in the <term>Salic Law</term>, which is the oldest of the so-called <term>Leges Barbarorum</term>—

<summary>This page explores the blurring of legislative and judicial functions in ancient Irish society, arguing that early law-making was often a declaration of existing custom rather than the creation of new rules. It compares the Irish system to other ancient legal codes like those of Solon, Alfred, and the Salic Law.</summary>

<header>LECT. II. NATURE OF ANCIENT LEGISLATION.</header>

barorum, but in all probability the quantity of new law was extremely small. Thus, when a body of <term>Brehon laws</term> (judgments) was proclaimed by an <term>Irish Chief</term> to a <term>tribal assembly</term>, it is probable that convenience was the object sought rather than a new legal authority. A remarkable poem, appended to <term>O'Curry's Lectures</term>, tells us how certain Chiefs proceeded every third year to the '<term>Fair of Carman</term>' and there proclaimed "the rights of every law and the restraints;" but it does not at all follow that this proclamation had any connection to <term>legislation</term> in the modern sense. The innovative legislatures of the modern world appear to have grown up where certain conditions were present which were virtually unknown to ancient Ireland—where the primitive groups of which society was formed were broken up with some completeness, and where a central government was established acting on individuals from a distance with coercive and irresistible force.

There are, moreover, some independent reasons for thinking that, among the <term>Celtic races</term>, the half-judicial, half-legislative power originally possessed by the tribal Chief, or by the tribal Assembly, or by both in combination, passed very early to a special class of learned persons. The Prefaces in Irish found at the commencement of some of the <term>law-tracts</term>, which are of much interest but of uncertain origin and date, contain several references to the order in Celtic

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of the Celtic literary and legal classes, moving from the general role of learned persons to the specific historical figure of the Druid.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>THE DRUIDS. LECT. II.</header>

society which has hitherto occupied men’s thoughts more than any other, the <term>Druids</term>. The word occurs in the Irish text. The writers of the prefaces seem to have conceived of the <term>Druids</term> as a class of pagan priests who had once practiced magical arts. The enchanters of Pharaoh are, for instance, called the Egyptian <term>Druids</term> in the Preface to the *<term>Senchus Mor</term>*. The point of view seems to be the one familiar enough to us in modern literature, where an exclusive prominence is given to the priestly character of the <term>Druids</term>; nor do the <term>Brehon</term> lawyers appear to connect themselves with a class of men whom they regard as having belonged altogether to the old order of the world. I am quite aware that, in asking whether the historical disconnection of the <term>Brehons</term> and the <term>Druids</term> can be accepted as a fact, I suggest an inquiry about which there hangs a certain air of absurdity. There has been so much wild speculation and assertion about <term>Druids</term> and <term>Druidical antiquities</term> that the whole subject seems to be considered as almost beyond the pale of serious discussion. Yet we are not at liberty to forget that the first great observer of <term>Celtic</term> manners describes the <term>Celts</term> of the Continent as before all things remarkable for the literary class which their society included. Let me add that in <term>Caesar</term>’s account of the <term>Druids</term> there is not a word which does not appear to me perfectly credible. The same remark may be made of <term>Strabo</term>. <header>LECT. II. CAESAR’S ACCOUNT OF CONTINENTAL CELTS.</header>

The source of at least a part of the absurdities that have gathered around the subject, I believe, is the *Natural History* of Pliny. These stories seem to belong to those tales about plants and animals to which a great deal of the nonsense written in the world can be traced.

You may remember the picture given by <term>Caesar</term> of the Continental <term>Celts</term>, as they appeared to him when he first used his unrivaled opportunities to examine them. He tells us that their tribal societies consisted substantially of three orders—two privileged and one unprivileged—and these orders he calls the <term>Equites</term>, the <term>Druids</term>, and the <term>Plebeians</term>. Someone has said that this would be a fairly accurate description of French society just before the first Revolution, with its three orders of Nobles, Clergy, and the unprivileged <term>Tiers-État</term> <note>The Third Estate</note>; however, that observation is much more clever than it is accurate. We are now able to compare <term>Caesar</term>’s account of the <term>Gauls</term> with the evidence concerning a <term>Celtic</term> community provided by the <term>Brehon</term> tracts. If we use this evidence as a test, we will soon conclude that, although his representation is accurate as far as it goes, it fails by omitting certain details. The <term>Equites</term>, or Chiefs—though to some extent they were a class apart—did not stand in as close a relation to one another as they did to the various <term>septs</term> or groups over which they presided. The mistake, so far as there was error, I believe to have been an effect of mental distance. It had the imperfections of the view obtained by looking on the <term>Gangetic plains</term> from the slopes of the <term>Himalayas</term>. The impression made is not incorrect, but a vast amount of detail is lost to the observer, and a surface varied by countless small elevations looks perfectly flat. <term>Caesar</term>’s failure to note the natural divisions of the <term>Celtic</term> tribesmen—the families and <term>septs</term> or <term>sub-tribes</term>—is to me particularly instructive. The theory of human equality is of <term>Roman</term> origin; the fragmentation of human society, and the unchecked competition among its members, which have gone so far in the Western Europe of our days, had their most efficient causes in the mechanism of the <term>Roman State</term>. Hence <term>Caesar</term>’s omissions seem to be those most natural in a <term>Roman</term> general who was also a great administrator and trained lawyer; and they are undoubtedly those to which an English ruler of <term>India</term> is most liable at this moment. There are a few very important points of detail to be noticed in <term>Caesar</term>'s description of what may be called the lay portion of <term>Celtic</term> society. I shall later call your attention to the significance of what he states concerning the classes whom he calls the clients and debtors of the <term>Equites</term>, and regarding the increased power which they give to the <term>Chief</term> on whom they are dependent. It is, however, remarkable that, when he speaks of the <term>Druids</term>, his statements are much more detailed. Here there were no home associations to mislead him, but, beyond that, it is plain that his interest was strongly aroused by the novel constitution of this privileged order whom he places alongside the <term>Chiefs</term>. Let me recall, then, the principal points of his description, from which I intentionally omit all statements concerning the priestly office of the class described. He tells us that the <term>Druids</term> were supreme judges in all public and private disputes; and that, for instance, all questions of homicide, inheritance, and boundaries were referred to them for decision. He says that the <term>Druids</term> presided over schools of learning, to which the <term>Celtic</term> youth flocked eagerly for instruction, remaining in them sometimes (so he was informed) for twenty years at a time. There are some strong and even startling points of correspondence between the functions of the <term>Druids</term>, as described by <term>Caesar</term>, and the office of the <term>Brehon</term>, as suggested by the law-tracts. The extensive literature of law just unearthed testifies to the authority of the <term>Brehons</term> in all legal matters, and raises a strong presumption that they were universal referees in disputes. Among their writings are separate treatises on inheritance and boundary, and almost every page of the translations contains a reference to the ‘<term>eric</term>’-fine for homicide. The method of choosing the chief <term>Druid</term> by election has its counterpart in the institution of <term>Tanistry</term>, which within historical times determined the succession to all high office in Ireland, and which was hateful to the English as it offered less security for order than their own less archaic form of primogeniture. Nor is this all. The Irish prefaces to the tracts contain a number of discussions on subjects which are in no way legal, or which are forced into some connection with law by the most extreme measures. They leave the impression of being a patchwork of materials, probably of very diverse antiquity, which happened to have been found in the archives of particular law schools. Now, the Preface to the *<term>Senchus Mor</term>* actually contains long discussions on all the matters about which <term>Caesar</term> declares the <term>Druids</term> to have been especially fond of debating. In one place it describes how God made the heaven and the earth, but the account is not in the least like the Mosaic account. It goes off, as <term>Caesar's</term> <term>Druids</term> did, into a number of extraordinary statements.

<header>COSMOLOGY OF SENCHUS MOR. LECT. II.</header>

‘*de sideribus atque eorum motu*’ [concerning the stars and their motion], ‘*de mundi ac terrarum magnitudine*’ [concerning the size of the world and the lands]. Among other things, it declares that God fixed seven divisions from the firmament to the earth, and that the distance he measured from the moon to the sun was 244 miles. ‘And the first form of the firmament was ordained in this way: just as the shell is around the egg, so the firmament is around the earth in fixed suspension . . . . there are six windows in each part of the firmament to let light through, so that there are sixty-six windows in it, and a glass shutter for each window; so the whole firmament is a mighty sheet of crystal and a protecting bulwark around the earth, with three heavens, and three heavens around it; and the seventh was arranged in three heavens. This last heaven, however, is not the dwelling place of the angels, but is like a wheel revolving around, and the firmament revolves in this way, as do the seven planets, ever since the time they were created.’ Parts of the passage reflect the astronomical ideas known to have been common in the Middle Ages, but much of it reads like a fragment of a pagan cosmology, to which a later revision has given a faint Christian coloring. The same Preface also contains some curious speculations on the etymology of law terms, and the Preface to the Book of <term>Aicill</term> explores, among other things, the question of the difference between genus and species.

<header>LECT. II. DRUIDS AND BREHONS.</header>

...which produced the class called the Druids among the Celts of the Continent, also produced the class known to us as the Brehons among the Celts of Ireland. Nor does it seem difficult to me to connect the results of these tendencies with other known phenomena of ancient society. There is much reason to believe that the Tribe-Chief, or King—whom the earliest Aryan records show standing alongside the Popular Assembly—was a priest and a judge as well as the commander of the army. Later Aryan history shows us this blended authority distributing or "differentiating" itself, and passing either to the Assembly or to a new class of custodians. Among the Achaeans of Homer, the Chief has ceased to be a priest, but he is still a judge; and his judicial sentences, θέμιστες <note>themistes</note>, or "dooms," however much they may actually be drawn from pre-existing usage, are believed to be dictated to him from on high. Among the Celts of both Gaul and Ireland, he has ceased to be a priest and probably also a judge, although some measure of judicial authority may still belong to his office as a "survival." The process of change thus differs from the one followed in Athenian history, where the institution of kingship survived only in the name of the King Archon, who was a judicial official; and from the process followed in Roman history, where the *Rex Sacrificulus* was a hierophant or priest. Meanwhile, the Popular Assembly, which

This evolution is compared to the historical developments of the Greeks and Romans.</summary>

<header>THE KING AND THE BREHON. LECT. II.</header>

virtually attracted to itself the whole civil and criminal jurisdiction of the kings among the Athenians, and which at Rome engrossed the whole administration of criminal justice through the commissions it appointed, seems to lose all judicial authority among the Celts. Perhaps I may be permitted to describe the change I believe to have taken place among the Celts of Ireland in this way: Themis, who in Homer is the assistant of Zeus and the source of judicial inspiration to kings, has (so to speak) set up for herself. Kings have delegated their authority to a merely human assistant, and we see by the story which begins the *Senchus Mor* that, even when a Saint is supposed to be present, the inspiration of which he is the source does not find expression through his lips, nor does it descend on the king; it descends on the professional judge. When we obtain our last glimpse of the class which has received this inheritance from Chief or King—the Brehons, Judges, or Authors of Judgments—they have sunk to the lowest depth of misery and degradation through the English conquest. At an earlier date, they are seen divided into families or <term>septs</term>, the hereditary law-advisers of some princely or powerful house. <header>LECT. II. THE KING AND THE BREHON.</header>

The <term>Tanaists</term> of the great Irish Chiefs—the successors elected from the kindred of each Chief to follow him after his death—are said to have occasionally served as judges; and one of the law tracts, still unpublished, contains the express rule that it is lawful for a king, although he is a judge himself, to have a judge in his place. Cormac Mac Airt, one of the traditional authors of the <term>Book of Aicill</term>, was a King in retirement. However apocryphal his story may be, it is of great significance to the student of ancient institutions. He had been accidentally blinded in one eye and is said to have been deposed from his royal office or chieftaincy because of the blemish. Coirpri, his son and successor (says the <term>Book of Aicill</term>), "in every difficult case of judgment that came to him, used to go and ask his father about it, and his father would say to him, 'My son, so that you may know'"—and then proceeded to lay down the law.

If, without committing ourselves to any specific theory concerning the exact extent of the correspondence, we can assume that there was substantial identity between the literary class which produced the law tracts and the literary order attributed to the Celtic races by <term>Caesar</term>, we not only do something to establish a historical conclusion that is perhaps more curious

The difference between the <term>Druids</term> and their successors, the <term>Brehons</term>, would in that case be mainly this: the <term>Brehons</term> would no longer be priests. All sacerdotal or religious authority must have passed, upon the conversion of the <term>Irish Celts</term>, to the <term>'tribes of the saints'</term>—to the missionary monastic societies founded at all points of the island—and to that multitude of bishops dependent on them, whom it is so difficult to reconcile with any of our preconceived ideas regarding ancient <term>ecclesiastical organisation</term>. The consequence would be that the <term>religious sanctions</term> of the ancient laws, the supernatural penalties threatened for their violation, would disappear, except insofar as the legal rules exactly coincided with the rules of the new Christian code, the <term>'law of the letter.'</term> Now, the lack of a sanction is occasionally one of the greatest difficulties in understanding the <term>Brehon law</term>. Suppose a man disobeyed the rule or resisted its application, what would happen? <header>LECT. II. SANCTIONS OF BREHON LAW.</header>

But then one cannot help constantly feeling that the compulsion is weak compared with the strictness of the process in modern Courts of Justice; and besides that, why should the man being distrained upon not constantly resist with success? Doubtless the law provides penalties for resistance; but where is the ultimate sanction? Caesar supplies an answer, which must, I think, contain a portion of the truth. He says that if a Celt of Gaul refused to abide by a Druid judgment, he was excommunicated: which was esteemed the heaviest of penalties. Another example I can give you of the lack or weakness of the sanction in the <term>Brehon law</term> is a very remarkable one, and I shall return to it later. If you have a legal claim against a man of a certain rank and you desire to compel him to discharge it, the <term>Senchus Mor</term> tells you to "fast upon him." "Notice," it says, "precedes distress in the case of the inferior grades, unless it is by persons of distinction or upon persons of distinction; fasting precedes distress in their case" (*Ancient Laws of Ireland*, vol. i. p. 113). ->FASTING AS A MODE OF COMPULSION. LECT. II.<-

with one widely diffused throughout the East, which is called by the Hindus '<term>dharna</term>.' It consists of sitting at your debtor's door and starving yourself until he pays. From the English point of view, the practice has always been considered barbarous and immoral, and the Indian Penal Code expressly forbids it. It suggests, however, the question—what would follow if the debtor simply allowed the creditor to starve? Undoubtedly the Hindu supposes that some supernatural penalty would follow; indeed, he generally gives definiteness to it by retaining a <term>Brahmin</term> to starve himself vicariously, and no Hindu doubts what would come of causing a <term>Brahmin's</term> death. We cannot but suppose that the <term>Brehon</term> rule of fasting was once thought to have been enforced in some similar way. <term>Caesar</term> states that the <term>Druids</term> believed in the immortality and transmigration of the soul, and considered it the key of their system. A <term>Druid</term> may thus very well have taught that penal consequences in another world would follow the creditor's death by starvation; and there is perhaps a pale reflection of this doctrine in the language of the <term>Senchus Mor</term>: 

> 'He who does not give a pledge to fasting is an evader of all; he who disregards all things shall not be paid by God or man.'

<header>LECT. II. IRISH AND HINDU LAW.</header>

Theoretically, I should state that a person who unjustly refused to submit to fasting had his legal liabilities considerably increased, at least according to the pronouncements of the <term>Brehon</term> commentators. However, such provisions only lead us back to the difficulty I first mentioned, and raise again the question of the exact value of legal rules at a time when Courts of Justice were not yet armed with the irresistible power to compel attendance and submission.

If we are justified in tracing the lineage of the <term>Brehon Code</term> to a system enforced by supernatural sanctions, we are able to contrast it in various ways with other bodies of law regarding its mode of development. It closely resembles <term>Hindu law</term>, because it consists of what was in all probability an original basis of <term>Aryan usage</term>, greatly expanded by a superstructure of interpretation built by a long succession of professional commentators; but it could not have possessed the same sacredness, and consequently the same authority, as <term>Brahmanical jurisprudence</term>. Both the Brahmins and the Brehons assume that Kings and Judges will enforce their law, and they emphatically urge its enforcement; but, while the Brahmin could declare that neglect or disobedience would be followed by endless degradation and torment, the Brehon could only assert that the unlearned brother who pronounced a false judgment—

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>IRISH AND EARLY ROMAN LAW. LECT. II.</header>

-ment would find blotches appearing on his cheeks, and that the Chief who allowed established usage to be abandoned would bring bad weather upon his country. The development of the <term>Brehon law</term> was again parallel to the path that there is strong reason to believe <term>Roman law</term> followed in early times. The author of the Preface to the Third Volume, from which I have more than once quoted, cites some observations which I published several years ago on the subject of the extension of Roman <term>jurisprudence</term> by the agency known as the *<term>Responsa Prudentum</term>*, the accumulated answers (or, as the Brehon phrase is, the judgments) of many successive generations of famous Roman lawyers; and he adopts my account as providing the most probable explanation for the growth of the <term>Brehon law</term>. But in the <term>Roman State</term>, a test was always applied to the "answers of the learned," which was not applied, or at least not systematically applied, to the judgments of the Brehons. We never encounter the Romans except as subjects of one of the strongest <term>central governments</term>, which armed the <term>Courts of Justice</term> with the force at its disposal. Although the Roman system did not function exactly in the manner to which our English experience has accustomed us, there can, of course, be no doubt that the ultimate criterion for the validity of professional legal opinion in Rome, as elsewhere, was the action of <term>Courts of Justice</term> enforcing rights and duties in accordance with such opinion.

<header>LECT. II. IRISH AND ENGLISH LAW.</header>

But in ancient <term>Ireland</term>, it is at least doubtful whether there was ever a <term>central government</term> in our sense of the words; it is also doubtful whether the public force at the command of any ruler or rulers was ever systematically exerted through the mechanism of <term>Courts of Justice</term>; and it is at least a tenable view that the institutions which stood in the place of <term>Courts of Justice</term> only exercised <term>jurisdiction</term> through the voluntary submission of intending <term>litigants</term>.

Perhaps, however, from our present point of view, the strongest contrast is between the <term>ancient law</term> of <term>Ireland</term> and the law of <term>England</term> at a period which an English lawyer would not call recent. The administration of justice in <term>England</term>, from library early times, has been more strongly centralized than in any other European country; but in <term>Ireland</term> there was no <term>central government</term> to empower the arm of the law. The process of the English Courts has for centuries past been practically irresistible; the process of the Irish Courts, even if it was compulsory, was at the most extremely weak. The Irish law was developed by <term>hereditary commentators</term>; but we in <term>England</term> have always attributed far less authority than does any European Continental community to the unofficial commentaries of the most learned writers of <term>text-books</term>. We obtain our law, and adjust it to the needs of each successive generation, either through <term>legislative enactment</term> or through the decisions of our

But, as I have already stated, the opinion I lean toward is that no part of the <term>Brehon</term> law originated in legislation. The author of innovation and improvement was the learned <term>Brehon</term>, and the <term>Brehon</term> appears to have invented at will the facts he used as the framework for his legal doctrine. His invention was necessarily limited by his experience, and therefore the cases suggested in the law tracts possess great interest, as they shed light on the society in which they were composed; but these cases seem to be purely hypothetical and intended only to illustrate the rule that happens to be under discussion.

As the law is administered among us (in England), the judge cannot move beyond the sets of facts presented before him or his predecessors. I do not think it can be doubted that this English practice of never declaring a legal rule authoritatively until a state of facts arises to which it can be fitted, is the secret of the apparent backwardness and barrenness of English law at particular epochs, as contrasted with the richness and reasonableness of other systems which it more than rivals in its present condition. It is true, as I said before, even of the <term>Brehon</term> law, that it does not wholly disappoint the patriotic expectations entertained of it. When they are disencumbered of archaic phrase and form, there are some things remarkably modern in it. I quite agree with one of the Editors that, in the ancient Irish Law of Civil Wrong, there is a singularly close approach to modern doctrines on the subject of <term>Contributory Negligence</term>; and I have found it possible to extract from the quaint texts of the *Book of Aicill* some extremely sensible rulings on the difficult subject of the <term>Measure of Damages</term>, for which it would be vain to study the writings of <term>Lord Coke</term>, though these last are relatively of much later date. <page-num>40</page-num>
<header>CHARACTERISTICS OF BREHON LAW. LECT. II.</header>

the various circumstances under which a wrong could be committed, and then to determine how some traditional principle of redress could be applied to the hypothetical cases. This indulgence of his imagination frequently led him into triviality or silliness, and resulted in an extraordinary multiplication of legal detail. Four pages of the *Book of Aicill* (a very large proportion of an ancient body of law) are concerned with injuries received from dogs in dog-fights, and they set forth in the most elaborate way the modification of the governing rule required for the owners—for the spectators—for the "impartial interposer"—for the "half-interposer," i.e., the man who tries to separate the dogs with a bias in favor of one of them—for an accidental bystander—for a minor, and for a person with an intellectual disability. The same law-tract also deals with the curious subjects of injuries from a cat stealing in a kitchen, from women using their distaffs in a woman-battle, and from bees, where a distinction is drawn between the case in which the sting draws blood and the case in which it does not. Countless other instances could be given; but I repeat that all this is mixed with much that even now has juridical interest, and with much which, in that state of society, probably had the greatest practical importance.

<header># LECT. II. ENGLISH CASE-LAW.</header>

by English writers on law that the method of stating legal principles with which our Courts of Justice have familiarized us is absolutely unique to England and to communities under the direct influence of English practice. In all Western societies, Legislation, which is the direct result of the commands of the sovereign state, tends more and more to become the exclusive source of law; but still, in all Continental countries, other authorities of various kinds are occasionally referred to. Among these are the texts of the Roman <term>Corpus Juris</term>, commentaries on Codes and other bodies of written law, the unofficial writings of famous lawyers, and other branches of the vast literature of law that hold, at most, a secondary place in the estimation of English Judges and the <term>Bar</term>. 

Nowhere, however, is anything like the same weight as with us attributed to a decided ‘case,’ and I have found it difficult to make foreign lawyers understand why their English colleagues should bow so unconditionally to what Frenchmen term the ‘<term>jurisprudence</term>’ of a particular court. From one point of view, English law has undoubtedly suffered through this reluctance to invent or imagine facts as the foundation of rules, and it will continue to show the signs of that damage until legislative reorganization and restatement fully reveal the stores of common sense which are currently hidden by its defects of language and form. <header># QUESTIONS OF FACT. LECT. II.</header>

seem to be closely connected with one of the most honorable characteristics of the English system: its extreme carefulness regarding facts. Nowhere else in the world is there the same respect for a fact, unless that respect originates from the English. This feeling is not shared by our European contemporaries and was not shared by our remote ancestors. It has been said—and the remark seems very accurate to me—that in early times, questions of fact were regarded as the simplest of all questions. Tests of truth such as <term>Ordeal</term> and <term>Compurgation</term> satisfied people's minds completely and easily, and the only recognized difficulty was the discovery of legal tradition and its application to the results of the test. 

To a certain extent, our own mechanism for determining a fact is undoubtedly also a mere artifice. We take the unanimous opinion of twelve men regarding statements made before them as our criterion of truth. But then, the method of convincing them, or attempting to convince them, is exactly what would have to be followed if one sought to obtain a decision based on evidence from the highest human intelligence. The old procedure was sometimes completely senseless and sometimes only distantly rational; the modern English procedure is, at most, imperfect, and some of its imperfection arises from the very constitution of human nature and human society. Therefore, I quite agree with the common professional opinion that its view of facts and its methods of ascertaining

<header>LECT. II. FACT AND LAW. 49</header>

them are the great glory of English law. I am afraid, however, that facts will always be the despair of the law reformer. Based on several of his expressions, Bentham seems to have supposed that if the English Law of Evidence were reconstructed according to his principles, questions of fact would cease to present any serious difficulty. Almost every one of his suggestions has been adopted by the Legislature, and yet inquiries into facts have become longer and more complex than ever. 

The truth is that the facts of human nature, with which Courts of Justice must primarily deal, are far more obscure and more intricately involved than the facts of physical nature. The difficulty of determining them with precision constantly increases in our age through the progress of invention and enterprise, through the ever-growing varied composition of all modern communities, and through the ever-accelerating activity of modern social movements. 

It is possible that we may see English law take the form for which Bentham hoped and labored; every successive year brings us slightly closer to this achievement. Consequently, however little we may agree with his opinion that all questions of *law* result from some judicial delusion or legal abuse, we may reasonably expect them to become less frequent and easier to solve. But neither facts nor the methods of determining them show any tendency to

<header>ANCIENT IRISH CUSTOMARY LAW. LECT. II.</header>

I have taken some effort to explain what kind of authority the Irish Brehon law did *not*, in my opinion, possess. The "law of nature" had lost all supernatural sanction, except so far as it coincided with the "law of the letter." It had not yet acquired, or had very imperfectly acquired, that binding power which law obtains when the State exerts public force through Courts of Justice to compel obedience to it. Did it, then, have any authority at all; and if so, what kind of authority? I attempted to provide part of the answer to this question three years ago (*Village Communities in the East and West*, pp. 56, 57); and though much more could be said on the subject, I will delay that until another opportunity. Insofar as the Brehon law declared actual ancient and indigenous practices, it shared in the persistent vitality of all customs when observed by a society organized into corporate natural groups. But, besides this, it had another source of influence over men’s minds in the bold and never-flagging self-assertion of the class which explained it. A portion of the authority enjoyed by Indian Brahminical jurisprudence is undoubtedly to be explained in the same way. The Brehon could not, like the Brahmin, make any such significant assertion as that his order sprang from the head of <term>Brahma</term>,

<header>LECT. II. SELF-ASSERTION OF BREHONS.</header>

But the <term>Brehon</term> did claim that St. Patrick and other great Irish saints had sanctioned the law which he declared, and that some of them had even revised it. Like the <term>Brahmin</term>, too, he never missed an opportunity to assert the dignity of his profession. In these law tracts, the heads of this profession are consistently placed—just as Caesar placed the <term>Druids</term>—on the same level as the highest classes of Celtic society. The fines payable for injuring them, and their rights to feast at the expense of other classes (a type of right which will require much of our attention later), are set at the same level as those of bishops and kings. It is more than likely that the believing multitude eventually accepted these claims. From what we know of that stage of thought, we can hardly limit the amount of authority spontaneously granted to the statements of a single literary class. It must have occurred to many that the influence of the equivalent class in our own modern society far exceeds anything that could have been predicted by simply looking at our social structure. There is perhaps a common impression that the influence it exerts increases as history progresses—an impression possibly created and certainly reinforced by the brilliant passages in which Lord
