<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>THE LITERARY CLASS. LECT. II.</header>

Macaulay contrasted the well-paid literary labor of his own day with the miseries of the literary "hack" of Grub Street a century before. I think that this opinion, if broadly stated, is at the very least doubtful. The class which—to use a modern neologism—"formulates" the ideas dimly conceived by the multitude (saving it mental trouble by collecting through generalization, which is an essentially labor-saving process, the scattered fragments of its knowledge and experience) has not always consisted of philosophers, historians, and novelists, but had earlier representatives in poets, priests, and lawyers. It is not at all a paradoxical opinion that these last were its most powerful members. For, nowadays, it has to cope with the critical faculty, found more or less everywhere, and enormously strengthened by observation of the methods of physical discovery. No authority of our day is possibly comparable with that of the men who, in an utterly uncritical age, simply said of a legal rule, "So it has been laid down by the learned," or used the still more impressive formula, "It is thus written."

While, however, I fully believe that the <term>Brehon</term> law possessed great authority, I think also that it was in all probability irregularly and intermittently enforced, and that partial and local departures from it were common all over ancient Ireland. Anyone who interested himself in the question of its practical

I do not know if the failure of the English, once they had thoroughly conquered the country, to enforce the <term>Brehon law</term> through the Courts which they established, has ever been counted among the wrongs of Ireland. But if they had done this, they would have brought about the very change which at a much later period they caused in India—ignorantly, but with the very best intentions. They would have given immensely greater force and a much larger scope to a system of rules that were only loosely and occasionally administered before the English armed them with a new authority. Even as it was, I cannot doubt that the English did much to perpetuate the Brehon law in the form in which we find it. Before I close this chapter, it is necessary to state that the <term>Brehon law</term> has not been unaffected by the two main influences which have made the modern law of Western Europe different from the ancient: Christian morality and Roman jurisprudence. It has been modified by Roman legal ideas to some degree, though it would be risky to attempt to state with any precision to what degree. I have reliable information that, in the tracts translated but not yet published, a certain number of Roman legal maxims are cited, and one Roman <term>jurisconsult</term> is mentioned by name. As far as the published tracts provide material for an opinion, I am inclined to think that the influence of the Roman law has been very slight, and I attribute it not to a study of the writings of the Roman lawyers, but to contact with Churchmen who were more or less steeped in Roman legal notions. <header>LECT. II. CONTRACT IN ANCIENT IRISH LAW.</header>

> ‘There are three periods at which the world dies: the period of a plague, of a general war, and of the dissolution of verbal contracts.’
> ‘The world is worthless at the time of the dissolution of contracts.’

At first sight, this looks much more like the doctrine of the eighteenth century than of any century between the sixth and the sixteenth. Let us see, however, what follows when the position stated so broadly has to be worked out. We come, in the <term>Corus Bescna</term>, upon the following attempt at classification, which I fear would have deeply shocked Jeremy Bentham and John Austin: ‘How many kinds of contracts are there?’ asks the <term>Brehon</term> text-writer. ‘Two,’ is the answer. ‘A valid contract, and an invalid contract.’ This, no doubt, is absurd, but the explanation appears to be as follows. The principle of the absolute sacredness of contracts was probably of foreign origin and was insisted upon for a particular purpose. It was therefore laid down too broadly for the actual state of the law and the actual condition of Irish Celtic society. Under such circumstances, a treatise on Contract necessarily takes the form, in great measure, of a treatise on the grounds of invalidity in contracts—on the many exceptions to an over-broad general rule. Anciently, the power of contracting is limited on all sides. It is limited by the rights of your family, by the rights of your

<header>CHRISTIANITY AND THE BREHON LAW. LECT. II</header>

distant kinsmen, by the rights of your co-villagers, by the rights of your tribe, by the rights of your Chief, and, if you contract in a way that adversely affects the Church, by the rights of the Church. The <term>Corus Bescna</term> is, for the most part, a treatise on these archaic limitations. At the same time, some of the modern grounds for invalidity are very well presented, and this merit may possibly be due to the influence of <term>Roman doctrine</term> on the <term>Brehon law-schools</term>.

Something must be said regarding the extent to which Christian opinion has influenced these <term>Brehon</term> writings. Christianity has certainly had a considerable negative influence over them. It was no longer possible for the <term>Brehon</term> to claim that a person who broke his rules would suffer a supernatural penalty, and the consequences of this change were undoubtedly important. But still, as you have seen, in the case of "fasting on a man" or "<term>sitting dharna</term>," the pagan rule remained in the system, even though its original meaning was lost. Again, one positive result of the Brehons' adoption of the so-called "law of the letter" appears to have been the development of a large body of rules concerning the territorial rights of the Church, and these constitute a very interesting department of the <term>Brehon law</term>. But there has certainly been nothing like a deep integration of Christian principles into ancient <term>Irish law</term>. If this kind of influence is to be looked for anywhere, it must be in the law of Marriage, and the

I do not question the accepted opinion that Christianity greatly mitigated and did much to abolish personal and <term>predial</term> <note>Slavery where the laborer is attached to the land, such as serfdom.</note> slavery in the West, but the Continental lawyers of whom I spoke considerably antedate its influence, and take far too little account of the massive effects subsequently produced by the practical equality of all men within the pale of the Catholic priesthood.

But I principally object to these statements, which in some countries have almost become professional commonplaces, for two reasons. They gloss over a very instructive fact—the great resistance to change inherent in all bodies of law; and they obscure an interesting and yet unsettled problem: the origin of the <term>Canon law</term>.

<header>KINSHIP AS THE BASIS OF SOCIETY. LECT. III.</header>

# LECTURE III.

## KINSHIP AS THE BASIS OF SOCIETY.

THE most recent research into the primitive history of society points to the conclusion that the earliest tie which joined men together in communities was Consanguinity or Kinship. The subject has been approached in recent years from several different perspectives, and there has been much dispute as to what primitive blood relationship implied and how it arose; but there has been general agreement regarding the fact I have stated. Perhaps the caution is needed that we must not form too loose a conception of the kinship which once stood in the place of the many influences that are now the cement of human societies. It was regarded as an actual bond of union, and in no respect as a sentimental one. The notion of what, for lack of a better phrase, I must call a moral brotherhood in the whole human race has been steadily gaining ground during the whole course of history, and we now have a broad abstract term

<vocab>Kinship, Consanguinity, primitive history, blood relationship, moral brotherhood</vocab>

<summary>This lecture introduces the theory that kinship, or consanguinity, was the primary bond that held early human communities together. It emphasizes that this ancient form of kinship was a literal and practical bond of union rather than a sentimental or abstract moral concept.</summary>
<keywords>kinship, consanguinity, primitive society, blood relationship, social evolution, moral brotherhood, human communities</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Lecture III, expanding on the primitive conception of kinship by contrasting it with modern ideas of humanity and identifying the specific racial groups relevant to the study of early jurisprudence.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>LECT. III.    PRIMITIVE VIEW OF KINSHIP.    66</header>

The most powerful of the agencies which have brought about this broader and looser view of kinship has undoubtedly been Religion, and indeed one great Eastern religion extended it until for some purposes it embraced all sentient nature. All this modern enlargement of the primitive conception of kinship must be discarded before we can truly understand it. No brotherhood was recognized by our savage forefathers except actual consanguinity regarded as a fact. If a man was not related to another, there was no bond between them. He was an enemy to be killed, or plundered, or hated, as much as the wild beasts upon which the tribe made war, belonging indeed to the craftiest and the cruelest order of wild animals. It would scarcely be too strong an assertion to say that the dogs which followed the camp had more in common with it than the tribesmen of an alien and unrelated tribe.

The tribes of men with which the student of jurisprudence is concerned are exclusively those belonging to the races now universally classed, on the ground of linguistic affinities, as *Aryan* and *Semitic*. Besides these, he has at most to take into account that portion of the outlying mass of mankind which has lately been called *Uralian*—the Turks, Hungarians, and Finns. The characteristic of all these races, when in the tribal state, is that the tribes themselves,

The method of forming groups of kinsmen which they see proceeding before their eyes is believed to be identical to the process by which the community itself was formed. Thus, the theoretical assumption is that all the tribesmen are descended from some common ancestor, whose descendants have formed subgroups, which in turn have branched off into others, until the smallest group of all, the existing family, is reached. I believe I may say that there is substantial agreement regarding the correctness of these statements as long as they are restricted to the Aryan, Semitic, and Uralian races. At most, it is asserted that among the recorded customs of parts of these races, there are obscure indications of a different and earlier state of affairs. Mr. Lewis Morgan, of New York, the author of a remarkable and very magnificent volume on 'Systems of <term>Consanguinity</term> and <term>Affinity</term> <note>relationship by marriage</note> in the Human Family,' published by the Smithsonian Institute at Washington, identifies no fewer than ten stages (p. 486) through which communities founded on kinship have passed before the development of that form of the family from which the Aryan tribes believe they originated. But Mr. Morgan also says of the system known from the evidence actually prevailing among the Aryan, Semitic, and Uralian divisions of mankind that (p. 469) it 'manifestly proceeds upon the assumption of the existence of marriage between single pairs, and of the certainty of parentage through the marriage relation.' 'Hence,' he adds, 'it must have come into existence after the establishment of marriage between single pairs.'

A point of significant importance to the student of ancient customs must now be made regarding the bond of union recognized by these major races. Kinship, as the tie binding communities together, tends to be regarded as the same thing as submission to a common authority. The notions of Power and <term>Consanguinity</term> blend, but they in no way replace one another. We have a familiar example of this mixture of ideas in the subordination of the smallest group, the Family, to its patriarchal head. Wherever

In some cases, the Tribe can hardly be described as anything other than a group of men subject to one specific chieftain. This peculiar blending of ideas is undoubtedly connected with the expansion (a fact familiar to most of us) of the size of ancient kinship groups through artificial means or fictions. Just as we find the Family recruited by strangers brought under the paternal power of its head by <term>adoption</term>, so we find the Tribe, or Clan, including a number of persons who are in theory related to it, yet in fact are connected with it only by their common dependence on the Chief. I do not claim to provide any simple explanation for the subjection of various groups of kindred to forms of power for which the <term>patriarchal power</term> of the head of the family is the model. Doubtless, it is partly accounted for by deep-seated instincts. But Mr. Morgan's research seems to me to have supplied another partial explanation. Every man is related to an extraordinary number of men called his brothers, to an extraordinary number called his sons, and to an extraordinary number called his uncles. Mr. Morgan explains this fact in his own way, but he points out the practical convenience served by this method of classification and nomenclature. Though the point may not strike us at first, kinship is a clumsy basis for communities of any size because of the difficulty which the mind—and particularly the uneducated mind—has in embracing all the persons bound to any one man by blood ties, and who are therefore (which is the important matter) connected with him by common responsibilities and rights. A broad extension and significant loosening of the notion of kinship overcomes this difficulty among the lower races, but it may be that, among the higher, <term>Patriarchal Power</term> serves the same purpose. It simplifies the concepts of kinship and of joint responsibility, first in the <term>Patriarchal Family</term> and ultimately in the <term>Clan</term> or <term>Tribe</term>.

We must next consider the era, reached at some time by all portions of mankind destined for civilization, at which tribal communities settle down upon a specific area of land. <page-num>71</page-num>
<header>LECT. III. INDIAN MEMORIAL VERSES.</header>

In a very interesting volume published by the Government of Madras titled 'Papers on Mirasi Right' (Madras, 1862), some ancient "Memorial Verses" are printed. These describe the manner in which the Vellalee, a possibly Aryan tribe, followed their chief into Tondeimandalam, a region roughly corresponding to a state once famous in modern Indian history, Arcot. There, the Vellalee conquered and exterminated or enslaved a more primitive population and took permanent possession of its territory. The poetess—as the lines are attributed to a woman—compares the invasion to the flowing of sugar-cane juice over a flat surface. ('Mirasi Papers,' p. 233.) The juice crystallizes, and the crystals represent the various village communities. In the middle is one lump of exceptionally fine sugar, marking the location of the god’s temple. Simple as the image is, it seems to me particularly appropriate in one respect. It represents the tribe, though moving as a blended mass of people, as containing within itself a principle of cohesion that began to take effect as soon as the migration ended. This point is not always remembered. Social history is frequently thought to begin only with the tribal settlement, as if no principles of union had been brought by the tribe from an older home. However, we have no actual knowledge of any truly aboriginal or <term>autochthonous</term> tribe. Wherever we have reasonably reliable information concerning the tribes we observe

<header>SETTLEMENT OF TRIBES ON LAND. LECT. III</header>

In the far distance of history, they have always come from some more ancient home. The Vellalee, in the Indian example, must have been agriculturalists somewhere else, since they crystallized at once into village communities.

It has long been assumed that the tribal organization of society belonged originally to nomadic communities, and that when associations of men first settled down upon land, a great change came over them. But the manner of transition from nomadic to settled life, and its effects upon custom and idea, have been described too often—it seems to me—from mere conjecture about the probabilities. As I have just observed, the whole process has been conceived as more abrupt than such knowledge as we have would lead us to believe it to have been. Attention has thus been diverted from one assertion on this subject which may be made, I think, upon trustworthy evidence—that, from the moment when a tribal community settles down finally upon a definite space of land, the Land begins to be the basis of society in place of Kinship. The change is extremely gradual, and in some particulars it has not even now been fully accomplished, but it has been going on through the whole course of history. The organization of the Family through actual blood relationship is, of course, an observable fact, but, for all groups of men larger than the Family, the Land on which they

<header>LECT. III. KINSHIP AND THE LAND.</header>

live tends to become the bond of union between them, at the expense of Kinship, which becomes ever more and more vaguely conceived. We can trace the development of this idea both in the large and now extremely diverse groups of men combined in States or Political Communities, and also in the smaller groups collected in village communities and manors, among whom landed property originated. The barbarian invaders of the Western Roman Empire—though not uninfluenced by former settlements in older homes—brought back to Western Europe a mass of tribal ideas that the Roman dominion had banished from it; but, from the moment of their final occupation of definite territories, a transformation of these ideas began. Some years ago I pointed out (*Ancient Law*, pp. 103 *et seq.*) the evidence provided by the history of International Law that the notion of territorial sovereignty, which is the basis of the international system and which is inseparably connected with dominion over a definite area of land, very slowly replaced the notion of tribal sovereignty. Clear traces of the change can be seen in the official titles of kings. Of our own kings, King John was the first who always called himself King of England. (Freeman, *Norman Conquest*, I. 82, 84.) His predecessors commonly or always called themselves Kings of the English. The title of the king reflected the older tribal sovereignty for a much

<header>KINGS OF FRANCE AND OF THE FRENCH. LECT. III.</header>

longer time in France. The title of King of France undoubtedly came into use in the common language soon after the accession of the Capetian dynasty, but it is a striking fact that, even at the time of the Massacre of St. Bartholomew, the Kings of France were still titled in Latin as *Reges Francorum* (Kings of the Franks); and Henry IV only abandoned this designation because it could not be made to fit conveniently on his coins alongside the title of King of Navarre, which was the purely feudal and territorial principality of the Bourbons. (Freeman, *loc. cit.*) We may understand this transformation of ideas in another way. England was once the country inhabited by Englishmen. Now, Englishmen are the people who inhabit England. The descendants of our ancestors maintain the tradition of kinship by calling themselves people of the English race, but they steadily tend to become Americans and Australians. I do not say that the notion of consanguinity <note>kinship or shared biological descent</note> is absolutely lost; but it is extremely diluted, and entirely secondary to the newer view of the territorial constitution of nations. These blended ideas are reflected in such expressions as "Fatherland," which is itself an indicator of the fact that our thoughts cannot separate national kinship from a shared country. No doubt it is true that in our day the older conception of national union through consanguinity has seemed to be revived by theories which are generally called theories of

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>LECT. III. THEORIES OF NATIONALITY.</header>

Nationality, and of which particular forms are known to us as Pan-Slavism and Pan-Teutonism. Such theories are in truth a product of modern philology, and have grown out of the assumption that linguistic affinities prove community of blood. But wherever the political theory of Nationality is distinctly conceived, it amounts to a claim that men of the same race shall be included, not in the same tribal, but in the same territorial sovereignty.

We can perceive, from the records of the Hellenic and Latin city-communities, that there, and probably over a great part of the world, the substitution of common territory for common race as the basis of national union was slow, and not accomplished without very violent struggles. "The history of political ideas begins," I have said elsewhere, "with the assumption that kinship in blood is the sole possible ground of community in political functions; nor is there any of those subversions of feeling which we emphatically term revolutions so startling and so complete as the change which is accomplished when some other principle—such as that, for instance, of local contiguity—establishes itself for the first time as the basis of common political action." The one object of ancient democracies was, in fact, to be counted as kin to the aristocracies, simply on the ground that the aristocracy of old citizens, and the democracy of new citizens, lived within the same territorial boundaries <note>territorial circumscription</note>. The goal was

<summary>Maine argues that modern nationality theories often confuse shared language with shared blood, whereas political history shows a revolutionary shift from kinship to territorial location as the basis for citizenship. He notes that ancient democracies struggled to be recognized as political equals to aristocracies based on shared territory rather than shared ancestry.</summary>

I have before stated that a certain confusion, or at any rate a lack of clear distinction, between <term>consanguinity</term> and common subjection to power is traceable among the rudiments of <term>Aryan thought</term>, and no doubt the mixture of notions has helped to bring about that identification of common nationality with common allegiance to the King, which has greatly facilitated the absorption of new bodies of citizens by modern commonwealths. But the majesty with which the memory of the Roman Empire surrounded all kings has also greatly contributed to it, and without the victory of the Roman Plebeians there would never have been, I need hardly say, any Roman Empire.

The new knowledge which has been rapidly accumulating of late years enables us to track precisely the same transformation of ideas amid the smaller groups of kinsmen settled on land and forming, not Commonwealths, but <term>Village-Communities</term>. <header>LECT. III.  STATES AND VILLAGE-COMMUNITIES.</header>

It has often, indeed, been noticed that a **Feudal Monarchy** was an exact counterpart of a **Feudal Manor**, but the reason for this correspondence is only now beginning to dawn upon us: it is that both of them were in their origin bodies of assumed kinsmen settled on land and undergoing the same transformation of ideas through the fact of settlement. The history of the larger groups ends in the modern notions of **Country** and **Sovereignty**; the history of the smaller in the modern notions of **Landed Property**. The two courses of historical development were for a long while strictly parallel, though they have ceased to be so now.

The naturally organized, self-existing, **Village-Community** can no longer be claimed as an institution specially characteristic of the **Aryan races**. M. de Laveleye, following Dutch authorities, has described these communities as they are found in **Java**; and M. Renan has discovered them among the more obscure **Semitic tribes** in **Northern Africa**. <header>STAGES OF THE COMMUNITY. LECT. III</header>

...here <note>that is, in England</note> as elsewhere, formed of men bound together by a tie of kinship—in its first state natural, and in a later stage either natural or artificial kinship.' The evidence, however, is now quite sufficient to provide us with strong indications not only of the way in which these communities began, but of the way in which they transformed themselves. The world, in fact, contains examples of farming groups in every stage: from those that are actually bodies of kinsmen, to those in which only the slightest shadow of <term>consanguinity</term> survives and the group of farmers is held together solely by the land which they till in common. 

The major steps in this scale of transition seem to me to be marked by the **Joint Family** of the Hindus, by the **House-Community** of the Southern Slavonians, and by the true **Village-Community**, as it is found first in Russia and next in India. The group which I have placed at the head, the Hindu **Joint Family**, is really a body of kinsmen—the natural and adoptive descendants of a known ancestor. Although the modern law of India provides such facilities for its dissolution that it is one of the most unstable of social structures and rarely lasts beyond a couple of generations, it still possesses a legal corporate existence for as long as it survives. It exhibits, in its most perfect state, that community of <term>proprietary enjoyment</term> which has been so often observed and (I should add) so often misunderstood, in

### LECTURE III. THE HOUSE-COMMUNITY.

...cultivating societies of an archaic type. "According to the true notion of a joint undivided Hindu family," the Privy Council said, "no member of the family, while it remains undivided, can assert regarding the joint undivided property that he, that particular member, has a certain definite share. . . . The proceeds of undivided property must be brought, according to the theory, into the common chest or purse, and then dealt with according to the modes of enjoyment of the members of an undivided family." (Per Lord Westbury, *Appovier v. Rama Subba Aiyan*, 11 Moore’s Indian Appeals, 75.) However, while these Hindu families, "joint in food, worship, and estate," are constantly engaged in the cultivation of land and dealing with its produce "according to the modes of enjoyment of an undivided family," they are not village communities. They are only accidentally connected with the land, however extensive their landed property may be. What holds them together is not land, but <term>consanguinity</term>, and there is no reason why they should not occupy themselves—as indeed they frequently do—with trade or with the practice of a handicraft. The House-Community, which comes next in the order of development, has been examined by M. de Laveleye (*P. et s. F. P.*, p. 201), and by Mr. Patterson ("Fortnightly Review," No. xliv.), in Croatia, Dalmatia, and Illyria—countries which,

These House-Communities seem to me to be simply the Joint Family of the Hindus, allowed to expand itself without hindrance and settled for ages on the land. All the chief characteristics of the Hindu institution are here: the common home and common table, which are always theoretically the center of Hindu family life; the collective enjoyment of property; and its administration by an elected manager. Nevertheless, many instructive changes have begun which show how such a group modifies itself over time. The community is a community of kinsmen; but, though the common ancestry is probably real to a great extent, the tradition has become weak enough to allow for a significant amount of artificiality to be introduced into the association, as it is found at any given moment, through the absorption of strangers from the outside. <header>LECT. III. THE VILLAGE-COMMUNITY.</header>

true basis of the group; it is recognized as of preeminent importance to its vitality, and it remains common property, while private ownership is allowed to show itself in movables and cattle. In the true Village-Community, the common dwelling and common table which belong alike to the Joint Family and to the House-Community, are no longer to be found. The village itself is an assemblage of houses, contained indeed within narrow limits, but composed of separate dwellings, each jealously guarded from a neighbor's intrusion. The village lands are no longer the collective property of the community; the arable lands have been divided between the various households; the pasture lands have been partially divided; only the waste remains in common. In comparing the two extant types of Village-Community which have been longest examined by competent observers, the Russian and the Indian, we may be led to think that the traces left on usage and idea by the ancient collective enjoyment are faint exactly in proportion to the decay of the theory of actual kinship among the co-villagers. The Russian peasants of the same village really believe, we are told, in their common ancestry, and accordingly we find that in Russia the arable lands of the village are periodically redistributed, and that the village craftsman, even should he carry his tools to a distance, works for the profit of his co-villagers. In India, though the villagers are

<header>THE VILLAGE-COMMUNITY. LECT. III.</header>

still a brotherhood, and though membership in the brotherhood separates a man from the world outside, it is very difficult to say what the tie is conceived to consist of. Many obvious facts in the composition of the community are constantly inconsistent with the actual descent of the villagers from any one ancestor. Accordingly, private property in land has grown up, though its outlines are not always clear; the periodic redistribution of the domain has become a mere tradition, or is only practiced among the ruder portions of the race; and the results of the theoretical kinship are mostly confined to the duty of submitting to common rules of cultivation and grazing, of refraining from sale or transfer without the consent of fellow villagers, and (according to some opinions) of refraining from imposing a <term>rack-rent</term> <note>an excessive or extortionate rent</note> upon members of the same brotherhood. Thus, the Indian Village-Community is a body of men held together by the land which they occupy; the idea of common blood and descent has almost died out. A few steps more in the same course of development—and these the English law is actually accelerating—will spread the familiar ideas of our own country and time throughout India; the Village-Community will disappear, and landed property, in the full English sense, will come into existence. Mr. Freeman tells us that Uffington, Gillingham, and Tooting were in all probability English village communities originally.

<header>LECT. III. TRANSFORMATION OF VILLAGE-COMMUNITIES.</header>

The "natural communism" of the primitive farming groups has sometimes been described in recent years, and more particularly by Russian writers, as an early version of the most advanced and incisive democratic theories. In my judgment, no account of the matter could be more misleading. If terms such as "aristocratic" and "democratic" are to be used at all, I think it would be a more plausible statement that the transformation and occasional destruction of the village-communities were caused, over much of the world, by the successful assault of a democracy on an aristocracy. The reason why Russian village-communities have changed so little from what is believed to have been the primitive type appears to me to lie in the ancient Russian practice of colonization. By this practice, "swarms" were constantly sent out from the older villages to settle somewhere in the enormous wilderness; however, the Indian communities, located in a region where the population has been much denser than in the North since time immemorial, show many marks of past conflicts between the ancient brotherhood of kinsmen and a

I am aware that there is some incongruity at first sight in a comparison of Indian villagers—in their obscurity and ignorance, and often in their squalid misery—to the citizens of Athens or Rome; yet no tradition concerning the origin of the Latin and Hellenic states seems more trustworthy than that which represents them as formed by the merging of two or more village communities. Indeed, even in their most glorious forms, they appear to me throughout their early history to belong essentially to that type. It has often occurred to me that Indian officials, in their vehement controversies about the respective rights of the various classes which make up the village community, are unconsciously striving to adjust, through a beneficial arbitration, the claims and counter-claims of the Eupatrids and the Demos, or the Populus and the Plebs. There is even reason to think that one well-known result of long civil conflict in the great states of antiquity has shown itself every now and then in the village communities, and that all classes have had to submit to that sort of authority which assumed its most innocent shape in the office of the Roman Dictator, and its more hateful form in the usurpation of the Greek Tyrant. Other causes must be assigned for that great transformation of the village community which has been carried out everywhere in England, a little less completely in Germany, and much less so in Russia and in all of Eastern Europe. I have attempted in another work (*Village-Communities in the East and West*, pp. 131 and following) to give a brief account of all that is known or has been conjectured on the subject of that "Feudalization of Europe" which has had the effect of converting the Mark into the Manor and the village community into the fief; and I shall presently say much regarding the new light which the ancient laws of Ireland have thrown on the early stages of the process. At present, I will only observe that, when completed, its effect was to make land the exclusive bond of union between people. The manor or fief was a social group based entirely upon the possession of land, and the vast body of feudal rules which clustered around this central fact are influenced by it throughout. That land is the foundation of the feudal system has, of course, been long and fully recognized; but I doubt whether the place of this fact in history has been sufficiently understood. <header>DISSOLUTION OF FEUDAL GROUPS. LECT. III.</header>

The link between Lord and Vassal produced by **Commendation** is of a completely different kind from that produced by **Consanguinity**. When the relationship it created had lasted for some time, there would have been no deadlier insult to the lord than to attribute to him a common origin with the great majority of his tenants. Language still retains a trace of the hatred and contempt with which the higher members of the feudal groups regarded the lower; the words of abuse traceable to this aversion are almost as strong as those traceable to differences in religious belief. There is, in fact, little to choose between *villain*, *churl*, *miscreant*, and *boor*.

The dissolution of the feudal group—which is far advanced in most European countries and complete in France and England—has brought us to the state of society in which we live today. To write its course and causes would be to rewrite most of modern history, economic as well as political. It is not, however, difficult to see that without the ruin of the smaller social groups, and the decay of the authority which they possessed over the men composing them (whether they were governed popularly or autocratically), we should never have had several great conceptions which lie at the base of our current way of thinking. Without this collapse, we should never have had the conception of land as an exchange-

<header>### LECT. III. MODERN CONCEPTIONS.</header>

Consequently, we would not have had those theories that form the foundation of the most recent systems of jurisprudence—the theory of Sovereignty (or, in other words, of a portion in each community possessing unlimited coercive force over the rest) and the theory of Law as exclusively the command of a sovereign individual or group. <header>NO SEPARATION OF STAGES. LECT. III.</header>

latter assemblages, I would not for a moment be understood to assert that this series of changes can be divided into stages abruptly separated from one another. The most that can be affirmed is that certain periods in this history are distinguished by the predominance, though not the exclusive existence, of ideas characteristic of them. Here, as elsewhere, the world is full of ‘survivals,’ and the view of society as being held together by kinship still survives even when it is beginning to be held together by land. Similarly, the feudal conception of social relations still exercises a powerful influence even when land has become a merchantable commodity. There is no country in which the theory of land as a form of property like any other has been more unreservedly accepted than our own. Yet English lawyers live in *fæce feodorum* <note>Latin: in the dregs of feudalism</note>. Our law is saturated with feudal principles, and our customs and opinions are largely shaped by them. Indeed, within the last few years, we have even discovered that vestiges of the <term>village-community</term> have not been wholly effaced from our law, our usages, and our methods of tillage.

The caution that the sequence of these stages does not imply an abrupt transition from any one to the next seems to me especially needed by the student of the <term>Ancient Laws of Ireland</term>. The instructiveness of the Brehon tracts, at least to the student of legal history, seems to me to arise from their showing that institutions of a modern stamp may exist alongside a number of rules that suggest another and a much older order of ideas. It cannot be doubted, I think, that the primitive notion of kinship, as the cement binding communities together, survived longer among the Celts of Ireland and the Scottish Highlands than in any Western society, and that it is stamped on the <term>Brehon law</term> even more clearly than it is upon the actual land law of India. It is perfectly true that the form of private ownership in land which grew out of the appropriation of portions of the tribal domain to individual households of tribesmen is plainly recognized by the Brehon lawyers; yet the rights of private owners are limited by the controlling rights of a brotherhood of kinsmen, and the control is in some respects even more stringent than that exercised over separate

Yet, although the Chief of the Clan is rapidly rising to a position corresponding to the Lordship of a Manor, he has not yet fully reached it; and the most original information contained in these documents is what they provide concerning this process of ascent.

The first instructive fact that strikes us at the beginning of the <term>Brehon law</term> is that the same word, ‘Fine,’ or Family, is applied to all subdivisions of Irish society. It is used for the Tribe in its largest sense, claiming some degree of political independence, and for all intermediate groups down to the Family as we understand it today, and even for portions of the Family (Sullivan, ‘*Introduction*,’ *clxii*.). It seems certain that each of the various groups into which ancient Celtic society was divided believed itself to be descended from a single common ancestor, from whom the name, or one of the names, of the entire body of kinsmen was taken. <header>LECT. III. FAMILIES AND PLACES.</header>

political unit. But it is probable that it was occasionally, and even often, true of the smaller group—the Sept, sub-Tribe, or Joint Family—which appears to me to be the *legal* unit of the <term>Brehon tracts</term>. The traditions regarding the eponymous ancestor of this group were distinct and apparently trustworthy, and its members were related to one another by virtue of their common descent from the ancestor who gave his name to all. The chief for the time being was, as the Anglo-Irish judges called him in the famous 'Case of Gavelkind,' the *caput cognationis*.

Not only was the Tribe or Sept named after this eponymous ancestor, but the territory which it occupied also derived its most common name from him. I make this remark primarily because a false inference has been drawn from an assertion of learned men concerning the connection between names of families and names of places, which, properly understood, is perfectly sound. It has been established that, whenever a family and place have the same name, it is the place which almost certainly gave its name to the family. This is no doubt true of feudalized countries, but it is not true of countries as yet unaffected by feudalism. It is likely that such names as 'O'Brien's Country' and 'Macleod's Country' are as old as any appropriation of land by man; and this is worth remembering when we are tempted to judge the intelligence of an early writer

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>CONSTITUTION OF IRISH TRIBE. LECT. III</header>

by the absurdity of his etymologies. 'Hibernia' from an eponymous discoverer, 'Hyber,' sounds ridiculous enough; but the chronicler who gives it may have been close enough to the age of tribal society to think that the connection between the place and the name was the most natural and probable he could suggest. Even the most fanciful etymologies of the Greeks, such as Hellespont from Helle, may have been 'survivals' from a primitive tribal system of naming places. In the relationship between names and places, as in much more important matters, feudalism has remarkably added to the importance of land.

Let me now state the impression which, partly from the examination of the translated texts—legal and non-legal—and partly by the help of Dr. Sullivan's Introduction, I have formed of the agrarian organization of an Irish Tribe. It has, in all probability, been long settled upon the tribal territory. It is of sufficient size and importance to constitute a political unit, and possibly at its head is one of the numerous chieftains whom the Irish records call Kings. The primary assumption is that the whole of the tribal territory belongs to the entire tribe, but in fact, large portions of it have been permanently assigned to minor bodies of tribesmen. A part is allotted in a special way to the Chief as belonging to his office, and descends from Chief to Chief according to a special rule of succession. Other portions are

<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>LECT. III. CONSTITUTION OF IRISH TRIBE.</header>

occupied by fragments of the tribe, some of which are under minor chiefs or ‘<term>flaiths</term>,’ while others, though not strictly ruled by a chief, have someone of a noble class to act as their representative. All the unappropriated tribe lands are in a more special way the property of the tribe as a whole, and no portion can theoretically be subject to more than a temporary occupation. Such occupations are, however, frequent, and among the holders of tribe land on these terms are groups of men calling themselves tribesmen, but who are in reality associations formed by contract, primarily for the purpose of pasturing cattle. Much of the common tribe land is not occupied at all, but constitutes, to use the English expression, the ‘waste’ of the tribe. Nevertheless, this waste is constantly brought under cultivation or permanent pasture by settlements of tribesmen, and upon it cultivators of servile status are permitted to settle, particularly towards the border. It is the part of the territory over which the authority of the Chief tends steadily to increase, and here it is that he settles his ‘<term>fuidhir</term>,’ or stranger tenants—a very important class—the outlaws and ‘broken’ men from other tribes who come to him for protection, and who are only connected with their new tribe by their dependence on its chief, and through the responsibility which he incurs for them.

<page-num>94</page-num>
<header>UNIFORMITY OF GROUPS. LECT. III.</header>

There is probably great uniformity in the composition of the various groups occupying, permanently or temporarily, the tribal territory. Each seems to be more or less a miniature of the large tribe which includes them all. Each probably contains freemen and slaves, or at least men who vary significantly in personal status, yet each calls itself in some sense a family. Each very possibly has its appropriated land and its waste, and conducts tillage and grazing on the same principles. Each is either under a Chief who truly represents the common ancestor of all the free kinsmen, or under someone who has taken on the responsibilities that fall, according to primitive social ideas, upon the natural head of the kindred. In inquiries of the type we are currently conducting, the important fact I stated here three years ago should always be kept in mind. When the first English emigrants settled in New England, they distributed themselves into village communities; it is so difficult to forge new paths of social life and new routines of social habit. It is almost certain that, in such a society as the one we are discussing, a single model of social organization and social practice would prevail, and only slight or unnoticeable departures from it would be practical or imaginable.

But still, the society formed this way is not entirely stationary. The temporary occupation of the common tribal land tends to become permanent, either

<page-num>95</page-num>
<header>LECT. III. GROUPS NOT STATIONARY.</header>

through the silent permission or the active consent of the tribesmen. Particular families manage to avoid the theoretically periodic re-division of the group’s common inheritance; others obtain allotments with its consent as a reward for service or as a benefit of their office; and there is a constant transfer of lands to the Church, and a close mixing of tribal rights with ecclesiastical rights. The establishment of <term>Property in Severalty</term> <note>Private ownership of land by an individual rather than a group</note> is undoubtedly slowed both by the abundance of land and by the very law under which—to repeat the metaphor of the Indian poetess—tribal society has crystallized. This is because each family that has taken a portion of tribal land for itself always tends to expand into a large group of tribesmen who all have equal rights. But still, there is a combination of causes always tending to result in <term>Several Property</term>, and the <term>Brehon law</term> shows that by the time it was written down, these causes had largely taken effect. As might be expected, the separation of land from the common territory appears to have been most complete in the case of Chiefs, many of whom have large private estates held under ordinary tenure in addition to the <term>demesne</term> <note>Land attached to a manor or office for the owner's own use</note> specially attached to their <term>signory</term> <note>The territory or lordship of a lord</note>.

Such is the picture of Irish tribal organization in relation to the land which I have been able to form. All such descriptions must be received with caution: among other reasons, because

But if the account is in any degree correct, all who have attended to this class of subjects will observe at once that the elements of what we are accustomed to consider the specifically Germanic land system are present in the territorial arrangements of the Irish tribe. Doubtless there are significant distinctions. Kinship as yet, rather than landed rights, binds the members of the Irish groups together. The Chief is as yet a very different figure from the Lord of the Manor. And there are no signs as yet even of the beginnings of great towns and cities. Still the assertion, which is the theme of Dr. Sullivan’s treatise, may be proposed without rashness: that everything in the Germanic has at least its embryo in the Celtic land system. The study of the <term>Brehon law</term> leads to the same conclusion pointed to by so many branches of modern research. It conveys a stronger impression than ever of a wide separation between the <term>Aryan race</term> and races of other stocks, but it suggests that many, perhaps most, of the differences in kind alleged to exist between Aryan sub-races are really differences merely in degree of development. It is to be hoped that contemporary thought will before long make an effort to free itself from those habits of frivolity in adopting theories of race which it seems to have acquired. Many of

<header>TRIBAL PROPERTY IN IRELAND. LECT. IV.</header>

## LECTURE IV.

### THE TRIBE AND THE LAND.

It has been very commonly believed that, before the land reforms of James I, Ireland was one of the countries in which private property in land was treated with the least respect, and in which forms of ownership generally considered primitive were most widespread. Spenser and Davis certainly suggest this view, and several modern writers have adopted it. The <term>Brehon law tracts</term> prove, however, that this view can only be accepted with significant qualification and modification. They show that private property—and especially private property in land—had long been known in Ireland during the era to which they belong, having come into existence either through the natural breakdown of collective ownership or through the separation of specific estates from the general tribal domain. Nevertheless, I think it cannot be doubted that during the period indicated by these tracts, much land throughout Ireland was held under rules or customs reflecting the ancient

The author cites Brehon law tracts to show that private ownership had already evolved from or alongside collective tribal systems.</summary>

<header>LECT. IV. TANISTRY AND GAVELKIND.</header>

Part of the evidence of the fact just stated is reasonably familiar to students of Irish history. At the beginning of the seventeenth century, the Anglo-Irish judges declared the English Common Law to be in force throughout Ireland; from the date of this decision, all land in the country descended to the eldest son of the last owner, unless its transfer was otherwise determined by a settlement or will. In Sir John Davis’s report of the case and of the arguments before the Court, it is stated that until then all land in Ireland had descended either under the rule of <term>Tanistry</term> or under the rules of <term>Gavelkind</term>. The system of inheritance here called <term>Gavelkind</term> is described as follows: When a landowning member of an Irish <term>Sept</term> died, its chief made a redistribution of all the lands of the <term>Sept</term>. He did not divide the estate of the dead man among his children, but used it to increase the allotments of the various households of which the <term>Sept</term> was made up. The judges treated both <term>Tanistry</term> and <term>Gavelkind</term> as systems of succession after death of a peculiarly barbarous and harmful kind; and, as systems of succession, I shall consider them later. But all systems of succession after death bear a close relation to ancient modes of enjoyment during life; for instance, in the Joint Undi-

<header>IRISH GAVELKIND. LECT. IV</header>

vided Family of the Hindus, the <term>*stirpes*</term>, or stocks—which are only known to European law as branches of inheritors—are actual divisions of the family and live together in distinct parts of the common dwelling. (*Calcutta Review*, July 1874, p. 208.) The so-called Irish <term>Gavelkind</term> belongs to a class of institutions very common in the infancy of law; it is a mechanism for securing comparative equality among the joint owners of a common fund. The redistribution here takes place at the death of a head of a household; but if equality were secured by what is practically the same process—namely, re-division after a fixed period of years—an institution would be produced which has not quite died out of Europe at the present moment, and of which there are traditions in all ancient countries. At the same time, I have no doubt that when Irish <term>Gavelkind</term> was declared illegal, it was very far from being the only system of succession known to Ireland besides <term>Tanistry</term>, and I think it probable that many different modes of possession and inheritance were abolished by the decision giving the land to the eldest son.

It was the actual observation of peculiar agricultural usages, special methods of cultivation, and unusual rules of tenure which mainly enabled G. L. <header>LECT. IV. RUNDALE HOLDINGS.</header>

I venture to think that this kind of observation has not been carried far enough in Ireland to provide material for a confident opinion, but there certainly seem to be remnants of ancient collective enjoyment in the extensive prevalence of ‘<term>rundale</term>’ holdings in parts of the country. Under this system, a definite area of land is occupied by a group of families. In the form now most common, the arable lands are held in <term>severalty</term>, while pasture and bog are held in common. But as recently as fifty years ago, cases were frequent in which the arable land was divided into farms which shifted among the tenant families periodically, and sometimes annually. Even when no such division was made, a well-known relic of the <term>Mark system</term>, as it showed itself in Germany and England, was occasionally found: the arable portion of the estates was composed of three different qualities of soil, and each tenant had a lot or lots in the land of each quality, regardless of position. What was virtually the same system of <term>tenure</term> prevailed quite recently in the Scottish Highlands. I have confirmed that the families which formed the village communities that have only recently become extinct in the Western Highlands had the lands of the village redistributed among them by lot at fixed intervals of time; and I gather from Mr. Skene’s valuable note on ‘<term>Tribe Communities in Scotland</term>’

-># INFLUENCE OF THE CHURCH. LECT. IV<-

...explicitly devoted to the law of Church property and the organization of religious houses. When this writer claims that, under certain circumstances, a tribesman may grant or contract away tribal land, his ecclesiastical bias often raises a doubt about his legal doctrine. Does he mean to establish that the land may be given away generally to anyone, or only that it may be alienated specifically in favor of the Church? This difficulty of interpretation is interesting in its own right. I am convinced that the influence of the Christian Church on law has generally been looked for in the wrong place, and that legal historians have too often overlooked its role in spreading the concepts of free contract, individual property, and <term>testamentary succession</term> through regions beyond the Roman Empire. These regions were inhabited by communities bound together by the primitive tie of <term>consanguinity</term> (blood relation). It is generally agreed among scholars that churchmen introduced these peoples to wills and bequests; the <term>Brehon tracts</term> suggest to me at least that, along with the sacredness of bequests, they insisted upon the sacredness of contracts. It is well known that, in the Germanic countries, their ecclesiastical societies were among the earliest and largest recipients of public or ‘folk’ land (Stubbs, *Constitutional History*, vol. i. p. 154). The Will, the Contract, and Separate Ownership were, in fact, indispensable to the Church as the <term>donee</term>

<summary>This page argues that the Christian Church played a vital role in introducing concepts of individual property, contracts, and wills to tribal societies to facilitate its own acquisition of land. The author suggests that these legal innovations were necessary for the Church to act as a legal recipient of gifts and bequests.</summary>

<keywords>Church property, tribal land, free contract, individual property, testamentary succession, Brehon tracts, consanguinity, folk land, William Stubbs, legal history</keywords>

->### LECT. IV. THE ‘FINE’ AND THE SEPT.<-

of pious gifts; and they were also essential and characteristic elements in the civilization within which the Church had been raised to maturity. It is possible that the compiler of the *Corus Bescna* may have been a member of the clergy, as he certainly would have been in any society except the Irish; but, if he were a lawyer, he writes as a lawyer would state the case on behalf of a favorite and important client. Let me add that all the Brehon writers seem to me to have a bias toward private or individual, as distinguished from collective, property. No doubt it was then, as always, the great source of legal business, and it may have seemed to them—and it possibly was—the measurement of such advancement in civilization as their country was capable of making.

My own strong opinion is that the ‘Fine,’ whose rights and powers are the principal theme of the *Corus Bescna*, and whose name the translators render as ‘Tribe,’ is neither the Tribe in its largest extension, nor, on the other hand, the modern Family or group of descendants from a living ancestor, but the Sept. It is a body of kinsmen whose ancestor is no longer living, but whose descent from him is a reality, and neither a myth nor a fiction. It is the Joint Family of the Hindus, but with the characteristics of that group considerably modified through settlement on the land. This peculiar assemblage or corporation of blood-relatives, which I have referred to

->THE JOINT UNDIVIDED FAMILY. LECT. IV.<-

...several times before, is formed by the continuation of the family union through several—and it may be through an indefinite number of—generations. The rule throughout most of the civilized world is that, for all legal purposes, families are broken up into individuals or dissolved into a number of new families upon the death of their head. But this is not necessarily the case. The group made up of those whom we vaguely call our relatives—of our brothers, nephews, great-uncles, uncles, and cousins, no less than those related to us in the direct ascending and descending lines—might very well, after any number of deaths, remain joined together not only by blood and affection, but by mutual rights and duties prescribed or sanctioned by the law. An association of this sort is well known in the law of India as the <term>Joint Undivided Family</term>, or, to give the technical description, the Family, "joint in food, worship, and estate." If a <term>Hindu</term> has become the root of a family, it is not necessarily broken up by his death; his children continue united for legal purposes as a corporate brotherhood, and some specific act by one or more of the brothers is required to effect a dissolution of the network of mutual rights and a partition of the family property. The family thus formed by the continuation of several generations in union is identical in outline with a group very familiar to the students of the older Roman law—the <term>Agnatic Kindred</term>. The <term>Agnates</term>

<summary>This page explains the structure of the Joint Undivided Family in Indian law, where a family remains a single legal and economic unit across generations rather than dissolving upon the patriarch's death. It identifies this structure as being nearly identical to the "Agnatic Kindred" found in ancient Roman law.</summary>

<keywords>Joint Undivided Family, Hindu law, Indian law, corporate brotherhood, family property, inheritance, Agnatic Kindred, Roman law, Agnates</keywords>

# LECT. IV. THE TRIBE OF THE BREHON TRACTS.

...were that group of people who would have been under the patriarchal authority of some common ancestor if he had lived long enough to exercise it. The <term>Joint Family</term> of the <term>Hindus</term> is that group of people who would have joined in the sacrifices at the funeral of some common ancestor if he had died during their lifetime. In this latter case, the religious point of view simply takes the place of the legal or civil one.

As far as we are able, despite the disadvantages caused by the obscurity of our sources, let us examine the legal qualities which ancient Irish law attributes to this brotherhood of kinsmen as it was found in Ireland. First of all, the 'Tribe' of the <term>Brehon tracts</term> is a corporate, organic, and self-sustaining unit. 'The Tribe sustains itself' (*Ancient Laws of Ireland*, vol. ii, p. 283). Its continuity has begun to depend on the land which it occupies—'land,' says one of the still unpublished tracts, 'is perpetual man'—but it is not purely a land-owning body; it has 'live chattels and dead chattels' (livestock and inanimate property), which are distinguished from those of individual tribesmen (*Ancient Laws of Ireland*, vol. ii, p. 289). Nor is it purely a farming body; it may follow a professional occupation (*Ibid.*, vol. iii, pp. 49–51). A portion of the tribal territory, probably the arable and choice pasture lands, has been assigned to separate households of tribesmen, but they hold their allotments subject

-> ALIENATION OF TRIBE LAND. LECT. IV. <-

to the controlling rights of the entire brotherhood, and the primary or fundamental rule is that they are to keep their shares of tribe-land intact. ‘Every tribesman is able to keep his tribe-land; he is not to sell it or alienate or conceal it, or give it to pay for crimes or contracts.’ (*Ancient Laws of Ireland*, ii. 283.) ‘No person should leave a rent upon his land or upon his tribe which he did not find upon it.’ (*Ibid.*, iii. 52, 53.) ‘Everyone is wealthy who keeps his tribe-land perfect as he got it, who does not leave greater debt upon it than he found on it.’ (*Ibid.*, iii. 55.)

Under certain circumstances the tribesman may alienate, by grant, contract, or bequest, a certain quantity of the tribe-land allotted to him; but what those circumstances are, and what the quantity is, are points on which we cannot venture to make any precise statement, so obscure and contradictory are the rules set forth. But the grantee primarily contemplated is certainly the Church, though it seems clear that there is a general power of alienation, either with the consent of the entire tribal brotherhood or under pressure of strong necessity. It further appears to be beyond question that the tribesman has considerably greater power of disposition over property which he has acquired than over property which has devolved on him as a member of a tribe, and that he has more power over acquisitions made by his own unaided

"No person should grant land except such as he has purchased himself, unless by the common consent of the tribe." (*Ancient Laws of Ireland*, iii. 52, 53.) "He who has not sold or bought (that is, he who keeps his tribe-land as he obtained it) is allowed to make grants, each according to his status (that is, as the commentator explains, to the extent of one-third or one-half of his tribe-land)." "He who neither sells nor purchases may give as far as one-third of his tribe-share in case of little necessity and one-half in case of great necessity." (*Ancient Laws of Ireland*, iii. 47.) "If it is land that acquires it, it is one-half; . . . if he is a professional man, it is two-thirds of his contracts" (iii. 49).

The distinction between acquired property and property inherited or received from kinsmen, and the expanded power of parting with the former, are found in many bodies of ancient law—in our own early law among others. The rule that alienations, otherwise unlawful, may be made under pressure of necessity, is found in many parts of Hindu law. The rule requiring the consent of the collective brotherhood to alienations, with many minor rules of this part of <term>Brehon law</term>, constantly forms part of the customs of Indian and Russian village communities; and the duty of following common practices of

But by far the most striking and unexpected analogies in the <term>Brehon law</term> on the subject of Tribesmen and the Tribe are those which it has with the <term>Hindu law</term> of <term>Joint Undivided Families</term>. Under the <term>Brahminical Indian law</term>, whenever a member of a joint family has acquired property through special scientific knowledge or the practice of a liberal art, he does not bring it into the common fund, unless his accomplishments were obtained through a training given to him by his family or at their expense. The whole law on the subject was thoroughly considered in a strange case which arose before the <term>High Court of Madras</term> (*Madras High Court Reports*, ii. 56), where a joint family claimed the earnings of a <term>dancing-girl</term>. The decision of the Court is summarized by the reporter as follows: "The ordinary gains of science are divisible (that is, they are brought into <term>hotchpot</term> upon the partition of an undivided estate), when such science has been imparted at the family expense and acquired while receiving a family maintenance. It is otherwise when the science has been imparted at the expense of persons not members of the learner's family." The exact counterparts of the Indian rule and of the Indian

> 'If (the tribesman) is a professional man—that is, if the property is acquired by <term>judicature</term> or poetry, or any profession whatsoever—he is capable of giving two-thirds of it to the Church . . . but, if it was the lawful profession of his tribe, he shall not give of the earnings of his profession except just as he could give of the land of his tribe.' It will be seen from the instances which I have given that the rules of the Irish <term>Brehon law</term> regulating the power of individual tribesmen to *alienate* their separate property correspond to the rules of Indian <term>Brahminical law</term> which regulate the power of individual members of a joint family to *enjoy separate* property. The difference is *significant*. The <term>Hindu law</term> assumes that collective enjoyment by the whole brotherhood is the rule, and it treats the enjoyment of separate property by individual brothers as an *exception*—an exception, I may add, around which an enormous mass of law has now gathered. On the other hand, the <term>Brehon law</term>, so far as it can be understood, seems to me reconcilable with no other assumption than that individual proprietary rights have grown up and attained some stability within the circle of the tribe. The exercise of these rights is at the same time limited by the controlling powers of the collective brotherhood of tribesmen; and to

->IRISH CO-TENANCY. LECT. IV.<-

to these last, as with the <term>Agnatic Kindred</term> at Rome, some ultimate right of succession appears to be reserved. Hence, the Irish legal unit is not precisely a <term>Joint Family</term>; if the <term>Brehon law</term> is to be trusted, it has considerably less of the 'natural communism' which characterizes the Indian institution. The '<term>Fine</term>' of the tracts is constantly spoken of in connection with landed property, and, whenever it is so connected, I imagine it to have undergone some of the changes which are constantly brought about by contact with the land, and I figure it to myself in that case as a <term>Mark</term> or <term>Village-Community</term>, in which the ideas proper to the older group out of which it grew, the <term>Joint Family</term>, have survived in exceptional strength. In this respect, it approaches the Russian rather than the Indian type of <term>village-community</term>.

The '<term>Judgments of Co-Tenancy</term>' is a <term>Brehon law</term> tract, still unpublished at the time at which I write, and presenting, in its present state, considerable difficulties of interpretation. It puts, at the outset, the question—'Whence does <term>Co-Tenancy</term> arise?' The answer given is, 'From several heirs and from their increasing on the land.' The tract then goes on to explain that the land is, in the first year, to be tilled by the kinsmen just as each pleases; that in the second year they are to exchange lots; that in the third year the boundaries are to be fixed; and that the whole process of severance is to

# LECT. IV. STAGES IN HISTORY OF CULTIVATING GROUPS.

be completed in the tenth year. I trust it is not a presumptuous guess that the order of change indicated here is more reliable than the timeframe set for each of its stages. The period of ten years for the entire transition from <term>collective ownership</term> to <term>separate property</term> seems to me far too short and hard to reconcile with other Irish evidence; and I suggest that the <term>Brehon lawyer</term>, who like the rest of his class was attached to the institution of <term>separate property</term>, is depicting an ideal rather than an actual set of arrangements. The process, however, which is described here—if it is spread over a much longer space of time—is really in harmony with all our knowledge of the rise and progress of cultivating communities. First, a <term>Joint Family</term>, composed of "several heirs increasing on the land," is found to have established a settlement. In the earliest stage, the various households reclaim the land without fixed rules. Next comes the system of exchanging lots. Finally, the portions of land are held in <term>severalty</term>.

The references to ancient <term>collective ownership</term> and ancient collective enjoyment in non-legal Irish literature appear to be very rare. But my friend Mr. <term>Whitley Stokes</term> has supplied me with two relevant passages. The "*Liber Hymnorum*," attributed to the eleventh century, contains (folio 5A) the following statement: "Numerous were the human beings in <term>Ireland</term> at that time (i.e. the time of the

->PERIODICAL ALLOTMENTS IN IRELAND. LECT. IV.<-

Another Irish manuscript, believed to date from the twelfth century, the ‘*Lebor na Huidre*,’ says that ‘there was no ditch, fence, or stone wall around land until the time of the sons of Aed Slane; there were only open fields. Because of the large number of households during their time, they introduced boundaries in Ireland.’ 

These curious statements can, of course, only be regarded as evidence that, at the time they were written, there was a belief that a change from a system of collective ownership to a system of restricted use had occurred at some point in Ireland, and as a tradition regarding the date of that change. However, it is insightful to find both sources attributing this change to the growth of population, and a particular interest attaches to the account given in the ‘*Liber Hymnorum*’ of the newer distribution of land that was thought to have replaced an older system. The periodic allotment to each household of a specific portion of bog land, woodland, and arable land bears a strong resemblance to the distribution of pasture, wood, and arable land which still occurs today under the communal rules of the Swiss <term>Allmenden</term> (see Laveleye,

<summary>This page examines Irish manuscripts that credit the introduction of land boundaries to population growth during the 7th century. It notes the similarities between these ancient Irish land allotments and the communal land systems of the Swiss Allmenden.</summary>

<keywords>Aed Slane, Lebor na Huidre, Liber Hymnorum, Allmenden, land allotment, Ireland, communal property, population growth, land boundaries</keywords>

->LECT. IV. DOUBLE ORIGIN OF LANDED PROPERTY.<-

(*P. et s. F. P.*, pp. 268 *and following*), and which is an undoubted legacy from the ancient constitution of certain Swiss Cantons as Teutonic Hundreds.

Property in land, wherever it has grown out of the gradual dissolution of the ancient cultivating communities, has many characteristics which distinguish it from the form of landed property with which English people and those of English descent are best acquainted. The area within which this last form of property is the sole or dominant kind of ownership is now much larger than it was, through its spread over all of North America, except Mexico, and over all colonies settled for the first time by the English; but our nearly exclusive familiarity with it has led, I think, to our very commonly overestimating the extent to which it prevails over the world, and even over Western Europe. Its origin may be traced, not to the decaying authority of the Tribe over the individual holdings of the tribesmen, but to the ever-increasing authority of the Chief, first over his own domain and <term>booked land</term> <note>Land held by a written charter or "book," rather than by traditional customary right.</note>, and secondarily over the tribe-lands. The early growth of the power of the Chief is thus of the utmost interest in the history of landed property, and I propose to discuss it at some length in the succeeding lectures. In the meantime, let me say something on the transformations which Patriarchal Power is observed, as a fact, to undergo in the assemblages of

It suggests that such property rights developed from the increasing authority of the Chief over tribal lands, rather than from the original communal authority of the Tribe.</summary>

EXPANSION OF THE PATRIARCHAL FAMILY. LECTURE IV.

men held together by kinship who are still found forming a part of Aryan communities.

The <term>Joint Undivided Family</term>, wherever its origins are seen in such communities, universally arises from the <term>Patriarchal Family</term>—a group of natural or adoptive descendants held together by their subjection to the eldest living ancestor: the father, grandfather, or great-grandfather. Regardless of the formal requirements of the law, the head of such a group is always practically despotic. He is the object of a respect, if not always an affection, that is likely rooted deeper than any formal institution. But in the more extensive groups of kinsmen that make up the <term>Joint Family</term>, the eldest male of the senior line is never the parent of all the members, and he is not necessarily the oldest among them. To many of them, he is merely a distant relative, and he might even be an infant. The sense of patriarchal right does not fade away in such groups. Each father or grandfather has more power than anyone else over his own wife, children, and descendants; and there is always what might be called a belief that the blood of the collective brotherhood flows more truly and purely in one specific line than in any other. Among the Hindus, the eldest male of this line—if he has full mental capacity—is generally placed at the head of the joint family's affairs; but where the institution survives in its complete form, he is not a <term>Paterfamilias</term>, nor is he

<summary>This page explains how the Joint Undivided Family evolves from the Patriarchal Family, shifting from a group led by a literal ancestor to a broader kinship group where the head is a representative of the senior lineage. It highlights that while individual fathers retain authority over their immediate descendants, the collective group maintains a belief in the purity of a specific bloodline.</summary>

<header>REVIVAL OF PATRIA POTESTAS. LECT. IV.</header>

True patriarchal authority, however, revives whenever the process of expansion into a group is interrupted and whenever one of the brotherhood establishes himself at a distance from the rest. A <term>Hindu</term> who separates himself from a <term>Joint Family</term>—which the law, as administered by the English tribunals, gives him great facilities for doing—acquires much greater power over his family, in our modern sense of the word, than he had as a member of the larger brotherhood. Similarly, in the developed <term>Joint Family</term> or <term>Village-Community</term>, as the small society becomes more populous, as the village spreads, as the practice of living in separate dwellings extends, and as the land, rather than the common lineage, comes to be regarded as the cement of the brotherhood, each man in his own house practically obtains strict patriarchal authority over his wife, children, and servants. But then, on the other hand, the separated member of the joint family, or the head of the village household, will himself become the root of a new joint brotherhood, unless his children voluntarily dissolve the family union after his death. Thus, all the branches of human society may or may not have been developed from joint families which arose out of an original patriarchal cell; but, wherever the <term>Joint Family</term> is an institution of an <term>Aryan race</term>, we see it springing from such a cell, and, when it dissolves, we see it dissolving into a number of such cells.

<summary>This page explains that patriarchal authority is strengthened when an individual separates from a larger joint family or when a village community begins to live in separate dwellings. It argues that the joint family structure among Aryan races consistently originates from and dissolves back into individual patriarchal units.</summary>

->LECT. V.  DOUBLE ORIGIN OF LANDED PROPERTY.<-

## LECTURE V.

### THE CHIEF AND HIS ORDER.

Nothing seems to me to have been more clearly shown by recent research than the necessity of keeping the Tribe and the Tribal Chief separate as distinct sources of established institutions. The lines of descent are constantly entwined, but each of them is found to lead back in the end to an independent origin. If I were to apply this assertion to political history, I would only be repeating much of what has been said by Mr. Freeman in his excellent work on *Comparative Politics*. Restricting myself to the history of private institutions, let me observe that the distinction which I have drawn should be carefully kept in mind by those who desire to understand the beginnings of <term>Property in Land</term>. The subject has been greatly obscured by the practice, now attributed to the early writers on <term>feudal law</term>, of systematically ignoring or misinterpreting all forms of proprietary use which they could not explain according to their own principles; and until now the truth has only been directly seen through some of the rules of
