->DOUBLE ORIGIN OF LANDED PROPERTY. LECT. V.<-

tenure. It may now, however, be stated without hesitation that Property in Land, as known to communities of the Aryan race, has had a twofold origin. It has arisen partly from the separation of the individual rights of relatives or tribesmen from the collective rights of the Family or Tribe, and partly from the growth and transformation of the sovereignty of the Tribal Chief. The phenomena resulting from this dual process seem to me easily distinguishable from one another. Both the sovereignty of the Chief and the ownership of land by the Family or Tribe passed through the crucible of feudalism in most of Western Europe; but the first reappeared in certain well-marked characteristics of military or knightly tenures, and the last in the principal rules of non-noble holdings, including <term>Socage</term>, the distinctive tenure of the free farmer. The status of the Chief has thus left us one legacy in the rule of <term>Primogeniture</term>, which, however, has long since lost its most ancient form; another in the right to receive certain dues and to enforce certain monopolies; and a third in a particularly absolute form of property which was once exclusively enjoyed by the Chief, and after him by the Lord, in the portion of the tribal territory which formed his own domain. On the other hand, several systems of succession after death, including the equal division of the land among the children, have emerged from tribal ownership in various

The fate of this double set of institutions in England and in France seems most instructive to me. I have frequently emphasized here the error of the popular opinion that dates the extreme subdivision of the soil in France to the first French Revolution and to the sale of the Church lands and the estates of the emigrant nobility. He estimates that more than a third of the kingdom was occupied by them—a very large proportion, when the extent of Church land in France is taken into account; but recent French investigations have shown reasons for thinking that the true proportion was still larger, and that it was growing rather than diminishing, through that extravagance of the nobles which Court life fostered, and which

It seems to have very generally escaped notice that the law of equal or nearly equal division after death was the general law of France. The rule of primogeniture was of exceptional application and was for the most part confined to lands held by knightly tenure; indeed, in the South of France, where the custom of equal division was strengthened by the identical rule of Roman law, the privileges of the eldest son were only secured by invoking the exceptional rules of which the Roman Law gives the benefit to <term>*milites*</term> (or soldiers on service) when making their wills or regulating their successions, and by laying down that every <term>*chevalier*</term>, and every noble of higher degree, was a <term>*miles*</term> within the meaning of the Roman legal writers. The legacy from tribal sovereignty to seignorial privilege, which was truly resented, was the one I placed second in order. The right to receive feudal dues and to enforce petty monopolies—now almost extinguished in England by the measures implemented by the Copyhold Commission—had ceased long before the end of the last century to be of any considerable importance to the class that held these rights. However, M. de Tocqueville has explained in his *Ancien Régime* (i. 18) that these rights made up almost the entire livelihood which the majority of the French nobility possessed. The sense of ownership of the soil was thus held not by the lord but by the peasantry; and the peasantry viewed the exercise of seignorial rights with a feeling closely akin to that which is inspired by a highly oppressive tax. The state of sentiment produced by it is even now a political force of some significance in France; and a similar, though far weaker, repulsion is known to have been caused in this country <note>England</note> by the taking of tithes in kind. It is a significant fact that, where the ownership is acknowledged to reside in the superior holder, the collection of even an extreme rent from the tenants below has very rarely been regarded with the same bitterness of resentment.

The change, therefore, which took place in France during the first Revolution was this: the land law of the people replaced the land law of the nobles. In England, the opposite process has occurred, and what has happened is obviously in harmony with much else in English history. The system of the nobles has become, in all essential details, the system of the people. The rule of <term>primogeniture</term>, which once applied only to knightly holdings, came to apply to the great majority of English tenures, except for the <term>Gavelkind of Kent</term> and some others of only local importance. This grievance did more than any other to bring about the first French Revolution and to prevent the re-establishment of the ancient political order. # ENGLISH FORM OF OWNERSHIP. LECT. V.

This last form of ownership was very generally restricted by the duty of cultivation in some particular way, and, as a rule, could not be handled so as to block the rights reserved to the children and widow of the owner by the law of succession <note>inheritance law</note>. Traces of a similar kind of ownership, which was probably once widely widespread, may still be seen here and there through the customs of specific English manors. I repeat the opinion which I expressed three years ago: that our modern English concept of absolute property in land is actually descended from the special ownership enjoyed by the Lord, and more anciently by the tribal Chief, in his own Domain. It would be out of place to enter here into a discussion of the changes which seem desirable to me in order to make the soil of England as freely exchangeable as the theory now generally accepted demands; but to the principle of individual and absolute property in land, I believe this country is committed. I believe I am stating the conclusion suggested by all known legal history when I say that there can be no significant progress in civilization unless landed property is held by groups at least as small as Families; and I again remind you that we are indebted to the uniquely absolute English form of ownership for such an achievement as the cultivation of the soil of North America.

<header>LECTURE 5. IRISH THEORIES OF CHIEF AND TRIBE.</header>

Ancient Laws of Ireland throw on the primitive condition of the institutions of which I have been speaking, let me give you one word of caution as to the statements of modern Irish writers respecting the original relations of the Irish Tribe and of the Irish Tribal Chief. Unhappily, the subject has been discussed in the spirit of the later agrarian history of Ireland. On the one hand, some disputants have thought to serve a patriotic purpose by contending that the land of each Tribe belonged absolutely to itself and was its common property, and that the Chief was a mere administrative officer, rewarded for his services in making a fair distribution of the territory among the tribesmen by a rather larger share of its area than the rest, which was allotted to him as his domain. Contrariwise, some writers, not perhaps motivated by much kindness to the Irish people, have at least suggested that they were always cruelly oppressed by their superiors, and probably by their natural chiefs more than any others. These authors point to the strong evidence of oppression by the Chiefs which the books of the English observers of Ireland contain. Edmund Spenser and Sir John Davis <note>Sir John Davies, 1569–1626, Attorney-General for Ireland</note> cannot have merely intended to slander the native Irish aristocracy when they emphatically declared that the ‘chiefs do most shamefully rack-rent <note>charge excessive or extortionate rent</note> their tenants,’ and spoke with vehement indignation of the exactions from which the tribesmen suffered, the

The Norman nobles who first settled in Ireland are well known to have become in time Chieftains of Irish Tribes; and it is suggested that they were the first to forget their duties to their tenants and to think of nothing but their privileges. <header>LECT. V. THE AGRARIAN SYSTEM NOT STATIONARY.</header>

I cannot wholly agree with any one of these theories concerning the Chief and the Tribe. Each seems to me to contain a portion of the truth, but not the whole. Let me first say that the entire land system outlined in the Brehon laws does seem to me to have as its basis the primary ownership of the tribe-land by the Tribe. It is also true that the Chief appears to exercise certain administrative duties in respect to this land, and that he has a specific portion of the tribe-land allotted to him, in the vicinity of his residence or stronghold, for the maintenance of his household and relatives. But this is not all. As we see the system through the law, it is not stationary, but shifting, developing, disintegrating, and recombining. Even according to the texts that are apparently the oldest, much of the tribal territory appears to have been permanently alienated <note>Transferred to the ownership of another.</note> to sub-tribes, families, or dependent chiefs; and the glosses <note>Brief explanations or interpretations of a text.</note> and commentaries show that, before they were written, this process had gone very far indeed. Whatever, again, may have been the original dignity and authority of the Chief, they are plainly growing, not merely through the introduction of foreign principles and ideas, but from natural causes, more or less active all over Europe. The general charac-

<header>GROWING POWER OF CHIEF. LECT. V.</header>

character of these causes is very much the same as in Germanic countries. The power of the Chief grows first through the process which is called elsewhere "commendation"—the process by which the free tribesman becomes "his man" and remains in a state of dependence having various degrees. It further grows from his increasing authority over the waste lands of the tribal territory and from the servile or semi-servile colonies he plants there; and lastly, it increases from the material strength which he acquires through the numbers of his immediate retainers and associates, most of whom stand to him in more or less servile relations. But the Brehon law tells us much that is novel and surprising concerning the particular course of these changes and their nature in detail. It provides us with some wholly new ideas concerning the transition of society from early to complete feudalism, and helps us to complete the account of it derived from Germanic sources. In this, as it seems to me, the greatest part of its interest lies.

With the Chieftaincy of the Tribe, the early history of modern Aristocracy and modern Kingship begins. These two great institutions had, in fact, at first the same history, and the Western world long continued to bear the marks of their original identity. The Manor, with its tenemental lands held by the free tenants of the Lord and with its domain which was in immediate dependence on him, was the model for all

In every County, Dukedom, or Kingdom, there were great tenants holding land directly from its head and on a sort of parity with him; and there was a Domain under his more immediate government and at his immediate disposal. There is no more obscure or difficult subject than the origin of the class whose power was the keystone of all these political and proprietary structures, and none on which even the smallest contributions to our knowledge are more welcome.

There is one view of the original condition of privileged classes which, though held by learned men, has been significantly weakened recently by German research, and seems to me even further shaken by portions of the <term>Brehon law</term>. This is the impression that they always constituted—as they practically do now—a distinct class or section of the community, where each member of the class stands in a closer relation to the other members than to the rest of the national or tribal society to which they all belong. It cannot be doubted that the earliest modern aristocracies do, in fact, have this particular appearance when they are first identified. Mr. Freeman (*Norman Conquest*, i. 88) says that the "difference between <term>eorl</term> and <term>ceorl</term> <note>These terms refer to the Anglo-Saxon distinction between a high-ranking noble and a free commoner.</note> is a primary fact from which we start." <header>ORIGIN OF NOBILITY. LECT. V.</header>
<page-num>132</page-num>

distinguished the noble from the non-noble freeman in the Germanic societies which he observed; and Caesar, as I stated in another lecture, divides all the continental Celtic tribes into the *Equites* and the *Plebs*. We can understand that a spectator looking at a set of tribal communities from the outside would naturally group together all men visibly elevated above the rest; but nevertheless, this is not quite the appearance which early Germanic society presents to researchers who follow the methods of Von Maurer and Landau. Each chief or lord appears to them to have been noble less in relation to other noblemen than in relation to the other free tribesmen included in the same group as himself. Nobility has many diverse origins; but its chief source seems to have been the respect of fellow villagers or groups of kinsmen for the lineage in which the purest blood of each small society was believed to be preserved. Similarly, the <term>Brehon law</term> suggests that the Irish chiefs were not a class by themselves—as the corresponding order among the continental Celts appeared to Caesar—but were necessarily the heads of separate groups composed of their kinsmen or of their vassals. "Every chief," says the text which I quoted before, "rules over his land, whether it be large or whether it be small." Nevertheless, the people elevated in this way undoubtedly tend, for various reasons, to become a class of their own and a special section of the general community; it is very probable that this tendency existed from the earliest times. It should further be noted that some aristocracies were actually a distinct section of the community from the very beginning. This social structure is created when one entire tribal group conquers or imposes its supremacy over other tribal groups that also remain intact, or where an original body of tribesmen, villagers, or citizens gradually gathers a diverse collection of protected dependents around itself. There are many known examples of both processes, and the specific relationship between tribal groups implied by the former was certainly not unknown to Celtic societies. Among the Scottish Highlanders, it is stated that some entire septs or clans were enslaved to others; and at the very beginning of Irish history, we find a distinction between free and rent-paying tribes, which may possibly imply the same kind of superiority and subordination.

Here let me add the remark that the contrast commonly made between birth and wealth—and particularly wealth other than landed property—is entirely modern. In French literature, as far as my knowledge extends, it first appears when the riches of the financial officers of the French monarchy—the Superintendents and <term>Farmers-General</term>—began to attract attention. In our own society, it seems to be exclusively the result of the great expansion and productivity of industrial undertakings on the largest scale. But the heroes of the Homeric poems are not only valiant but wealthy (*Odyssey* xiv. 96–106); the warriors of the *Nibelungen-Lied* are not only noble but rich. In later Greek literature, we find pride of birth identified with pride in seven wealthy ancestors in succession, ἑπτα πάπποι πλούσιοι; and you are well aware how rapidly and completely the aristocracy of wealth assimilated into the aristocracy of blood in the Roman State. Turning to the Irish Chief, we find the tract called the <term>Cain-Aigillne</term> stating (p. 279) that *‘the head of every tribe should be the man of the tribe who is the most experienced, the most noble, the most wealthy, the most learned, the most truly popular, the most powerful to oppose, the most steadfast to sue for profits and to be sued for losses.’* There are many other passages to the same effect; and on closely examining the system (as I pro-

# LECT. V. NOBILITY AND WEALTH.
<page-num>135</page-num>

...pose to do now), we can see that personal wealth was the primary condition for the Chief to maintain his position and authority.

But while the Brehon laws suggest that possessing personal wealth is a condition for maintaining chieftainship, they show very clearly that through the acquisition of such wealth, the road to chieftainship was always open. We are not entirely without knowledge that in some European societies, the humble freeman might be raised by wealth to the position which later became modern nobility. One fact, among the very few that are fairly well established regarding the specific origin of particular modern aristocracies, is that a portion of the Danish nobility were originally peasants; and there are in early English laws some traces of a process by which a <term>Ceorl</term> might become a <term>Thane</term>. These might be isolated facts, and undoubtedly there is strong reason to suspect that the beginnings of aristocracy were multiple; but the Brehon tracts point out in several places, with legal precision, the way in which a peasant freeman in ancient Ireland could become a chief. There are few figures of greater interest mentioned in these laws than the <term>Bo-Aire</term>, literally the ‘cow-nobleman.’ He is, to begin with, simply a peasant who has grown rich in cattle, probably through obtaining the use of large portions of tribal land.

<summary>This page discusses how wealth, specifically in cattle, was the primary requirement for chieftainship and provided a path for peasants to ascend to the nobility. It highlights the Bo-Aire, or "cow-nobleman," as a key example of this social mobility in ancient Ireland.</summary>

<keywords>Brehon laws, chieftainship, Bo-Aire, wealth, cattle, social mobility, Ceorl, Thane, Irish history</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between wealth and nobility in ancient Ireland, specifically detailing the seven grades of the Aires and the process by which a Bo-Aire can achieve noble status.</meta>

->THE <term>BO-AIRE</term>. LECT. V.<-

<page-num>136</page-num>

The true nobles, or <term>Aires</term>—a word striking due to its similarity to words of similar meaning in the Teutonic languages—are divided into seven grades, though we can scarcely believe the classification corresponds to a universal fact. Each grade is distinguished from the others by the amount of wealth possessed by the Chief belonging to it, by the weight attached to his evidence, by his power to bind his tribe by contracts (literally of "knotting"), by the dues which he receives in kind from his vassals according to a system to be described shortly, and by his <term>Honor-Price</term>, or special damages incurred for injuring him. At the bottom of the scale is the chief or noble called the <term>Aire-desa</term>; and the <term>Brehon law</term> provides that when the <term>Bo-Aire</term> has acquired twice the wealth of an <term>Aire-desa</term>, and has held it for a certain number of generations, he becomes an <term>Aire-desa</term> himself. The advantage secured by wealth does not, you see, exclude respect for birth, but works with it. "He is an inferior chief," says the "<term>Senchus Mor</term>," "whose father was not a chief"; and there are many other strong assertions of the reverence due to inherited rank. The primary view of chieftainship is evidently that it springs from purity or dignity of blood, but noble birth is regarded as naturally associated with wealth, and he who becomes rich gradually climbs to a position indistinguishable from that which he would have occupied if he had been born into the nobility.

<summary>This page outlines the seven grades of Irish nobility, or Aires, and explains how wealth serves as a path for the Bo-Aire to achieve noble status over generations. It emphasizes that while the Brehon laws valued inherited rank, they also viewed wealth as a necessary and natural companion to noble birth.</summary>

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>LECT. V. POPULAR THEORY OF NOBILITY.</header>

What is new in the system is the clear account of <term>nobility</term> as a <term>status</term> that has its origin in the organic structure of ancient society, but nevertheless, in practice, has perpetually fresh beginnings.

The enormous importance that belongs to wealth, and especially to wealth in cattle, in the early <term>Aryan society</term> reflected by the <term>Brehon tracts</term>, helps, I think, to clear up one great difficulty that meets us at the beginning of an inquiry into the origin of <term>aristocracies</term>. I suppose that the popular theory on the subject of the privileged class in modern communities is that it was originally indebted for its status, if not for its power or influence, to <term>kingly grace</term>. An Englishman once questioned <term>Emperor Paul of Russia</term> on the position of the Russian nobility. "The only man who is noble in my dominions," said the <term>Czar</term>, "is the man to whom I speak, for the time that I am speaking to him." I merely take these words as the strongest possible statement of the view to which I am referring; but they were used by a monarch with a disturbed mind, whose authority had contracted something of an <term>Oriental character</term> from its long subordination to <term>Tartar power</term>, and they were never absolutely true even of Russia. Among ourselves, however, the favorite assumption certainly seems to be—however slight the practical consequences we draw from it—that all aristocratic privilege had its origin in <term>kingly grace</term>; and this

<header>NOBILITY AND THE KING. LECT. V.</header>

appears, on the whole, to be the theory of English law. But the institutions of many parts of the Continent long retained traces of a different set of ideas, and these were found where kingly power was actually much greater than in England. The French Noblesse, before the Revolution, would as a body have resented the assertion that they were a creation of the King, and the Kings of France more than once admitted that they were only the most exalted members of a class to which their own nobility belonged.

Kings everywhere nowadays—and in many countries for centuries—have had a monopoly on the power of ennobling. This road to nobility has been traveled for so long that people in general have almost forgotten there ever was another route. Yet historical scholars have long known that nobility conferred by royal grant was, in one sense, a modern institution, though they have not succeeded in completely explaining how it came to supplant or dwarf the institution upon which it was engrafted. There seems to be no doubt that the first aristocracy springing from kingly favor consisted of the <term>Comitatus</term>, or Companions of the King. Although there is a good deal of evidence that the class was at first considered in some way servile, it gradually became in some countries the type of all nobility. <header>LECT. V. THE ROYAL HOUSEHOLD.</header>

Among us, the great officers of the Royal Council and Household still take precedence over either all Peers or all Peers of their own rank. It seems certain that the Household sprang from very humble beginnings. Tacitus describes the companions of the Germanic chief as living with him in his house and supported by his generosity. <header>COMPANIONS OF THE KING OF ERIN. LECT. V.</header>

The King of Erin, though he never existed (strictly speaking) except for short intervals, yet always, so to speak, tended to exist; and the *Crith Gablach*, a <term>Brehon</term> tract of which a translation is given at the end of Sullivan’s edition of O’Curry’s Lectures, contains a picture of his palace and state. The building intended to be described is apparently very much the same as the great Icelandic house of which Mr. Dasent, in the ‘Story of Burnt Njal,’ has attempted to provide a drawing based on the descriptions found in Norse literature. In it, the King feasts his guests, ranging from kings and king’s sons to a ghastly company of prisoners in chains—the forfeited hostages of subject-chiefs or sub-septs who have broken their agreements. The Companions are there also, and they are stated to consist of his privileged tenantry and of his bodyguard, which is composed of men whom he has delivered from death, jail, or servitude—but never (a significant exception) of men whom he has saved on the battlefield. I fear that the picture of Irish society provided by the *Crith Gablach* must throughout be regarded as being, to a great extent, ideal or theoretical; at any rate, there is much testimony from English visitors to Ireland that many significant Irish Chiefs were much more humbly equipped than the King of Erin at Tara. Yet it is very likely that they all had Companions attending them, and I suspect that the obligation of maintaining a little

<header>LECT. V. RETINUE OF HIGHLAND CHIEFS.</header>

That even petty Chiefs of the Scottish Highlands had a retinue of the same character is known to all who can recall that immortal picture of Celtic society which, for the first time, brought home to men who were nearly our contemporaries the fact that ancient Celtic life and manners had existed almost down to their days—the novel *Waverley*.

It seems extremely probable that, in a particular stage of society, this personal service to the Chief or King was everywhere rendered in expectation of a reward in the form of a gift of land. The Companions of the Teutonic kings, in Continental Europe, shared largely in the <term>Benefices</term>—grants of Roman provincial land that were fully peopled and stocked. In ancient England, the same class is believed to have been the largest grantees of public land next to the Church; and doubtless we have here part of the secret of the mysterious change by which a new nobility of <term>Thanes</term>, deriving dignity and authority from the King, absorbed the older nobility of <term>Eorls</term>. <header>CHEAPNESS OF LAND IN PRIMITIVE TIMES. LECT. V.</header>

Mr. Thorold Rogers, writing about a much later period and basing his opinion on the surviving evidence of returns from manor lands, speaks of land as the ‘cheapest commodity of the Middle Ages.’ The practical difficulty was not obtaining land, but the tools for making it productive; and therefore, in a society relatively older than any Teutonic society of which we have any distinct knowledge—the very society which the <term>Brehon tracts</term> enable us to understand—it may very well have been that the purpose of a legal case at court was much less to obtain land than to obtain cattle. The Chief, as I have already said, was above all things wealthy in flocks and herds. He was a military leader, and a great part of his wealth must have been the spoils of war, but in his civil capacity he increased his cattle through his growing power to take over the wasteland for pasture, and through a system of distributing his herds among the tribesmen, which will be described in the next Lecture. The Companion who followed him into battle, or was ready to do so, must have been enriched by his generosity; and thus, if he was already noble, he became greater; if he was not noble, the path to nobility lay through wealth. <header>LECT. V. KAFIR CHIEFS.</header>

Celts. The Rev. H. Dugmore, in a most interesting volume called a *Compendium of Kafir Laws and Customs*, published at the Wesleyan Missionary Press, Mount Coke, British Kaffraria, writes the following concerning the most advanced of the South African native races, the Kafirs or Zulus (p. 27):

> “As cattle constitute the sole wealth of the people, they are also their only medium for transactions involving exchange, payment, or reward. The followers of a chief serve him in exchange for cattle; and it is not expected that he could maintain his influence, or indeed secure any number of followers, if he were unable to provide them with what simultaneously constitutes their money, food, and clothing. He requires, then, a constant fund from which to satisfy his dependents; and the size of the fund required may be judged by the nature of the demands made upon him. His retinue, court, or whatever it may be called, consists of men from all parts of the tribe—the young, the clever, and the brave—who come to perform court service for a time so that they may obtain cattle to provide them with the means of procuring wives, arms, or other desired objects. Upon obtaining these, they return to their homes and give place to others. Thus, the immediate retinue of a chief is continually changing and constitutes a permanent drain on his resources.”

Mr. Dugmore goes on to state that the sources of the chief’s wealth are the inherited cattle of his father,

<header>->CATTLE-STEALING. LECT. V.<-</header>

The remarkable role played by cattle in ancient Irish society will, I hope, be made more understandable in the next Lecture. In the meantime, let me observe that the two Celtic societies in these islands that kept their ancient customs the longest were both notoriously prone to cattle raiding. Lord Macaulay, when speaking of Irish cattle-stealing, sometimes—I must admit—seems to me to express himself as if he thought the practice was caused by some native flaw in the Irish character. However, it was undoubtedly what Mr. Tylor has taught us to call a "survival"—an ancient and deep-rooted habit that, in this case, continued because of the misfortune that denied Ireland the essential requirement for modern legal concepts: a strong central government. The very same practice, among the Celts of the Scottish Highlands and the unrefined Germanic population of the Lowland Border, has almost been given the dignity of a virtue by the genius of one man <note>Sir Walter Scott, whose novels popularized the history of the Scottish Borders and Highlands.</note>. Again, turning to ‘Waverley,’ I suppose there is no truer representative of the primitive Celtic chief than Donald Bean Lean, who steals the cattle of Tully Veolan and employs a fortune-teller to predict the number of cattle <note>Originally "beeves."</note> that are likely to come his way. He is a much more authentic ‘survival’ than Fergus McIvor, who nearly abandons his cause because of a disappointment over an earldom.

<summary>The author explains that cattle-stealing in Ireland and Scotland was not a character flaw but a "survival" of ancient tribal habits caused by a lack of central government. He references Sir Walter Scott’s characters to illustrate the difference between authentic primitive chiefs and those influenced by modern ambitions.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from discussing the wealth and habits of Celtic and Germanic chiefs to an analysis of the social status of the King's Companions and the origins of nobility.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>LECT. V. THE STATUS OF THE COMPANIONS.</header>

It has been pointed out that the status of the King’s Companions was at first, in some way, servile. Whenever the relationship between the <term>Comitatus</term> and the Teutonic kings had to be expressed legally, the sections of Roman law chosen were consistently those that declare the semi-servile relationship of the Client or Freedman to his Patron. Brehon law allows us to take the same view of the equivalent class in Celtic societies. Several texts indicate that a high-ranking Chief was always expected to surround himself with unfree dependants; and you will remember that the retinue of the King of Erin was to consist not only of free tribesmen but also of a bodyguard of men bound to him by servile obligations.

As far as it goes, I completely agree with the explanation provided by Mr. Freeman regarding the original connection between servile status and the nobility with which the ancient nobility of birth has become mixed and confused.

> ‘The humble clientage,’ he says, ‘of the Roman Patrician and the noble following of the Hellenic and Teutonic leader may actually come from the same source, and may both be parts of the same primeval heritage.’ (‘Comparative Politics,’ p. 261.)

But perhaps we may allow ourselves to go a step further than this account. The <term>Comitatus</term>, or Companions of the Chief, even when they were freemen, were not necessarily or usually his close relatives. Their dependence on

<summary>This page explores the historically servile origins of the "King's Companions" (Comitatus), comparing legal frameworks in Roman, Teutonic, and Celtic (Brehon) societies. It supports the theory that modern nobility developed from a mixture of ancient birthright and humble clientage.</summary>

<keywords>King’s Companions, Comitatus, Teutonic kings, Roman law, Client, Freedman, Patron, Brehon law, Celtic societies, King of Erin, unfree dependants, Mr. Freeman, nobility, Roman Patrician, Hellenic, Comparative Politics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the social standing of the King's Companions, explaining why their relationship with the Chief was modeled on servility or fictitious kinship rather than simple friendship.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>
<header>SERVILE POSITION OF COMPANIONS. LECT. V.</header>

him, bringing friendship and affection, would place them in a well-understood position in modern societies, and on something like an equality with him; but in the beginning of things, one man was always the kinsman, the slave, or the enemy of another, and mere friendship and affection would not, by themselves, create any bond between man and man. In order for these feelings to have any reality, they would have to be seen as establishing one of the relationships known to that stage of thought. Between equals, this would be assumed or fictitious kinship. But between the Chief, who embodied the purity of tribal descent, and his associates, it would more or less have to follow the pattern of the slave’s dependence on his master; and where the Companion was not actually the Chief’s slave, the bond that connected them would very probably be adapted to the more honorable model provided by the relationship between an ex-slave and an ex-master.

<summary>This page explains that in early societies, personal bonds like friendship were only legally or socially recognized when modeled after established relationships like kinship or servitude. Because of this, the connection between a Chief and his Companions was historically framed as a form of servile dependence.</summary>

<header>LECT. VI. OXEN IN PRIMITIVE TIMES.</header>

# LECTURE VI.

## THE CHIEF AND THE LAND.

THE Brehon law tracts strongly suggest that one of the things we in modern times have most forgotten is the importance of horned cattle—not merely in the infancy of society, but at a period when it had made considerable progress toward maturity. It is almost impossible to turn a page without finding some reference to oxen, bulls, cows, heifers, and calves. Horses, sheep, pigs, and dogs appear; and bees, the producers of the greatest primitive luxury, are assigned a place as property which corresponds to their status in old Roman law. But the animals mentioned much more frequently than any others are cattle. There are a few facts regarding both etymology and legal classification which point to the former importance of oxen. *Capitale*—cattle counted by the head—gave rise to one of the most famous terms in law and one of the most famous terms in political economy: Chattels and Capital. *Pecunia* was likely the word for money used

<summary>This page introduces the central role of cattle in ancient societies as described in the Brehon law tracts, noting that cattle were the primary form of property and the origin of modern economic terms like "capital."</summary>

<keywords>Brehon law, cattle, agriculture, property law, etymology, Capital, Chattels, Pecunia, Roman law</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the etymological and legal origins of property, specifically focusing on how cattle transitioned from commodities to sacred or high-status legal assets in Roman and Hindu cultures.</meta>

<page-num>148</page-num>
<header>OXEN IN PRIMITIVE TIMES. LECT. VI.</header>

by the majority of mankind for the longest period of time. But oxen—though they have provided a modern synonym for personal property—were not, I should hardly need to say, classified in the lower order of commodities in all ancient legal systems. Ancient Roman law placed them in the highest class and grouped them with land and slaves as items of the *Res Mancipi*. As seen in several other instances, the legal status of this type of property among the Romans appears to correspond to its religious status among the Hindus. Cattle, which the oldest Sanskrit literature shows were once eaten as food, became sacred at some unknown period, and their flesh was forbidden. Eventually, two of the primary "Things which required a Mancipation" in Rome—oxen and landed property—had their equivalents in the sacred bull of Siva and the sacred land of India.

The subject has perhaps been obscured by the impression that horned cattle were only of primary importance to mankind during that pastoral stage of society which has been the theme of so much speculation that is not entirely useful. The actual evidence seems to show that their greatest value was realized when groups of men settled on plots of land and devoted themselves to the cultivation of grain crops. It is quite possible that cattle were initially valued only for their meat and milk, but it is clear that in very early times a distinct, special

<summary>This page discusses the high legal and religious status of cattle in ancient Roman and Hindu societies, noting their transition from food sources to sacred or high-value property used in agriculture.</summary>

<page-num>149</page-num>
<header>LECT. VI. CATTLE AS A MEASURE OF VALUE.</header>

In Homeric literature, they are certainly a measure of value; there seems no reason to doubt the traditional story that the earliest coined money known in Rome was stamped with the figure of an ox; and in any case, the connection between '*pecus*' (cattle) and '*pecunia*' (money) is unmistakable. Part—but by no means all—of the prominence given by the <term>Brehon</term> lawyers to horned cattle certainly arises from their usefulness in exchange. Throughout the <term>Brehon</term> tracts, fines, dues, rents, and returns are calculated in livestock; not exclusively in cattle, but nearly so. Two standards of value are constantly referred to: the '<term>sed</term>' and the '<term>cumhal</term>.' The '<term>cumhal</term>' is said to have originally meant a female slave, just as '*ancilla*' in medieval Latin sometimes means the price of a slave girl; but the '<term>sed</term>' is plainly used for an amount or quantity of livestock, which was probably variable to some small extent. The next stage, however, in the history of cattle is the one at which their service to mankind is greatest. They are now valued chiefly—and in some communities exclusively—for their use in tillage, for their labor, and for their manure. Their place has been very generally taken in Western Europe by horses as beasts of the plow, but even there the change was both gradual and comparatively modern; and there are still large portions of the world where the horse is exclusively employed, as it seems

I think it likely that the economic causes which led to the disuse of oxen as a medium of exchange also led to the change in their legal position which we find took place in Rome and in India. The sanctification of the ox among the Hindus, rendering its flesh unlawful as food, must certainly have been connected with the desire to preserve the animal for tillage; and its elevation to a place among the *Res Mancipi* <note>A category of important property in Roman law that required formal ceremonies to transfer ownership.</note> may well be supposed to have had the same purpose, since it made its alienation <note>The legal transfer of ownership.</note> extremely difficult, and must have greatly hindered its use in exchange. At this point, the history of horned cattle becomes unhappily mixed up with that of large portions of humanity. The same causes which we perceive altering the position of the ox and turning it into an animal partially *adscriptus glebæ* <note>Bound to the soil.</note>, undoubtedly produced also a great extension of slavery. The abundance of land, even in what are considered old countries until relatively recent times, and the scarcity of capital even in its simplest forms, seem to me to be shown in the clearest light by Mr. Thorold Rogers's deeply instructive volumes on *Agriculture and Prices during the Middle Ages*; and much in history which has been only partially understandable is ex-

The importance of horned cattle to people in a particular state of society must, it seems to me, be carefully kept in mind if we are to understand one of the most remarkable parts of the ancient Irish law which relates to the practice of "giving stock." I stated before that, although I did not draw the same conclusions from the fact, I agreed with the writers who think that the land system of ancient Ireland was theoretically based on the division of the tribe lands among the free tribesmen. The lack of capital—taking the word in its original sense—was the necessity which pressured the small holder of land and reduced him occasionally to the most desperate straits. The fact that the wealth of the Chiefs in cattle was out of proportion to their power of dealing with the tribal lands, and the fact that the tribesmen were every now and then severely pressed by the necessity of procuring the means of tillage, appear to me to supply the best explanation of the system of giving and receiving stock, to which two sub-sections of the <term>Senchus Mor</term> are devoted: the <term>Cain-Saerrath</term> and the <term>Cain-Aigillne</term>, the Law of <term>Saer-stock tenure</term> and the Law of <term>Daer-stock tenure</term>.

<header>LECT. VI. ROMAN AND FEUDAL SOCIETY.</header>

the various agencies by which the relation of Lord and Vassal was first established. It was by these agencies that the Western Europe of Roman despotism was transformed into the Western Europe of feudal sovereignties. Nothing can be more strikingly different in appearance than the states of society seen on either side of the stormy interval filled with the movement and settling of the barbarian invasions. Just before that point is reached, we see a large part of mankind arranged, so to speak, on one vast level surface dominated in every part by the overshadowing authority of the Roman Emperor. On this surface they lie as many equal units, connected by no institutions that are not assumed to be the creation of positive Roman law; and between them and their sovereign, there is nothing but a host of functionaries who are his servants. When feudal Europe has been established, all this changes. Everyone has become the subordinate of someone else higher than himself, and yet exalted above him by no great distance. If I may again use an image I have used before, society has taken the form of a pyramid or cone. The great multitude of cultivators is at its base; and then it rises through ever-narrowing sections until it approaches a peak—not always visible, but always supposed to be discoverable—in the Emperor, the Pope, or God Almighty. There is strong reason to believe that

<header>LECT. VI. COMMENDATION.</header>

homage.' <term>Commendation</term>, in particular, spread across all of Western Europe with a remarkable universality of operation and a remarkable uniformity of result, and it helped to transform the ancient structure of <term>Teutonic society</term> as much as the institutions of the <term>Roman Provincials</term>. Yet there is considerable mystery regarding the motives of men for resorting to such a burdensome proceeding, and the statements of nearly all writers on the subject are general and mostly speculative. Perhaps the most precise assertion we have been able to make so far concerning the reasons why such a large part of the world voluntarily placed themselves in a condition of personal subordination is that it must have been connected with the system of civil and criminal responsibility that prevailed in those times.

Families—whether real or artificial, natural or formed by agreement—were responsible for the offenses and even the civil liabilities of their members; but corporate responsibility must have been replaced, for the convenience of everyone involved, by the responsibility of a single lord. This lord could prevent injury and pay compensation for it, and his testimony, in <term>compurgation</term> and other legal proceedings, was often assigned a weight exceeding that of several inferior persons combined. More generally, but with at least equal plausibility, we can state that the general disorder of the world had much to do with the growth of these new institutions; and that a little

->CELTIC ORIGIN OF COMMENDATION. LECT. VI.<-

a society compactly united under a feudal lord was much stronger for defense or attack than any group of kinsmen or co-villagers, and stronger than any collection of voluntary confederates. However, it would be absurd to assume that we have the materials to form a confident opinion regarding people’s motives for submitting themselves to a change that was probably suggested to or forced upon them by very different circumstances in various countries and at relatively different stages of society.

I do not wish to generalize excessively from the new information provided by the <term>Brehon law</term>, but there has long been a suspicion (I cannot call it more than that) among scholars that Celtic customs might shed some light on <term>Commendation</term>. In any case, given the scarcity of our materials, any addition to them from an authentic source is valuable. Let me restate my impression of the ancient Irish land system at the stage revealed to us by the <term>Brehon tracts</term>. The land of the tribe, whether cultivated or waste, belongs to the tribe; this remains true whether the tribe is a joint family of kinsmen or a larger and more artificial group. Theoretically, this is the principle, if the traditional view of the primitive state of things can be called a theory. However, much of the territory belonging to the larger tribes has been permanently assigned to Chiefly families or to smaller subdivisions of tribesmen, and the

<page-num>157</page-num>
# LECT. VI. THE CHIEFS AND STOCK.

land of the smaller subdivisions tends always to be divided among their members, subject to certain reserved rights of the collective brotherhood. Every significant tribe, and almost every smaller body of men contained within it, is under a Chief—whether he is one of the many tribal rulers whom the Irish records call Kings, or whether he is one of those heads of joint families whom the Anglo-Irish lawyers at a later date called the *<term>Capita Cognationum</term>*. But he is not the owner of the tribal land. He may have his own land, consisting of a private estate, an official domain, or both; and over the general tribal land, he has a general administrative authority, which is constantly growing greater over that portion of it which is unappropriated waste. He is, meanwhile, the military leader of his tribesmen, and, probably in that capacity, he has acquired great wealth in cattle. It has somehow become very important to him to distribute portions of his herds among the tribesmen, and they, on their part, occasionally find themselves, through the stress of circumstances, in pressing need of cattle to use for farming. Thus, the Chiefs appear in the <term>Brehon law</term> as perpetually "giving stock," and the tribesmen as receiving it. The remarkable thing is that out of this practice grew not only the familiar aspects of ownership, such as the right to collect rent and the obligation to pay it, together with some other features less pleasantly familiar to the student of Irish history,

<page-num>158</page-num>
<header>SAER AND DAER TENANTS. LECT. VI.</header>

but, in addition to these, nearly all the well-known incidents of feudal tenure. It is by taking stock that the free Irish tribesman becomes the *Ceile* or *Kyle*, the vassal or man of his Chief, owing him not only rent but service and homage. The exact effects of "commendation" are thus produced, and the interesting circumstance is that they are produced from a simple and intelligible motive. The transaction between Chief and Vassal is very burdensome to the latter, but the necessity which leads to it is pressing, and the force of this necessity would be greater the more primitive the society in which it arose, and the more recent its settlement on its lands. All this is especially instructive, because there is no reason whatever to suppose that Beneficiary grants and Commendation arose suddenly in the world at the disruption of the Roman Empire. They were probably, in some form or other, deeply seated among the rudimentary customs of all Aryan societies.

The new position which the tribesman assumed through accepting stock from a Chief varied according to the quantity of stock he received. If he took much stock, he sank to a much lower status than if he had taken little. On this difference in the quantity accepted turns the difference between the two great classes of Irish tenantry, the *Saer* and *Daer* tenants, between whose status and that of the free and higher base tenants of an English manor

# LECT. VI. SAER-STOCK TENANCY.

there is a resemblance not to be mistaken. The <term>Saer-stock</term> tenant, distinguished by the limited amount of stock which he received from the Chief, remained a freeman and retained his tribal rights in their entirety. The normal period of his tenancy was seven years, and at the end of it, he became entitled to the cattle that had been in his possession. Meanwhile, he had the advantage of employing them in tillage, and the Chief on his part received the 'growth and increase and milk,' the first two words implying the offspring and the manure. So far, there is nothing very remarkable in the arrangement, but it is expressly laid down that, besides this, it entitled the Chief to receive homage and manual labor; manual labor is explained to mean the service of the vassal in reaping the Chief's harvest and in assisting to build his castle or fort, and it is stated that, instead of manual labor, the vassal might be required to follow his Chief to the wars. Any large addition to the stock deposited with the <term>Saer-stock</term> tenant, or an unusual quantity accepted in the first instance by the tribesman, created the relationship between vassal and chief called <term>Daer-stock</term> tenancy. The <term>Daer-stock</term> tenant had unquestionably surrendered some portion of his freedom, and his duties are always referred to as very burdensome. The stock given to him by the Chief consisted of two portions, of which one was proportionate to the rank of the recipient, the other to the

<header>DAER-STOCK TENANCY. LECT. VI.</header>

The technical standard of the first was the tenant’s ‘honor-price,’ the fine or compensation which was payable for injuring him, and which in these ancient systems of law varies with the dignity of the person injured. The relation between the second portion of stock and the rent is elaborately defined in the <term>Brehon law</term>: ‘The proportionate stock for a calf of the value of a sack with its accompaniments, and meals for three persons in the summer, and work for three days, is three “<term>samhaisc</term>” heifers or their value’ (‘<term>Cain-Aigillne</term>,’ p. 25); or, in other words, so that the Chief may entitle himself to the calf, the meals, and the labor, he must deposit three heifers with the tenant. ‘The proportionate stock of a “<term>dartadh</term>” heifer with its accompaniment, is twelve “<term>seds</term>,”’ explained to mean twelve ‘<term>samhaisc</term>’ heifers, or six cows. And so on in many places. The rent in kind, or food-rent, which was thus proportioned to the stock received, undoubtedly developed over time into a rent payable in respect of the tenant’s land; but it is certainly a curious and unexpected fact that the rent of the class which is believed to have included a very large part of the ancient Irish tenantry did not, in its earliest form, correspond in any way to the value of the tenant’s land, but solely to the value of the Chief’s property deposited with the tenant. <page-num>161</page-num>

<header>LECT. VI. THE RIGHT OF REFECTION.</header>

stock tenant is what I expressed in the quotation I just made with the word ‘refections.’ Besides the rent in kind and the feudal services, the Chief who had given stock was entitled to come, with a company of a certain number, and feast at the <term>Daer-stock</term> tenant’s house at particular periods for a fixed number of days. This ‘right of refection,’ and the liability to it, are among the most distinctive features of ancient Irish custom. Their origin is probably explained by the fact that the Irish Chief, though far more privileged than his tenants, was little better housed and almost as poorly furnished; he could not have managed to consume at home the provisions to which his gifts of stock entitled him. But the practice had a most unhappy history. The <term>Brehon law</term> defines and limits it narrowly on all sides; however, its inconvenience and its tendency to degenerate into abuse are manifest. From it are doubtless descended those oppressions that revolted English observers of Ireland such as Spenser and Davis—the ‘coin and livery’ and the ‘cosherings’ of the Irish Chiefs—which they denounce with such indignant emphasis. Perhaps there was no Irish usage that seemed to Englishmen to so amply justify what I believe, as a whole, to have been a great mistake and a great wrong: the entire judicial or legislative abolition of Irish customs. The precautions by which the <term>Brehon</term> lawyers could fence

<header>DAER-STOCK AND METAYER TENANCY. LECT. VI.</header>

After <term>food-rent</term> and service had been provided for seven years, if the Chief died, the tenant became entitled to the stock; while, on the other hand, if the tenant died, his heirs were partly, though not entirely, relieved from their obligation. At the same time, it is very probable that <term>Daer-stock tenancy</term>, which must have begun in the necessities of the tenant, was often for that same reason rendered practically permanent.

It has frequently been conjectured that certain incidents of feudal tenure pointed back to a system similar to the one the <term>Brehon tracts</term> describe to us. The <term>Heriot</term> of <term>English Copyhold tenure</term>—the "best beast" taken by the Lord upon the death of a <term>base tenant</term>—has been explained as an acknowledgment of the Lord's ownership of the cattle with which he anciently stocked the land of his <term>villeins</term>, just as the <term>Heriot</term> of the military tenant is believed to have had its origin in a deposit of arms. <term>Adam Smith</term> recognized the great antiquity of the <term>Metayer tenancy</term>, which is still widely spread over the Continent, and of which one variety was in his day found in Scotland under the name of "<term>steelbow</term>." I am not at all surprised that, in one of the Prefaces to the official translation of the Brehon

The outward resemblance is significant, and the history of <term>Metayer tenancy</term> is so obscure that I certainly cannot claim that practices similar to those I have described did not, in some countries, contribute to its original form. However, the differences between the ancient and the modern tenancies are more important than the similarities. In <term>Metayer tenancy</term>, a landlord supplies the land and stock, while a tenant supplies only the labor and skill; but in <term>Saer</term> and <term>Daer-stock</term> tenancy, the land belonged to the tenant. Furthermore, the effect of the ancient Irish relationship was to create not merely a contractual obligation, but a social status. The tenant had his social and tribal position distinctly changed by accepting stock. Moreover, the acceptance of stock was not always voluntary. A tribesman, at one stage of Irish custom at least, was bound to receive stock from his own ‘King’—or, in other words, from the Chief of his tribe in its broadest sense—and everywhere the <term>Brehon laws</term> seem to me to describe the acceptance of stock as a hard necessity. Finally, the Tribe to which the prospective tenant belonged had in some cases a veto on his adoption of the new position, which was clearly regarded as a proceeding that encroached on tribal rights and was intended to weaken them. It seems clear to me that it was discouraged by the popular morality of the time. One of those rules, frequently found in ancient bodies of law, which are more like moral precepts than legal provisions, declares that "no man should leave a rent on his land which he did not find there."

The system I have been describing must have powerfully contributed to the dissolution of the more ancient <term>tribal organisation</term> and family organization. If the Chief who gave and the <term>Ceile</term> who accepted stock belonged to the same Tribe, the effect of the transaction was to create a relationship between them that was not entirely unlike a tribal connection, yet was still materially different from it in many respects and much more to the advantage of the chieftain. However, the superior from whom a man took stock was not always the Chief of his own <term>Sept</term> or Tribe. So far as the <term>Brehon law</term> can be said to show any favor to the new system of <term>vassalage</term>, it encourages it between a natural chief and a natural tribesman; on the other hand, it places difficulties in its way when there is an attempt to establish it between a tribesman and a strange Chief. <header>LECT. VI. THE KING OF ERIN AND THE EMPEROR.</header>

Every nobleman, as I said before, is assumed as a rule to be rich in cattle, and it appears to have been a goal for everyone to disperse his herds through the practice of giving stock. The enriched peasant who was on his way to being ennobled, the <term>Bo-Aire</term>, seems to have had <term>Ceiles</term> who accepted stock from him, just as the higher-ranking nobles did. Accordingly, the new groups formed by the Lord and his Vassals—if we may use these last terms somewhat prematurely—were sometimes completely distinct from the old groups composed of the Chief and his <term>Clan</term>. Furthermore, this new relationship was not limited to <term>Aires</term>, or noblemen, and <term>Ceiles</term>, or free but non-noble tribesmen. The <term>Bo-Aire</term> certainly, and apparently the higher Chiefs as well, accepted stock on occasion from chieftains more exalted than themselves; and in the end, to "give stock" came to mean the same thing as to assert feudal superiority, and to "accept stock" meant the same thing which, in the language of other societies, was called "<term>commendation</term>." It is strong evidence of the validity of the conclusions reached in recent years by historical scholars (including Mr. Bryce and others) regarding the deep and wide influence exercised by the Roman Empire, even in its later form, that the <term>Brehon law</term> represents the King of Ireland as "accepting stock" from the Emperor (by a legal fiction, of course). I hope it is unnecessary for me to insist on the interest that attaches to this part of the Brehon law. It has been not uncommon, based on the evidence provided by the practices of the Scottish Highlanders, to sharply contrast Celtic tribal customs with feudal rules; and doubtless between these customs and feudalism in its fully developed state, there are differences of the greatest importance. Yet, if the testimony of the Brehon tracts may be trusted, such differences arose, not from essential distinctions, but, to some extent at least, from distinctions of degree in comparative social development. The germs of feudalism lay deep in the more ancient social forms, and were ready to assert their vitality even in a country like Ireland, which, once it was Christianized, could have borrowed almost no institutions from its neighbors, cut off as it was from the Continent by

<header>LECT. VI. INDEBTEDNESS OF ANCIENT DEMOCRACIES.</header>

It is also worthy of observation that this natural growth of feudalism was not, as some eminent recent writers have supposed, entirely distinct from the process by which the authority of the Chief or Lord over the Tribe or Village was extended, but rather formed part of it. While the unappropriated waste-lands were falling into his domain, the villagers or tribesmen were coming through natural agencies under his personal power.

The Irish practice of ‘giving stock’ seems to me also to connect itself with another set of phenomena which have generally been thought to belong to a very different stage of history. We obtain from the law-tracts a picture of an aristocracy of wealth in its most primitive form; and we see that the possession of this wealth gave the nobles an immense power over the non-noble freemen who had nothing but their land. Cæsar seems to me to be clearly referring to the same state of relations in the Celtic sister society, when he speaks of the Gaulish chiefs, the <term>Equites</term>, having one principal source of their influence in the number of their debtors. (B. G., i. 4; B. G., vi. 13.) Now, you will remember how uniformly, when our knowledge of the ancient world commences, we find plebeian classes deeply indebted to aristocratic orders. <header>IMPORTANCE OF LAND AND CAPITAL. LECT. VI.</header>

This fact has been explained in many ways, and it has been plausibly suggested that it was the occurrence of repeated bad seasons which placed the small farmers of the Attic and Roman territory at the mercy of wealthy nobles. But the explanation is incomplete unless we keep in mind the chief lesson of these Brehon tracts, and remember that the relative importance of Land and Capital has been changing throughout history. The general proposition that Land is limited in quantity and is distinguished by this limitation from all other commodities which are practically capable of indefinite multiplication, has always of course been abstractly true; but, like many other principles of Political Economy, its value depends on the circumstances to which it is applied. In very ancient times land was in oversupply, while capital was extremely perishable, increased with the greatest difficulty, and held in very few hands. The proportionate importance of the two requisites of cultivation changed very slowly, and it is only quite recently that in some countries it has been almost reversed. The ownership of the instruments of <term>tillage</term> other than the land itself was thus, in early agricultural communities, a power of the first order, and, as it may be believed that a stock of the primitive capital larger than usual was very

Solon, dealing with a society in which coined money had probably only recently replaced something like the <term>seds</term> of the Brehon law, had no option available to him but the debasement of the currency and the cancellation of debts. However, he was attacking the same evil as the Brehon lawyers and was similarly interfering with that freedom of contract which takes on a very different appearance according to the condition of the society in which it exists.

<header>THE ERIC-FINE. LECT. VI.</header>

As I said before, no Irish institution was so strongly denounced by Englishmen as this one, or with such a great show of righteous indignation. As members of a wealthy community, long accustomed to a strong government, they were revolted partly by its apparent inadequacy and partly by the unjust impunity which it seemed to give to the rich man and deny to the poor. Although the English system of criminal penalties, which they sought to substitute for the Irish system of compensations, would nowadays be described by an ordinary writer in colors nearly as dark as those used by Spenser and Davis for the Irish institution, it is very possible that in the sixteenth century it would have been an advantage to Ireland to have the English procedure and the English punishments. There is much evidence that the usefulness of "eric"-fines had died out, and that they unjustly profited the rich and powerful. But that only shows that the chaos of Ireland had kept alive an institution beyond its time—one which, in the beginning, had been a great step forward from barbarism. ### LECT. VI. ETYMOLOGY OF FEUD.

found that, by this customary law, the <term>sept</term> or family to which the perpetrator of a crime belonged forfeited a considerable portion of its lands, I am not sure that they would not have regarded the institution as showing an extremely strict system of policing for that age. But in the infancy of society, a fine on the cultivating communities—of the kind later called pecuniary—was a much more severe punishment than the forfeiture of land. They had plenty of land within their domains, but very few resources for cultivating it; and it was from these latter resources that <term>compositions</term> were paid. The system naturally lost its meaning as the communities broke up and as property became unequally divided. Nevertheless, in its day, it had been a great achievement, and there are traces of it everywhere, even in Roman law, where, however, it is a mere survival.

Before I leave the subject, let me say something about the etymology of the famous word <term>Feodum</term>, Feud, or Fief. The derivation from <term>Emphyteusis</term> is now altogether abandoned, and there is general, though not quite universal, agreement that *Feodum* is descended from one or another of the numerous family of old Teutonic terms whose modern representative is the German word *Vieh* ("cattle"). ### SERVILE CLASSES ON TRIBAL TERRITORY. LECT. VI.

The Roman lawyers, in fact, tell us that it is the most comprehensive term for all of a person’s property; and in the same way, <term>feodum</term> is supposed to have come to mean "property" after originally meaning "cattle." The territory of every Irish tribe appears to have had settled on it—besides the <term>Saer</term> and <term>Daer Ceiles</term>—certain classes of people whose condition was much closer to slavery than that of the free tribesman who, by accepting livestock from the Chief, had fallen the furthest from his original position in tribal society. <page-num>173</page-num>
<header>LECT. VI. THE FUIDHIR TENANTS.</header>

The status of the classes, vaguely indicated in the Domesday Book and other ancient English records as <term>Cotarii</term> and <term>Bordarii</term>, was probably very similar to that of the <term>Sencleithes</term> and <term>Bothachs</term>; and in both cases, it has been suspected that these servile orders had an origin distinct from that of the dominant race and belonged to the older or aboriginal inhabitants of the country. Families or sub-tribes formed from them were probably laborers—"hewers of wood and drawers of water"—for the ruling tribe or its subdivisions. Others were certainly in a state of special servitude to the Chief or dependent on him; and these latter individuals were either engaged in cultivating his immediate estate and herding his cattle, or were placed by him in separate settlements on the uncultivated land of the tribe. The rent or service which they paid to him for the use of this land was apparently determined solely by the pleasure of the Chief.

By far the most important and interesting of these classes from a historical perspective was the one just described as settled by the Chief on the unappropriated tribal lands. Indeed, it has been suggested that their history is identical with that of the vast majority of the Irish people. It consisted of the <term>Fuidhirs</term>, the strangers or fugitives from other territories—men, in fact, who had broken the original tribal bond which gave them a place in the...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Fuidhir class, specifically explaining how individuals became "broken men" by losing their tribal connections and how the Brehon law managed the expulsion of violent members to protect the collective from legal liability.</meta>

<page-num>174</page-num>
<header>THE FUIDHIR TENANTS. LECT. VI.</header>

community, and who had to obtain another as best they could in a new tribe and a new place. The Brehon law shows through abundant evidence that this class must have been very numerous. The desertion of their lands by families or parts of families is mentioned repeatedly. Indeed, under certain circumstances, the breaking of the tribal bond and the flight of those who break it are possibilities clearly anticipated by the law. In the Brehon law, as in other ancient <term>juridical systems</term>, the collective responsibility of tribes, sub-tribes, and families takes the place of that individual responsibility for crime—and even, to some extent, civil obligation—which weighs upon the individual under modern institutions. However, this collective responsibility could be prevented from taking effect by forcing or persuading a member of the group who was habitually violent or committed to revenge to withdraw from its circle. The <term>Book of Aicill</term> provides the legal procedure to be followed during such an expulsion: the tribe would pay certain fines to the Chief and the Church and then formally proclaim the individual as a fugitive. Such provisions assume a specific level of order in the society to which they apply; yet we know for a fact that for many centuries it was violently disordered. The result was likely that the country was filled with "broken men," and such men could only find a home and protection by becoming <term>Fuidhir tenants</term>. In short, everything that tended to disturb the Ireland of the <term>Brehon laws</term> also tended to increase the size of this particular class.

<summary>This page explains how "broken men" were created in ancient Ireland through the breaking of tribal bonds and legal expulsion, leading many to become Fuidhir tenants. It highlights how the collective responsibility of the tribe for an individual's crimes necessitated the removal of violent members.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Fuidhir class, moving from their social origin as "broken men" to their specific legal and economic status as dependents under a Chief's protection.</meta>

<page-num>175</page-num>
# LECT. VI. RENT PAID BY FUIDHIRS.

Now, the <term>Fuidhir</term> tenant was exclusively a dependent of the <term>Chief</term> and was connected with the <term>Tribe</term> through him alone. The responsibility for crime, which in the natural state of <term>Irish society</term> belonged to the Family or Tribe, belonged, in the case of the Fuidhir, to the Chief. He, in fact, became to this class of tenants what their original tribesmen or kindred had been. Moreover, the land they cultivated in their place of refuge was not theirs, but his. They were the first <term>‘tenants at will’</term> known to Ireland, and there is no doubt that they were always theoretically subject to a <term>rackrent</term>. 

The "three rents," says the <term>Senchus Mor</term>, are the "rackrent from a person of a strange tribe, a fair rent from one of the tribe, and the stipulated rent which is paid equally by the tribe and the strange tribe." The "person from a strange tribe" is undoubtedly the Fuidhir; and though the Irish expression translated as "rackrent" cannot, of course, denote an extreme competition rent in the ancient state of the relationship between population and land, it certainly indicates an extreme rent. In one of the <gloss>glosses</gloss>, it is vividly compared to the milk of a cow which is forced to give milk every month until the end of the year. At the same time, there is no reason to suppose that, in the first instance, the Fuidhir tenants were treated oppressively by the Chiefs. The Chief had a strong interest in encouraging them; "he brings in Fuidhirs," says one of the tracts,

<header>IRISH AGRARIAN HISTORY. LECT. VI</header>

The Irish tenant, after being recognized since the beginning of the century as a social problem of the highest importance, finally became a political problem that was resolved only recently. I am not saying that there is no connection between these stages of Irish agrarian history, but there are two opposite errors we might fall into if we assume that connection was consistent throughout. First, we might be tempted to apply economic laws to an earlier period than they belong—laws which later had such a powerful effect on Ireland until their force was nearly exhausted by the consequences of the Great Famine of 1845–46. A surging population and a limited amount of cultivable land had much to do—and probably more than anything else—with Ireland's condition during that period; however, neither of these factors characterized the country at the end of the sixteenth century. Next, we might be inclined—as some highly respected writers seem to be—to date the social changes too late, specifically those changes that caused so much of the Irish soil to be subject to the unregulated Law of the Market or, to use more common language, which greatly increased the number of "tenants at will" to an unusual extent. But, by the end of the sixteenth century, our evidence shows that the Chiefs already had so much power over their tenants that any addition to it is scarcely conceivable. "The Lords of land," says Edmund Spenser, writing no later than 1596, "do not there customarily lease out their land for a term of years to their tenants, but only from year to year, or at will; nor indeed will the Irish tenant or husbandman take his land in any other way than for as long as he himself chooses. The reason for this on the tenant's part is that the landlords there are accustomed most shamefully to rack-rent their tenants, imposing <term>coign and livery</term> upon them at will, and demanding from them whatever else they please beyond their formal agreements. So the poor husbandman either dares not bind himself to the landlord for a longer term, or thinks that, by his constant freedom to change, he might better keep his landlord in awe and prevent him from doing him wrong. And the reason why the landlord will no longer make a covenant with him is that he daily looks for change and upheaval, and lingers in expectation of new social orders." Sir John Davis, writing shortly before 1613, used even stronger language: "The Lord is an absolute tyrant and the tenant a mere slave and <term>villein</term>, and in one respect more miserable than a bond-man..."

There is very little in common between the miserable position of the Irish tenant described here and the standing of even the lower sort of <term>Ceiles</term>, or <term>villeins</term>, who had taken stock from the chief. If the <term>Brehon law</term> is to be trusted, the <term>Daer Ceile</term> was to be pitied more because he had diminished his rights as a free tribesman of the same blood as the chief, rather than because he had exposed himself to unbridled oppression. Besides paying dues that were more like modern rent, he certainly was under that unfortunate liability of supplying periodic refreshment for his chief and his followers. But not only was the amount of his dues settled by the law, but the very size of the cuts of meat and the quality of the ale with which he entertained his chief were minutely and explicitly regulated. And, if one provision of the law is clearer than another, it is that the normal period of the relationship of tenancy or vassalage was not one year, but seven years. How, then, are we to explain this discrepancy? Is the explanation that the Brehon theory never actually corresponded with the facts? It may be so to some extent, but the careful student of the Brehon tracts will be inclined to think that the general bias of their writers was toward the exaggeration of the privileges of chiefs, rather than toward the overstatement of the immunities of tribesmen. <header>LECT. VI. EXPLANATIONS OF ENGLISH TESTIMONY.</header>

On the other hand, is it likely that, as some patriotic Irishmen have asserted, Spenser and Davis were under the influence of English prejudice and grossly misrepresented the facts of Irish life in their day? Plenty of prejudice of a certain kind is revealed by their writings, and I do not doubt that they were capable of occasionally misunderstanding what they saw. However, nothing they have written suggests that they were likely to willfully misdescribe facts open to their observation. I can well imagine that some aspects of the relationships between the Chiefs and tenants escaped them—possibly a good deal of freely given loyalty on one side, and of kindliness and good-humored joviality on the other. But it cannot be seriously questioned that the Irish Chief had, in their day, the power or right which they attribute to him.

The power of the Irish Chiefs and their severity toward their tenants in the sixteenth century being admitted, they have been explained, as I stated before, by supposing that the Norman nobles who gradually assumed Irish chieftainships—the Fitzgeralds, the Burkes, and the Barrys—abused an authority which, in native hands, would have been subject to natural limitations, and thus set a bad example for all the Chiefs of Ireland.
