<header>STATUS OF THE FUIDHIR. LECT. VI.</header>

...plausible theory of the cause of change to Dr. Sullivan, who, in his *Introduction* (p. cxxvi), has suggested that it was determined by the steady multiplication of *Fuidhir* tenants. It must be remembered that this class of persons would not be protected by the primitive or natural institutions arising from a community of blood. The *Fuidhir* was not a tribesman but an alien. In all societies held together by kinship, the position of the person who has lost or broken the bond of union is always extraordinarily miserable. He has not only lost his natural place within them, but they have no room for him anywhere else. The wretchedness of the outcast in India—understood as the man who has lost or been expelled from his caste—does not arise from his having been degraded from a higher to a lower social standing, but from his having no standing whatever; there is no other order of society open to receive him when he has descended from his own. It was true that the *Fuidhir*, although he had lost the multifaceted protection of his family and tribe, was not actually exposed to violent wrongdoing. From that, he was protected by the new Chief to whom he had attached himself, but between him and this Chief there was nothing. The principle would always be that he was at the mercy of the Chief. At the most, some customs favorable to him might establish themselves through the passage of time, but they would have none of the obligatory

The "refections" <note>Formal meals or provisions provided to a lord by a tenant.</note> of the Brehon law would, through a miserable degradation, become (to borrow the language of Spenser and Davis) coin and livery, cuttings, cosherings, and spendings, in the case of the <term>Fuidhirs</term>. Meanwhile, there were causes at work, powerfully and for long periods of time, to increase the numbers of this class. Even those Irishmen who believe that in the distant past there was once a tolerably well-ordered Ireland admit that for many centuries their country was racked with perpetual disturbance. Danish piracies, internal feuds, Anglo-Norman attempts at conquest that were never consistently carried out or thoroughly completed, the very existence of the Pale, and—above all—the policy directed from it of playing the Chiefs beyond its borders against one another, are acknowledged by all to have distracted the island with civil war, however the responsibility for it is to be assigned. But this process is one which must have broken up tribes far and wide, and broken tribes imply a multitude of "broken men." It is, therefore, a highly plausible conjecture that the tenantry of the Irish Chiefs, whose sufferings provoked the indignation of Spenser and Davis, consisted largely of <term>Fuidhirs</term>.

The explanation may, however, be carried beyond this point. You will remember the passage I quoted from Hunter’s *Orissa*, which shows how a tenantry enjoying hereditary rights is harmed—even under a government that strictly enforces peace and order—by a large immigration of cultivators who are dependent on the landlord or <term>Zemindar</term>. They reduce the amount of available waste land by their appropriations; and, although they do not compete directly for the anciently cultivated land with the tenants who have hereditary rights, they greatly raise the standard of rent in the long run. At the same time, they provide the landlord with the power to exact that rent—power which in ancient Ireland consisted of the Chief’s own physical force, and which in modern India consists of the money that sets the legal system in motion. I have no doubt whatsoever that a large increase in <term>Fuidhir</term> tenants would always seriously worsen the position of the tenants holding <term>Saer-stock</term> and <term>Dear-stock</term> tenure.

<header>LECTURE VII. ABOLITION OF NATIVE IRISH TENURES.</header>

# LECTURE VII.

## ANCIENT DIVISIONS OF THE FAMILY.

"Before the establishment of the (English) common law, all the possessions within the Irish territories followed either the course of <term>Tanistry</term> or the course of <term>Gavelkind</term>. Every <term>Signory</term> or <term>Chiefry</term>, along with the portion of land which passed with it, went without partition to the <term>Tanist</term>, who always came in by election or by force, and not by descent; but all inferior tenancies were divisible among males in <term>Gavelkind</term>." (Sir J. Davis' Reports, "The Case of Gavelkind," Hil. 3, Jac. 1, before all the Judges.) <note>Hil. 3, Jac. 1 refers to the Hilary legal term in the third year of the reign of King James I (1605).</note>

This passage occurs in one of the famous cases in which the Anglo-Irish Judges affirmed the illegality of the native Irish land tenures. They declared the English common law to be in force in Ireland, and from that point forward, the eldest son succeeded as heir-at-law both to lands attached to a <term>Signory</term> and to estates which had been divided according to the peculiar Irish custom called <term>Gavelkind</term> here. The Judges knew thoroughly that they were making

It will be helpful for us to first consider the so-called <term>Gavelkind</term> of Ireland, which is described by Sir John Davis as follows:

> "By the Irish custom of <term>Gavelkind</term>, the lower tenancies were divisible among all the males of the <term>Sept</term>, both illegitimate and legitimate; and, after a division was made, if any member of the <term>Sept</term> died, his portion was not divided among his sons, but the Chief of the <term>Sept</term> made a new division of all the lands belonging to that <term>Sept</term>, and gave everyone his share according to his seniority."

The latter was a much smaller body, whose proximity to a common ancestor was close enough to allow for their kinship to be either a fact or believed to be a fact. It apparently corresponded to the small Highland communities observed in Scotland by an English officer of Engineers around 1730. ‘They (the Highlanders) are divided into tribes or clans under chiefs or chieftains, and each clan is again divided into branches from the main stock, which have chieftains over them. These are subdivided into smaller branches of fifty or sixty men, who trace their origin from their particular chieftains.’ (Quoted by Skene, ‘Highlanders,’ i. p. 156.) Such a body, as I have already stated, seems to be the <term>Joint Family</term> well known to the Hindus, but continued as a corporate unit (which is very rarely the case in India) through several successive generations. There is no difference in principle, and little in practical effect, between the mode of succession described by Davis and the way in which a Hindu <term>Joint Family</term> is affected by the death of one of its members. All the property being held in common, and all earnings being brought into the ‘common chest or purse,’ the end of any one life would have the effect, potentially if not actually, of distributing the dead man’s share among all the kindred united in the family group. dissolution of the <term>Joint Family</term>, the distribution of its property was not *per capita* (by the head) but *per stirpes* (by the branch), this would correspond to what Davis probably means when he describes the Chief as giving to each man "according to his antiquity."

The special novelty of the information provided to us by ancient <term>Irish law</term> consists in its revealing to us a society of the <term>Aryan race</term> that was settled, indeed, on the land and much influenced by its settlement, but which preserved an exceptional number of the ideas and rules belonging to the time when <term>kinship</term>, and not the land, was the basis of <term>social union</term>. There is, therefore, nothing extraordinary in our finding among the ancient customs of the Irish an institution suggesting the "<term>natural communism</term>" of the primitive forms of property as much as this Irish <term>Gavelkind</term> does. This "<term>natural communism</term>," I have repeatedly urged, does not arise from any theory or *à priori* assumption as to the best or fairest mode of dividing the land of a community, but from the simple impossibility, according to primitive notions, of making a distinction between a number of kinsmen solely connected by their real or assumed descent from a <term>common ancestor</term>. The natural solvent of this communism is the land itself upon which the kindred are settled. As the common ancestry fades away into indistinctness, and the community comes to consider itself less an assemblage of <term>blood-relations</term> than a body of <term>co-villagers</term>,

<summary>This page explains that Irish Gavelkind was a form of "natural communism" rooted in kinship ties rather than land ownership. <page-num>189</page-num>
<header>LECT. VII. EXTANT EXAMPLES OF IRISH GAVELKIND.</header>

each household clings with increasing tenacity to the allotment which it has once obtained, and re-divisions of the land among the whole community, whether at fixed periods or upon a death, become rarer and rarer, and at last cease altogether, or survive only as a tradition. In this way, the widely diffused but modified form of tribal succession, which in England is called <term>Gavelkind</term>, is at last established; the descendants of the latest holder take his property, to the exclusion of everybody else, and the rights of the portion of the community outside the family dwindle to a veto on sales, or to a right of controlling the modes of cultivation. Nevertheless, surveying the <term>Aryan world</term> as a whole, and looking to societies in which some fragments of the ancient social organization still survive, we can discover forms of succession or property which come surprisingly near to the <term>Irish Gavelkind</term> described by <term>Davis</term>. The best example of this occurs in a practice which existed down to our own day over a large part of Russia. The principle was that each household of the village was entitled to a share of the village-lands proportional to the number of adult males it contained. Every death, therefore, of a grown-up man diminished *pro tanto* the share of the household, and every member of it grown to manhood increased its lot in the cultivated area. There was a fixed unit of acreage corresponding to the extent of soil cultivable by one man's labor.

<header>DIFFICULTIES SUGGESTED BY IRISH GAVELKIND. LECT. VII.</header>

The principal distinction between this system and that which seemed so monstrous and unnatural to Sir John Davis is that, under the first, the re-division took place not as each death occurred, but at stated intervals. I must not, indeed, be understood to mean that I think the distinction is unimportant. It is very possible that redistributions of a common fund upon deaths may mark a more advanced stage in the history of Property than periodical redistribution, and that the recognition of interests for an entire life may have preceded and paved the way for the final allotment of permanent shares to separate households. Until, however, this last point has been reached, all the modes of re-division known to us are plainly referable to the same principle.

The difficulty suggested by the account in the "Case of <term>Gavelkind</term>" is thus not a difficulty in believing it if it stood by itself, or if it were made with less generality. But it is distinctly stated that all the lands in Ireland which did not descend by the rule of <term>Tanistry</term> descended by the rule of <term>Gavelkind</term>. The indications of the state of law or custom furnished by the <term>Brehon tracts</term> certainly seem to me inconsistent with this assertion. <header>LECT. VII. DISCREPANCIES IN IRISH EVIDENCE.</header>

Dr. Sullivan, who appears to have consulted many more original authorities than have been translated or published, expresses himself as if he thought that the general law of succession in Ireland was closely similar to the Gavelkind of Kent. "According to the Irish custom, property descended at first only to the male heirs of the body, each son receiving an equal share. . . . .Ultimately, however, daughters appear to have become entitled to inherit all, if there were no sons" (Introd., p. clxx).

I do not expect that the apparent contradiction between the Brehon tracts and the language of Davis and his contemporaries regarding the Irish law of land succession will be fully explained until the whole of the ancient legal literature is available to the world; but in the meantime, it is a plausible explanation for the discrepancy that the Irish and the English writers focused on different sets of phenomena. <header>->PROBABLE VARIETY OF IRISH TENURES. LECT. VII.<-</header>

The statements of English authorities on the point are extremely precise. They affirm that 'no civil habitations were erected, and no enclosure or improvement was made of land where <term>Gavelkind</term> was in use,' and they say that this was especially the case in Ulster, 'which was all one wilderness.' Nevertheless, it is extremely probable that another set of facts justified the indications given by the <term>Brehon tracts</term>, and that there were other modes of succession known besides succession by <term>Tanistry</term> on the one hand, and besides on the other hand the peculiarly archaic system under which each lapsed share was at once divided between all the members of the <term>Sept</term>. Such an institution as the last, though exceptional circumstances may keep it alive, contains within itself a principle of decay. Each household included in the <term>Joint Family</term> gains a firmer hold on its share of the lands as the distance increases from the common ancestor; and finally appropriates it, transmitting it exclusively to offshoots from its own branch. Nothing is more likely than that there were frequent examples of Irish septs with their land-customs in this condition; and it is still more probable that usages of a similarly modern stamp prevailed in estates permanently severed or 'booked off' from tribal possession or established at a distance from the main seat of the tribe. It is true that, in society based on kinship, each family separated from the rest tends itself to expand into a joint family or

<header>LECT. VII. PRIVATE ESTATES OF CHIEFS.</header>

Thus, setting the rule of <term>Tanistry</term> aside, I can well imagine that Irish <term>Gavelkind</term>, the modern <term>Gavelkind</term> known in Kent, and many forms of inheritance between the two co-existed in Ireland. Both the English and Irish legal authorities had their own prejudices, which might lead them to focus their attention only on specific practices. The <term>Brehon</term> writers seem to me clearly biased in favor of property descending through individual families, which appealed to them as lawyers, as friends of the Church, and (perhaps) as well-wishers to their country. On the other hand, the strange, ancient form of ownership that he called <term>Gavelkind</term> would fascinate the attention of an Englishman living in Ireland. He would certainly not have the curiosity about it that we feel today, but surprise and dislike would fix his attention on it, perhaps preventing him from recognizing how widely institutions of the opposite type were spread.

This interpretation of the apparent contradiction between our sources is consistent with the very little we know about the actual division of land in ancient Ireland. <header>ACTUAL DIVISIONS OF LAND BY CHIEFS. LECT. VII.</header>

had a large estate held under what the English lawyers considered the inferior tenure. There are two recorded cases in which Irish Chiefs of significant status distributed such estates among their relatives. In the fourteenth century, Connor More O’Brien—a chief who had children of his own—is said to have divided his land based on principles that must have more or less corresponded to those condemned by the Anglo-Irish Judges. He assigned the majority of the estate to the various families of the <term>Sept</term> formed by his own relatives. For himself, he reserved only one-sixth of one-half of one-third, and even this sixth he divided among his three sons, keeping only a rent for himself. But at the end of the fifteenth century, Donogh O’Brien—son of Brien Duff, son of Connor, King of Thomond—divided all his lands among his eleven sons, keeping for himself only the mansion and the estate in its vicinity. The difference between the two cases (which, notably, are separated by at least a century) appears clear enough to me. In the first case, the land had remained undivided for several generations; in the second, it had been divided periodically. Connor More O’Brien was distributing the inheritance of a joint family; Donogh O’Brien was distributing that of a family. (Vallancey, ‘Collectanea de Rebus Hibernicis,’ i. 264, 265.)

<page-num>195</page-num>
<header>LECT. VII. SUCCESSION OF MODES OF DIVISION. 195</header>

ancient example, Connor More O’Brien appears to have paid regard to the various *stirpes* or stocks into which the descendants of the original founder of his family had branched out. The principle he followed, I suppose, is the same as that pointed out by Davis when he speaks of the chief dividing a lapsed share between the members of a sept ‘according to their antiquity.’ The proceeding deserves to be noted as showing an advance on the oldest known tribal customs. In the most archaic forms of the Joint Family, and of the institution which grew out of it, the Village-Community, these distributions are *per capita*; no one person who is entitled takes more than another, whether the whole estate or a portion is divided, and no respect is paid to the particular way in which a given individual has descended from the common ancestor. Under a more advanced system, the distribution is *per stirpes*; careful attention is paid to the lines into which the descendants of the ancestor of the joint family have separated, and separate rights are reserved to them. Finally, the stocks themselves escape from the sort of shell constituted by the Joint Family; each man’s share of the property, now periodically divided, is distributed among his direct descendants at his death. At this point, property in its modern form has been established; but the Joint Family has not wholly ceased to influence successions. When direct descendants fail, it is even now the rule

<header>DISTRIBUTION DURING LIFE. LECT. VII.</header>

of the Joint Family which determine the taking of the inheritance. Collateral successions, when they are distant, follow the more primitive form of the old institution and are <term>*per capita*</term> (by the head); when they involve closer relatives, they are adjusted to its more modern shape and are <term>*per stirpes*</term> (by the line of descent).

The remark has further to be made that both Connor O'Brien and Donogh O'Brien divided their own land among their sons or kindred during their own lifetime. Like Laertes in the *Odyssey* and like Lear in the tragedy of Shakespeare, the old Chief, as his vigor decays, parts with his power and retains but a fraction of the property he had administered; and the poorer freeman becomes one of those "senior" pensioners of the tribe so often referred to in the tracts. Precisely the same practice is recognized, and even (as some think) enjoined, by the more archaic bodies of Hindu jurisprudence. The principle is that the right of each member of a family accrues at his birth; and, as the family has in theory a perpetual existence, there is no particular reason why, if the property is divided at all, it should be exclusively divided at a death. The power of distributing inheritances vested in the Celtic chiefs has been made the basis of some very doubtful theories, but I have no doubt it is essentially the same institution as the humble privilege which is reserved to the Hindu father by the <term>*Mitakshara*</term>. <header>LECT. VII. THE BIRTHRIGHT.</header>

Under some systems of Hindu law, the father, when making a distribution of property during his lifetime, is entitled to retain a double share, and by some Indian customs the eldest son, when dividing the patrimony with his brothers, takes twice as much as the others. There are many traces of the usage in this last form in a variety of communities. It is, for instance, the "birthright" of the Hebrew patriarchal history. I mention it particularly because it seems to me to be sometimes improperly confused with the right conferred by what we call the rule of Primogeniture. But the double share is rather given as the reward or (perhaps we should say) the security for impartial distribution, and we find it often coupled with the right to take exclusively such things as are deemed incapable of partition—the family house, for instance, and certain utensils. The proof that it is not essentially a privilege of the eldest son is found in the circumstances that it is sometimes enjoyed by the father and sometimes by the youngest of the sons, and in this way it is connected with our own custom of Borough English, of which I shall have more to say presently. <page-num>198</page-num>
<header>PRIMOGENITURE LECT. VII</header>

cession and that which we call Primogeniture. The first is descended from a custom of the tribe; the last, to which I now pass, seems to me traceable to the special position of the Chief.

The Brehon tracts translated at this time do not add much to the knowledge we already possessed regarding the Irish customs that correspond to the practice of exclusive succession by the eldest son; and Primogeniture remains what I called it thirteen years ago in '*Ancient Law*' (p. 227): "one of the most difficult problems of historical jurisprudence." The first of the difficulties surrounding it is the total absence, before a specific era in history, of recorded precedents for any such method of succession to property. It was unknown to the Hellenic world. It was unknown to the Roman world. It was unknown to the Jews, and apparently to the entire Semitic world. In the records of all these societies, there are traces of great differences between the succession of males and the succession of females; but there was nothing like the exclusive succession of a single son to property, although the passing of sovereignties to the eldest son of the last reigning king was a familiar fact, and although the Greek philosophers had conjectured that, in an earlier state of society than theirs, the smaller groups of men—families and villages—had been governed by eldest son after eldest son.

<summary>This page explores the origins of primogeniture, noting that while it may be traced to the position of a chief, there is a lack of historical precedent for exclusive property succession by the eldest son in ancient Hellenic, Roman, or Semitic societies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of primogeniture, moving from its absence in antiquity to its development following the movement of Teutonic tribes into Western Europe.</meta>

<page-num>199</page-num>
<header>LECT. VII. ANCIENT FORMS OF PRIMOGENITURE.</header>

Even when the Teutonic races spread over Western Europe, they did not bring with them Primogeniture as their ordinary rule of succession. The <term>allodial</term> property of the Teutonic freeman—that share which he had theoretically received at the original settlement of the brotherhood to which he belonged on their domain—was divided at his death, when it was divided at all, equally between his sons or equally between his sons and daughters. It is quite certain, however, that the appearance of Primogeniture in the West and its rapid diffusion must be connected with the invasion of the barbarians, and with the tribal ideas reintroduced by them into the Roman world. At this point, however, we encounter another difficulty. The Primogeniture which first meets us is not uniformly the Primogeniture with which we are now familiar. The right of the eldest son sometimes gives way to the right of the eldest male relative of the deceased, and occasionally it seems as if neither the succession of the eldest son nor that of the eldest relative could take effect without election or confirmation by the members of the aggregate group to which both belong.

As usual, we have to look for living illustrations of the ancient system in the customs of the Hindus. The Family, according to the Hindu theory, is despotically governed by its head; but if he dies and the Family separates at his death, the property is equally

<header>MANAGER OF JOINT FAMILY. LECT. VII</header>

If, however, the Family does not separate, but allows itself to expand into a <term>Joint Family</term>, we have the exact mixture of election and doubtful succession which we find in the early examples of European primogeniture. The eldest son, and after him his eldest son, is ordinarily the manager of the affairs of the <term>Joint Family</term>, but his privileges theoretically depend on election by the brotherhood, and may be set aside by it. When they are set aside, it is generally in favor of a brother of the deceased manager, who, on the score of greater age, is assumed to be better qualified than his nephew for administration and business. In ancient <term>Irish society</term> the <term>Joint Family</term>, continued through many generations, has grown first into the <term>Sept</term> and then into the <term>Clan</term>, contracting a greater degree of artificiality in proportion to its enlargement. The importance, meanwhile, of the <term>Chief</term> to the <term>Tribe</term> has rather increased than diminished, since he is no longer merely administrator of its civil affairs but its leader in war. The system produced from these elements appears to me sufficiently intelligible. The veneration of the <term>Tribe</term> is not attracted by individuals of the Chieftain’s family, but by the family itself, as representing the purest blood of the entire brotherhood. It chooses its head and leader (save on the very rarest occasions) from this family, and there are instances of the choice being systemati-

<header>ORIGIN OF PRIMOGENITURE. LECT. VII.</header>

Primogeniture, therefore, considered as a rule of succession to property, appears to me to be a product of tribal leadership in its decay. Some such system as that represented by the Irish <term>Tanistry</term> probably belonged at one time to all the tribal communities which overran the Roman Empire, but no precise assertion can be made as to the stage in their history at which it began to be modified, especially since Sohm's investigations (in his *Fränkische Reichs-und Gerichtsverfassung*) have shown us how considerably the social organization of some of these communities had been affected by central or royal authority in the interval between the observations of Tacitus and the writing of the Salic Law. But I think we may safely conjecture that the transition from the older to the newer Primogeniture took place everywhere under circumstances nearly the reverse of those which kept <term>Tanistry</term> so long alive in Ireland. Wherever some degree of internal peace was maintained during tolerably long periods of time, wherever an approach was made to the formation of societies of the distinctive modern type, wherever military and

<page-num>208</page-num>
<header>LECT. VII. TRANSFORMATION OF PRIMOGENITURE.</header>

As civil institutions began to group themselves around the central authority of a king, the value of strategic ability in the subordinate chiefs diminished, and in the smaller brotherhoods, the respect for purity of blood had unchecked influence. The most natural object of this respect is the person who most directly derives his blood from the last ruler; thus, the eldest son, even if he is a minor, comes to be preferred in the succession over his uncle. In the absence of sons, the succession may even pass to a woman. There are many indications that this transformation of ideas was gradual. The disputes among great Highland families regarding the title to the chieftainship of particular clans appear to date from a period when there was still a conflict between the old principle of succession and the new one. At a relatively later period, when tribal customs had been replaced by feudal rules throughout most of Western Europe, there was a visible uncertainty about those rules that affected succession. Glanville, writing of English military <term>tenures</term> in the later part of the reign of Henry II, observes: "When anyone dies, leaving a younger son and a grandson (the child of his eldest son), great doubt exists as to which of the two the law prefers in the succession to the other—whether the son or the grandson. Some think the younger son has more right to the inheritance than the grandson . . . but others incline to think that the grandson"

This transition created historical legal confusion, specifically regarding whether a younger son or a grandson through the eldest son should inherit.</summary>

<header>BRUCE AND BALIOL. LECT. VII.</header>

This ancient doubt has left traces of itself in literature no less than in history, since it clearly affects the plot of Shakespeare’s *Hamlet*; but the very question of principle arose between the descendants of daughters in the controversy between Bruce and Baliol. The succession to the Crown of Scotland was ultimately settled, as it would have been in earlier times, by what amounted to a national election, but the decision of Edward I in favor of Baliol was undoubtedly in accordance with principles which were gaining ground everywhere, and I quite agree with Mr. Burton (ii. 249) that the celebrity of the dispute and the full consideration given to it did much to settle the rule which prevailed in the end: that all of the descendants of an elder child must be exhausted before those of the younger had a claim. When, however, the eldest son had once taken the place of his uncle as the heir to the humbler chieftaincies, he doubtless also obtained that ‘portion of land attached to the <term>Signory</term> or <term>Chiefry</term> which went without partition to the <term>Tanaist</term>;’ and, as each community gradually settled down into comparative peace under royal or central authority, this <term>demesne</term>, as it was afterward called, must have assumed more and more the character of mere property descending according to the rule of <term>primogeniture</term>. <header>LECT. VII. ABOLITION OF TANISTRY.</header>

One vestige of this later course of change may perhaps be traced in the noble tenure once widely extended on the Continent, and called in French '*Parage*,' under which the near kinsmen of the eldest son still took an interest in the family property, but held it from him as his peers. There were, however, other causes than those just stated which led to the great development of <term>Primogeniture</term> in the early part of the Middle Ages, but for an examination of them I may be allowed to refer to the work of mine which I mentioned above (*Ancient Law*, pp. 232 *et seq.*).

I do not think that the rejection of the legality of <term>Tanistry</term>, and the substitution of the rule of <term>Primogeniture</term> for it, can justly be reckoned among the mistakes or crimes of the English in Ireland. The practice had been perpetuated in the country by its disorders, which preserved little groups of kinsmen and their petty chiefs in an unnatural vitality; and probably Sir John Davis does not speak too harshly of it when he charges it with "making all possessions uncertain, and bringing confusion, barbarism, and incivility." The decision against the Irish <term>Gavelkind</term> was far less justifiable. <header>IRELAND AND INDIA. LECT. VII.</header>

<page-num>207</page-num>
<header>LECT. VII. IRELAND, INDIA, AND RUSSIA.</header>

Courts. But there is a closer and more unfortunate similarity between some of the English experiments in Ireland and those tried in India. Under an Act of the twelfth year of Queen Elizabeth, the <term>Lord Deputy</term> was empowered to take surrenders and regrant estates to the <term>Irishry</term> <note>the Irish leaders or clans</note>. The Irish lords, says Davis, "made surrenders of entire territories, and obtained grants of the whole again to themselves only, and none other, and all in <term>demesne</term> <note>land held by the lord for his own use</note>. In the passing of these grants, there was no care taken of the inferior <term>septs</term> <note>clans or family groups</note> of people. . . . So that upon every such surrender or grant, there was but one freeholder made in a whole territory, which was the lord himself; all the rest were but tenants at will, or rather tenants in <term>villenage</term> <note>a status of feudal servitude or serfdom</note>." There are believed to be many Indian <term>joint-families</term> or <term>septs</term> which, in their later form of <term>village-communities</term>, had the whole of their lands similarly conferred on a single family out of their number, or on a royal tax collector outside them, under the earliest Indian settlements. The error was not in introducing absolute ownership into Ireland or India, but in the distribution of the rights of which property is made up. How, indeed, this distribution shall be wisely and justly made—when the time has fully come for replacing collective property with individual property by a conscious act of the State—is a problem which tests to the utmost the statesmanship of the most advanced era, even when motivated by the highest benevolence and informed with the widest knowledge.

<summary>The author discusses how English officials in Ireland and India mistakenly granted entire communal territories to single lords, effectively stripping the lower clans of their traditional rights and turning them into mere tenants. He argues that the primary challenge for a modern state is not the concept of individual property itself, but the just distribution of rights when transitioning away from collective ownership.</summary>

<header>DIVISIONS OF THE IRISH FAMILY. LECT. VII.</header>

The Irish practice of <term>Tanistry</term> connects to the rule of <term>Primogeniture</term>, and Irish <term>Gavelkind</term> connects to the rules of succession most widely followed among both the Eastern and Western branches of the <term>Aryan race</term>; but there are some passages in the <term>Brehon tracts</term> which describe an internal division of the Irish Family, a classification of its members, and a corresponding system of succession to property, that is extremely unlike any arrangement which we, with our modern ideas, can imagine growing out of blood-relationship. Perhaps only a few years ago, these passages would have been regarded as having too little interest in proportion to their difficulty for anyone to think it worth their while to spend much effort on their interpretation. But there are reasons why we cannot completely ignore them.

<header>LECT. VII. FAMILY NOT DIVIDED ACCORDING TO DEGREES.</header>

At the Table of Prohibited Degrees, or when the law student turns to his Blackstone and examines the Table of Descents, he possibly knows that disputes have arisen about the rights and duties proper to be adjusted to these scales of relationship; but it perhaps has never occurred to him that any other view of the nature of relationship than that upon which they are based could possibly be entertained. Yet here in the *Book of Aicill* is a conception of kinship and of the rights flowing from it altogether different from that which appears in the Tables of Degrees and of Descents. The groups are not formed upon the same principles, nor distinguished from one another on the same principles. The English Tables are based upon a classification by degrees—upon identity in the number of descents by which a given class of persons are removed from a given person. But the ancient Irish classification obviously turns upon nothing of the sort. A <term>Geilfine</term> class may consist of a father and four sons who are not in the same degree, and the Brehon writers even speak of its consisting of a father, son, grandson, great-grandson, and great-great-grandson, which is a conceivable case of <term>Geilfine</term> relationship, though it can scarcely have been a common one. This singular family organization suggests, however, a question which, in the present state of inquiry on the subject that occupies us, cannot fairly be avoided. <header>LECT. VI. DESCRIPTIVE AND CLASSIFICATORY SYSTEMS. 213</header>

other of two distinct systems, which Mr. Morgan calls respectively the Descriptive and the Classificatory systems. The time available to us will only allow me to explain his meaning very briefly. The Descriptive system is the one to which we are accustomed. It has come to us from Canon law, or else from Roman law—more particularly as declared in the <term>118th Novel of Justinian</term>—but it is not at all confined to societies deeply affected by Civil and Canon law. Its essence consists of giving separate names to the classes of relatives who are formed by family members removed by the same number of descents from yourself (the <term>ego</term> or <term>propositus</term>) or from a common ancestor. Thus, your uncle is related to you in the third degree: there is one degree or step from yourself to your father or mother, a second from your father or mother to their parents, and a third from those parents to their other children, who are your uncles. And 'uncle' is a general name for all male relatives related to you in this third degree. The other names used under the Descriptive system are among the words in most common use; yet it should be noted that the system cannot be carried very far in practice. We speak of uncle, aunt, nephew, niece, and cousin; but then we move on to great-uncle, grand-nephew, and so forth, and eventually lose our way among complications of 'great' and 'grand' until we cease to distinguish our

<summary>The author explains the Descriptive system of kinship, derived from Roman and Canon law, which categorizes relatives based on the specific number of degrees or steps they are removed from a common ancestor. This system uses common terms like 'uncle' and 'cousin' but becomes increasingly complex and difficult to track as the distance of relationship increases.</summary>

<page-num>214</page-num>
<header>THE CLASSIFICATORY SYSTEM OF KINSHIP. LECT. VII.</header>

distant relatives by specific names. Technical <term>Roman law</term> went significantly further than we do with the specific <term>nomenclature</term> of relatives; however, there is reason to believe that the common Latin dialects were more limited, and no <term>Descriptive system</term> can continue this process indefinitely. On the other hand, the <term>Classificatory system</term> groups relatives into classes, often large ones, which do not necessarily correlate with degrees of <term>kinship</term>. Under this system, a man's father and his uncles are grouped together—sometimes his paternal uncles, sometimes his maternal uncles, sometimes both—and they might all be called his "fathers" without distinction. Similarly, a man's brothers and all his male cousins may be categorized together and called his brothers. The general effect of this system is to allow you to mentally encompass a much greater number of your <term>kindred</term> than is possible under the system we use. This advantage is gained, it is true, at the cost of the ability to distinguish between members of the various classes; however, it can still be very important in certain types of society, since each class usually shares a form of <term>conjoint responsibility</term>.

<page-num>216</page-num>
### ETYMOLOGY OF GEILFINE. LECT. VII.

...the Family. I believe, however, that I can provide at least some plausible reasons for believing that this perplexing four-fold division of the Celtic Family is neither a mere survival of ancient barbarism nor a purely arbitrary arrangement, as most people who have noticed it have supposed. Instead, it is a monument to that Power of the Father which is the first and greatest landmark in the course of legal history.

Let me repeat that the Irish Family is assumed to consist of three groups of four people and one group of five people. I have already stated that I consider the fifth person in the group of five to be the parent from whom all the other members of the four divisions originate, or to whom they are connected by adoptive descent. Thus, all the natural or adoptive descendants are distributed into four groups of four people each, with their rank in the Family being in the inverse order of their seniority. The <term>Geilfine</term> group is stated several times by the <term>Brehon</term> lawyers to be both the highest-ranking and the youngest.

Now, Mr. Whitley Stokes has shared his opinion with me that "<term>Geilfine</term>" means "hand-family." Since I have reason to believe that a different interpretation of the term has been adopted by prominent authorities, I will provide the reasons for Mr. Stokes’s view. "Gil" means "hand"—this was also the interpretation of O’Curry—and it is, in fact, the Greek word χείρ. In several <term>Aryan</term> languages, the term for "hand" is an expressive

# LECT. VII. IRISH AND ROMAN FAMILY.

equivalent for power, and especially for family or patriarchal power. Thus, in Greek we have ὑποχείριος and χέρης, for the person under the hand. In Latin we have *herus* ‘master,’ from an old word, cognate to χείρ; and we have also one of the cardinal terms of ancient Roman Family Law, <term>manus</term>, or hand, in the sense of patriarchal authority. In Roman legal phraseology, the wife who has become in law her husband’s daughter by marriage is *in manu*. The son discharged from paternal power is emancipated. The free person who has undergone <term>mancipation</term> is *in mancipio*. In the Celtic languages we have, with other words, ‘<term>Gilla</term>,’ a servant, a word familiar to sportsmen and travelers in the Highlands and to readers of Scott in its Anglicized shape, ‘Gillie.’

My suggestion, then, is that the key to the Irish distribution of the family, as to so many other things in ancient law, must be sought in the <term>Patria Potestas</term>. It seems to me to be founded on the order of emancipation from paternal authority. The <term>Geilfine</term>, the Hand-family, consists of the parent and the four natural or adoptive sons immediately under his power. The other groups consist of emancipated descendants, diminishing in dignity in proportion to their distance from the group which, according to archaic notions, constitutes the true or representative family.

<page-num>218</page-num>
# ANCIENT ROMAN AND IRISH FAMILY LECT. VII.

Roman law points to a range of ideas very similar to those which appear to have produced the Irish institution. The family under <term>Patria Potestas</term> was, along with the <term>Pater-Familias</term>, the true Roman family. The children who were emancipated from paternal power may have gained a practical advantage, but they undoubtedly lost theoretical dignity. They underwent that loss of status which, in ancient legal phraseology, was called a *capitis deminutio*. We also know that, according to primitive Roman law, they lost all rights of inheritance, and these were only gradually restored to them by a relatively modern institution, the Equity of the Roman <term>Prætor</term>. Nevertheless, there are hints on all sides that, as a general rule, sons were enfranchised from paternal power as they advanced in years; no doubt this practice provides a partial explanation for the durability of <term>Patria Potestas</term> as a Roman institution. The statements, therefore, which we find concerning the Celtic family would not be very inaccurate if applied to the Roman. The youngest children were first in dignity.

Of course, I am not arguing for an exact resemblance between the ancient Roman and ancient Celtic family. We have no trace of any systematized discharge of the sons from the Roman <term>Patria Potestas</term>; their enfranchisement seems always to have been dependent on the will of the <term>Pater-Familias</term>. The divisions of the Celtic family seem, on the other

# LECT. VII. BOROUGH ENGLISH.

number of ancient societies five is the representative number, no answer can be given except that there are five fingers on the human hand. I recommend to your attention on this point Mr. Tylor’s most instructive chapter on the infancy of the art of counting, in the first volume of his ‘*Primitive Culture*.’ ‘*Finger-counting*,’ he observes, ‘*is not only found among savages and uneducated men, carrying on a part of their mental operations where language is only partly able to follow it, but it also retains a place and an undoubted use among the most cultured nations as a preparation and means of acquiring higher arithmetical methods*’ (I. 246.) Five is thus a primitive natural maximum number. You will recall that the early English township was represented by the reeve and the four men. The council of an Indian village community most commonly consists of five persons, and throughout the East the normal number of a jury or board of arbitrators is always five—the *punchayet* familiar to all who have the smallest knowledge of India. The <term>Geilfine</term>, the representative group of the Irish family, consisting of the parent and the four descendants still retained under his <term>Patria Potestas</term>, falls in with this widely extended conception of representation.

The <term>Patria Potestas</term> seems to me the most probable source of a well-known English custom which has caused no little surprise to students

<page-num>222</page-num>
<header>EXPLANATIONS OF BOROUGH ENGLISH. LECT. VII.</header>

of our law. 'Borough English,' under which the youngest son and not the eldest succeeds to the <term>burgage-tenements</term> of his father, has been recognized from time immemorial as a widely diffused custom which it is the duty of our Courts to take judicial notice of, and many writers on our real property laws, from Littleton onward, have attempted to account for it. Littleton thought he saw its origin in the young age of the youngest son, who was not as well able to help himself as the rest of the brothers. Other authors, as Blackstone tells us, explained it by a supposed right of the <term>Seigneur</term> or lord—now very generally regarded as apocryphal—which raised a presumption of the eldest son's illegitimacy. Blackstone himself goes as far afield as North-Eastern Asia for an explanation. He quotes from Duhalde the statement that the custom of inheritance by the youngest son prevails among the Tartars.

> 'That nation,' he says, 'is composed entirely of shepherds and herdsmen; and the older sons, as soon as they are capable of leading a pastoral life, move away from their father with a certain allotment of cattle, and go to seek a new home. The youngest son, therefore, who stays longest with the father, is naturally the heir of his house, as the rest have already been provided for.'

And thus we find that, among many other Northern nations, it was the custom for all the sons but one to move away from the

<header>LECT. VII. PRIMOGENITURE AND BOROUGH ENGLISH.</header>

It is a remarkable circumstance that an institution closely resembling Borough English is found in the Laws of Wales, which provide the rule of descent for all cultivating villeins <note>Feudal tenants who were legally tied to the land</note>. 'Cum fratres inter se dividant hæreditatem,' says a rule of that portion of the Welsh Law which has survived in Latin; 'the youngest ought to have the *tygdyn*, that is, the buildings of his father, and eight acres of land, if they had them' (L. Wall., vol. ii. p. 780). And, when the youngest son has received the paternal dwelling-house, eight acres of land, and certain tools and utensils, the other sons are to divide what remains. It appears to me that the institution is founded on the same ideas as those which gave a preference to the <term>Geilfine</term> division of the Celtic family. The home-staying, unemancipated son, still retained under <term>*Patria Potestas*</term> <note>The legal power held by the head of a family</note>, is preferred over the others. If this is so, there is no reason for the surprise which the custom of Borough English has excited, and which arises from contrasting it with the rule of primogeniture. But the two institutions have a different origin. Primogeniture is not a natural outgrowth of the family. It is a political rather than a tribal institution, and comes to us not from the clansmen but from the Chief. But the rule of Borough English, like the privileges of the <term>Geilfine</term>,

<page-num>225</page-num>
<header>LECT. VIII. COMPARATIVE MYTHOLOGY.</header>

# LECTURE VIII.

## THE GROWTH AND DIFFUSION OF PRIMITIVE IDEAS.

Mr. Tylor has justly observed that the true lesson of the new science of Comparative Mythology is the barrenness in primitive times of the faculty which we most associate with mental fertility: the imagination. Comparative Jurisprudence, as might be expected from the natural stability of law and custom, suggests the same inference even more strongly; it points to the scarcity of ideas and the slowness of additions to the mental stock as being among the most general characteristics of mankind in its infancy.

The fact that the generation of new ideas does not proceed in all states of society as rapidly as in the one to which we belong is only unfamiliar to us because of our deep-seated habit of confining our observation of human nature to a small portion of its phenomena. When we undertake to examine it, we are very apt to look exclusively at a part of Western Europe and perhaps the American continent. We constantly leave aside India, China, and the whole Muhammadan

<summary>The author argues that both Comparative Mythology and Comparative Jurisprudence reveal a lack of imagination and a slow development of new ideas in early human societies. He suggests that the rapid intellectual change seen in the West is an exception, and that observers often overlook the more static nature of Eastern societies.</summary>
<keywords>Comparative Mythology, Mr. Tylor, Comparative Jurisprudence, primitive ideas, imagination, human nature, Western Europe, India, China, Muhammadan</keywords>

--- Page 233 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the tendency of Western observers to ignore the "non-progressive" societies of the East, which the author argues are essential for understanding the early stages of human intellectual development.</meta>

<page-num>226</page-num>
<header>IDEAS OF THE EAST. LECT. VIII.</header>

East. This limitation of our field of vision is perfectly justifiable when we are occupied with the investigation of the laws of Progress. Progress is, in fact, the same thing as the continued production of new ideas, and we can only discover the law of this production by examining sequences of ideas where they are frequent and of considerable length. But the primitive condition of progressive societies is best ascertained from the observable condition of those which are non-progressive; and thus we leave a serious gap in our knowledge when we set aside the mental state of the millions upon millions of men who fill what we vaguely call the East as a phenomenon of little interest and no instructional value. The fact is not unknown to most of us that, among these multitudes, Literature, Religion, and Art—or what corresponds to them—always move within a distinctly drawn circle of unchanging notions; but the fact that this condition of thought is a prolonged infancy of the human mind, rather than a different kind of maturity from the one most familiar to us, is very seldom brought home to us with enough clarity to make it instructive.

Indeed, I do not deny that the difference between the East and the West, regarding the different speeds at which new ideas are produced, is only a matter of degree. New ideas were produced in India even during the disastrous period just before

There must have been a series of ages during which the progress of China was maintained very steadily, and undoubtedly our assumption of the absolute immobility of Chinese and other societies is partly an expression of our own ignorance. Conversely, I question whether new ideas come into being in the West as rapidly as modern literature and conversation sometimes suggest. It cannot, indeed, be doubted that causes unknown to the ancient world lead among us to the multiplication of ideas. Among them are the never-ceasing discovery of new facts of nature, inventions changing the circumstances and material conditions of life, and new rules of social conduct; I take the chief of this last class, and certainly the most powerful in the domain of law proper, to be the famous maxim that all institutions should be adapted to produce the greatest happiness of the greatest number. Nevertheless, there are many signs that even conscious efforts to increase the number of ideas have very limited success. Look at Poetry and Fiction. <header>LIMITS TO FERTILITY OF HUMAN MIND. LECT. VIII.</header>

We speak of the caprices of Fashion; yet, on examining them historically, we find them singularly limited—so much so, that we are sometimes tempted to regard Fashion as passing through cycles of form that are constantly repeating themselves. There are, in fact, more natural limitations on the fertility of the intellect than we always admit to ourselves, and these, reflected in bodies of people, translate into that weariness of novelty which seems at intervals to overtake entire Western societies, including minds of every degree of information and cultivation.

My current object is to point out some of the results of mental sterility at a time when society is in the stage which we have been considering. Then, the relations between person and person were summed up in kinship. The fundamental assumption was that all people, if not united with you by blood, were your enemies or your slaves. Gradually, the assumption became untrue in practice, and people who were not blood relatives became related to one another on terms of peace and mutual tolerance or mutual advantage. Yet no new ideas came into being that exactly harmonized with the new relationship, nor was any new terminology invented to express it. The new member of each group was spoken of as kin to it, and was treated as

<header>LECT. VIII. TRANSFORMATION OF IDEAS.</header>

So little were ideas changed that, as we shall see, the very affections and emotions which the natural bond evoked were called forth with extraordinary strength by the artificial tie. The clear understanding of these facts sheds light on several historical problems, and among them on some aspects of Irish history. Yet they should not greatly surprise us, since, in a modified form, they are part of our everyday experience. Almost everyone can observe that, when new circumstances arise, we use our old ideas to understand them; it is only afterwards, and sometimes long afterwards, that our ideas are found to have changed. An English <term>Court of Justice</term> is in large part a mechanism for working out this process. New combinations of circumstance are constantly arising, but in the first instance they are exclusively interpreted according to old legal ideas. A little later, lawyers admit that the old ideas are not quite what they were before the new circumstances arose.

The slow development of ideas in ancient times may first be cited as necessary to the explanation of that great family of <term>Fictions</term> which meet us at the beginning of history and <term>historical jurisprudence</term>. The key to these unusual phenomena has recently been sought in ancient religions, and it has supposedly been found in the alleged universal practice of worshipping dead ancestors. However, very striking illustrations of them are provided by the law and usage of Ireland after it had been Christianized for centuries, and long after any eponymous progenitor could be imagined as being worshipped. The Family, House, and Tribe of the Romans—and, as far as my knowledge extends, all the similar divisions of Greek communities—were distinguished by separate special names. But in the <term>Brehon Law</term>, the same word, <term>Fine</term> (or 'family'), is used for the Family as we ordinarily understand it—that is, for the children of a living parent and their descendants; for the <term>Sept</term> or, in the phrasing of Indian law, the Joint Undivided Family—that is, the combined descendants of an ancestor long since dead; for the Tribe, which was the political unit of ancient Ireland; and even for the large Tribes in which the smaller units were sometimes absorbed. Nevertheless, the Irish Family undoubtedly received additions through adoption. Nor does this artificiality consist solely in extending the sphere of kinship to classes known to have been originally alien to the true brotherhood. An even more interesting example of it presents itself when the ideas of kinship and the terminology proper to <term>consanguinity</term> (blood relation) are extended to associations which we would now view as being exclusively founded on contract, such as partnerships and guilds. There are no more interesting pages in Dr. Sullivan’s Introduction (pp. ccvi and following) than those in which he discusses the tribal origin of Guilds. He claims a Celtic etymology for the word itself, and he traces the institution to the grazing partnerships common among the ancient Irish. Regardless of this, it is most instructive to find the same words used to describe bodies of co-partners, formed by contract, and bodies of <term>co-heirs</term> or <term>co-parceners</term> formed by common descent. <header>LECT. VIII. LONDON COMPANIES.</header>

The trading guilds that survive in our own country have undergone every sort of transformation that can disguise their parentage. They are artificial to begin with, though the hereditary principle has a certain tendency to assert itself. They have long since abandoned the occupations that gave them their names. They mostly trace their privileges and constitution to some royal charter; and kingly grants, whether real or fictitious, are the great cause of discontinuity in English history. Yet anyone who, with a knowledge of primitive law and history, examines the internal mechanism and proceedings of a London Company will see in many parts of them plain traces of the ancient brotherhood of kinsmen, "joint in food, worship, and estate"; and I suppose that the nearest approach to an ancient tribal holding in Ireland is to be found in those confiscated lands which are now the property of several of these Companies.

<page-num>284</page-num>
<header>ORIGIN OF PARTNERSHIP. LECT. VIII.</header>

One or two other of the great Roman contracts appear to me, when closely examined, to provide evidence of their having been gradually evolved through changes in the mechanism of primitive society. You have seen how brotherhoods of kinsmen transform themselves into alliances between persons whom we can only call partners, but still at first sight the link is missing which would enable us to say that here we have the beginning of the contract of partnership. Look, however, at the peculiar contract called by the Romans '<term>societas omnium (or universorum) bonorum</term>.' It is commonly translated 'partnership with unlimited liability,' and there is no doubt that the older form of partnership has had a great effect on the newer form. But you will find that, in the <term>societas omnium bonorum</term>, not only were all the liabilities of the partnership the liabilities of the several partners, but the whole of the property of each partner was brought into the common stock and was enjoyed as a common fund. No such arrangement as this is known in the modern world as the result of ordinary agreement, though in some countries it may be the effect of marriage. It appears to me that we are carried back to the joint brotherhoods of primitive society, and that their development must have given rise to the contract before us. Let us turn again to the contract of <term>Mandatum</term> or Agency. The only complete representation of one man by another which

The representation of the Principal by the Agent is much more incomplete, and it seems likely to me that we have in it a shadow of that thorough merging of two individuals which was only possible in ancient times when they belonged to the same family.

The institutions which I have taken as my examples are institutions of native growth, developed probably to some degree within all ancient societies by the expansion of the notion of kinship. But it sometimes happens that a wholly foreign institution is introduced from the outside into a society based upon assumed blood relationship, and then it is most instructive to observe how closely, in such a case, material which we would previously think likely to offer the most stubborn resistance to the influence of tribal ideas nonetheless adapts itself to the model of a Family or Tribe. You may be aware that the ancient Irish Church has long been a puzzle to church historians. There are difficulties suggested by it on which I do not pretend to shed any new light, nor, indeed, could they be easily considered here. Among puzzles of this class are the extraordinary increase in the number of bishops and their dependence—apparently an almost slave-like dependence—on the religious houses to which they were attached.

<page-num>236</page-num>
<header>THE ANCIENT IRISH CHURCH. LECT. VIII.</header>

But the relationship between the various ecclesiastical bodies was undoubtedly tribal in nature. The Brehon law seems to me to fully confirm the account of the matter given—based on purely ecclesiastical literature—by Dr. Todd in the introduction to his *Life of St. Patrick*. One of the great Irish or Scotic missionaries, who almost invariably reappears later as a saint, obtains a grant of land from some chieftain or tribe in Ireland or Celtic Britain and founds a monastery there; or, it may be that the founder of the religious house is already a chieftain of a tribe himself. The House becomes the parent of others, which in turn may establish minor religious institutions that are both monastic and missionary. The words meaning "family" or "tribe" and "kinship" are applied to all the religious bodies created by this process. Each monastic house, with its monks and bishops, constitutes a "family" or "tribe"; and its secular or servile dependents seem to be sometimes included under that name. The same name is given to the collective group of religious houses formed by the parent monastery and the various churches or monastic bodies that branched off from it. Together, these make up the "tribe of the saint," though this last expression is not used exclusively with this particular meaning. The abbot of the parent house and all the abbots of the minor houses are the "*comharbas*" or

### LECT. VIII. GROUPS OF RELIGIOUS HOUSES.

...co-heirs of the saint, and in yet another sense the "family" or "tribe" of the saint means his actual tribesmen or blood relatives. Iona, or Hy, was, as you know, the famous religious house founded by St. Columba near the coast of the newer Scotia. "The Abbot of Hy," says Dr. Todd, "or <term>Co-arb</term> of Columba, was the common head of Durrow, Kells, Swords, Drumcliff, and other houses in Ireland founded by Columba, as well as of the parent monastery of Hy, and the 'family of Colum-kille' was composed of the congregations or inmates and dependents of all those monasteries. The families, therefore, of such monasteries as Clonmacnois or Durrow might muster a very respectable body of fighting men." Let me add that there is very good evidence that these "families of the saints" were occasionally engaged in bloody little wars. But, "in general" (I now quote again from Dr. Todd), "the 'family' meant only the monks or religious members of the house."

It will be obvious to you that this application of the same name to all these complicated sets of relations is occasionally extremely perplexing, but the key to the difficulty is the concept of the kindred branching off in successive generations from the common stock, planting themselves occasionally at a distance, but never altogether breaking the bond which connected them with their original family and chief. The ecclesiastical rule, we know, required election by the monks, but the Corus Bescna declares that, upon a vacancy, the "family of the saint" (which here means the founder’s <term>sept</term>), if there is a qualified monk among them, ought to be preferred in elections to the Abbacy—"though there is only a psalm-singer among them, if he is fit, he shall have it." And it proceeds to say that, if no relative or tribesman of the saint is qualified, the Abbacy shall go to some member of the tribe which originally granted the land.

A very modern example of this plasticity of the notion of kinship has recently been brought to my notice. The co-villagers of an Indian village call themselves brothers, although, as I have frequently observed, the composition of the community is often artificial and its origin very diverse. There is, however, another set of phenomena belonging to the same category, but which seem to me to have been greatly misunderstood. When men, influenced by the mindset we are discussing, are placed in circumstances that naturally foster affection and sympathy, or when they are placed in a relationship which they are taught to consider especially sacred, not only their words and ideas but also their feelings, emotions, and prejudices mold themselves on the pattern of those that naturally result from blood relationship (<term>consanguinity</term>). I believe we have a striking example of the process in the history of the Christian Church. I expect you know that **Spiritual Relationship**—the bond between a sponsor and a baptized person, or between sponsors, or even between the sponsors and the family of the baptized—gradually became the source of a great number of prohibitions against intermarriage. These stood on the same level as those based on marriage (<term>affinity</term>), and almost on the same level as those founded on blood relationship (<term>consanguinity</term>). Nowadays, I am told that they merely survive formally in the Roman Catholic Church, and that dispensations relaxing them are obtainable as a matter of course. With such peoples, a very sacred tie was necessarily of the nature of a family tie, and carried with it the same associations and the same order of feeling. It seems to me, accordingly, in the highest degree natural that **Spiritual Relationship**, when introduced into a tribal society like that of the ancient Irish, should closely align itself with blood-relationship. We know, in fact, that it did so, and that the strictness of the relationship and the warmth of the affections which it produced provoked the scorn, the anger, and the astonishment of several generations of English observers, who derived their ideas from a social order that had become very different from that of Ireland. But alongside <term>Gossipred</term>, or **Spiritual Relationship**, there stood another much more primitive institution, which was extraordinarily developed among the ancient Irish, though not at all unique to them. This was **Fosterage**, the giving and taking of children for nurture. Of the reasons why this practice—now known to have been widely diffused among <term>Aryan</term> communities—should have had an exceptional importance and popularity in Ireland, we can say little more than that they probably belong to the accidents of Irish history and of Irish social life. But there is no doubt of the fact. An entire sub-section in the <term>Senchus Mor</term> is devoted to the **Law of Fosterage**, and it sets out with the greatest precision the rights and duties belonging to all parties when the children of another family were received for nurture and education. It seemed monstrous to them that the same mother's milk should produce in Ireland the same close affections as shared paternity did in their own country. The true explanation is one that is only now becoming clear to us. It was that Fosterage was an institution which, though artificial in its beginnings, was natural in its operation; and that the relationship between foster-parent and foster-child tended, at that stage of social feeling, to become indistinguishable from the relationship between father and son.

The form of Fosterage that is of most interest to the modern researcher is called *Literary Fosterage* by the translators of the <term>Brehon tracts</term>. It was an institution closely connected to the existence of the Brehon Law Schools, and it consists of the various relationships established between the Brehon teacher and the pupils he received into his house for instruction in Brehon lore. However much it may surprise us that the connection between Schoolmaster and Pupil was regarded as uniquely sacred by the ancient Irish, and as closely resembling natural fatherhood, the Brehon tracts leave no room for doubt on the point. While the law bound an ordinary foster-father to provide some form of education to his foster-children—instructing the sons of chiefs in riding, archery, swimming, and chess, and the daughters in sewing, cutting patterns, and embroidery—the <term>Brehon</term> trained his foster-sons in learning of the highest dignity: the lore of the primary literary profession. He received payment, but it was the law that determined the amount for him. This was a consequence of his social status, rather than the result of a private bargain.

There are some faint traces of Fosterage in <term>Hindu law</term>, but it has essentially disappeared from the system. However, the remnants of Literary Fosterage are quite abundant and very clear. According to the general custom of India, the <term>Brahmin</term> teacher of <term>Brahmin</term> pupils receives no payment for his services, but Hindu law repeatedly reserves for him a remote right of succession to their property. <header>LECTURE VIII. TRADES CALLED CASTES.</header>

In societies of an archaic type, a particular craft or kind of knowledge becomes in time a hereditary profession of families, almost as a matter of course. The difficulty for a native of <term>India</term>, uninfluenced by English ideas, is not to find a reason why a son should succeed to the learning of his father, and consequently to his office and duties; his difficulty would rather be to explain to himself why it should not be so, and how the public interest could be served by any other arrangement. The States governed by native Indian Princes are becoming significantly Anglicized, but it is still the practically universal rule within them that office is hereditary. However, we do not arrive at a complete account of the growth of those <term>castes</term>—which are definite sections of great populations—in this way. Only one of these <term>castes</term> really survives in <term>India</term>, that of the <term>Brahmins</term>, and it is strongly suspected that the whole literary theory of <term>Caste</term>, which is of <term>Brahmin</term> origin, is based on the existence of the <term>Brahmin</term> caste alone. A great profession would thus be formed, with stores of common knowledge; but the tie between the members would not be purely intellectual; it would, from the start, be conceived as being in the nature of <term>kinship</term>. We have thus strong reason for thinking that societies still under the influence of primitive thought labor under a certain incapacity for regarding men, grouped together by virtue of any institutions whatsoever, as connected otherwise than through blood-relationship. We find that, through this barrenness of conception, they are apt to extend the notion of consanguinity and the language originating in it to institutions of their own not really founded on community of blood, and even to institutions of foreign origin. We find also that the association between institutions arising from true kinship and institutions based on artificial kinship is sometimes so strong, that the emotions which they respectively call forth are practically indistinguishable. These phenomena of early thought and feeling appear to me to account amply for some facts of Irish history which nearly all English writers on Ireland have noticed with extreme surprise or indignation. <header>THE MANUSCRIPT OF GAIUS. LECT. IX.</header>

# LECTURE IX.

## THE PRIMITIVE FORMS OF LEGAL REMEDIES.

### I.

I stated on a previous occasion (Lecture I. p. 8) that the branch of law which we now call the Law of Distress occupies the largest part of the greatest Brehon law-tract, the *Senchus Mor*. The importance given to Distress in this way is very significant, and in this and the following Lecture I propose to discuss the questions it raises and the conclusions it suggests.

The value of the important discovery made by Niebuhr, when he recovered the manuscript of Gaius in 1816, does not only arise from the new light it immediately threw on the beginnings of the legal system that is the source of most civilized jurisprudence. There are portions of the treatise restored to the world at that time which give us glimpses of something older than law itself, and which allow us to connect law with the practices dictated to primitive men by impulses which it has become the primary duty of all law to

<header>LECT. IX. LEX AND LEGIS ACTIO</header>

At the head of the passages in the work of Gaius which allow the mind's eye to penetrate some little way into the chaos out of which social order sprang, I place the fragmentary and imperfect account, given near the beginning of the Fourth Book, of the old <term>Legis Actiones</term>, which in the age of Gaius himself had ceased to have more than an historical and antiquarian interest.

<term>Legis Actio</term>, of which the exact meaning does not seem to have been known to Gaius, may be conjectured to have been the substantive form of the verbal expression, *legem* or *lege agere*, and to have been equivalent to what we now call Procedure. It has been several times observed that among the <term>Legis Actiones</term> are included several proceedings which are not of the nature of Actions or Suits, but are rather modes of executing decrees. The fact seems to be that, by a course of change which may be traced in the history of Roman law, one portion, ‘Actio,’ of the venerable phrase ‘<term>Legis Actio</term>’ has been gradually separated from the rest, and has come to denote that stage of the administration of justice which is directly conducted by the Court, together, in some judicial systems, with the stage immediately preceding it. <header>->THE <term>LEGIS ACTIO SACRAMENTI</term>. LECT. IX.<-</header>

claring rights and duties and the rules according to which the law declaring rights and duties is administered. On the expression just mentioned, Adjective Law, with which Bentham and his school have familiarized us, I will make a remark which applies to much in the phraseology and classifications of the Analytical Jurists: that it is correct and convenient according to the ideas of their day, but that, if used regarding very old law, it is likely to lead to a historical misconception. It would not be untrue to assert that, in one stage of human affairs, rights and duties are more the accessory of procedure than procedure is a mere appendage to rights and duties. There have been times when the real difficulty lay not in conceiving what a man was entitled to, but in obtaining it; so that the method, violent or legal, by which an end was obtained was of more consequence than the nature of the end itself. In fact, it is only in the most recent times or in the most highly developed legal systems that remedies have lost importance in comparison with rights and have ceased to affect them deeply and in various ways.

The first and, in many respects, the most interesting of these ancient modes of proceeding is the <term>Legis Actio Sacramenti</term>, the undoubted parent of all the Roman Actions, and consequently of most of the civil remedies now in use in the world. Several years ago, I pointed out (*Ancient Law*, pp. 376, 377)

<header>LECTURE IX. ANCIENT DRAMATIZATIONS.</header>

that the technical formalities clearly appeared, upon inspection, to be a dramatization of the Origin of Justice. “Two armed men,” I said, “are wrangling over some disputed property. The Prætor, *vir pietate gravis* <note>a man respected for his piety</note>, happens to be passing by and intervenes to stop the contest. The disputants state their case to him and agree that he shall arbitrate between them, with the arrangement that the loser—besides resigning the object of the quarrel—shall pay a sum of money to the umpire as payment for his trouble and loss of time.” “This interpretation,” I then added, “would be less plausible than it is, were it not for a surprising coincidence: the ceremony described by Gaius as the mandatory course of proceeding in a <term>Legis Actio</term> is substantially the same as one of the two subjects that the god Hephaestus is described by Homer as molding into the first compartment of the Shield of Achilles.” Since these passages were written, the labors of more recent researchers enable us to categorize this judicial depiction of the origin of one great institution, Civil Justice, with other pictorial or dramatic representations of forgotten practices which survive in various parts of the world in the ceremonies surrounding institutions of at least equal importance. <header>SACRAMENTAL ACTION. LECT. IX.</header>

of the reign of force which, at least between tribes, everywhere preceded the reign of law. At the same time, it should not be assumed that these long-standing dramas imply, or ever implied, any disrespect for the institutions with which they are associated. In all probability, they intentionally commemorate not the evil, but the remedy for the evil. Until they degenerate into meaningless customs, they are performed not in honor of brute force, but in honor of the institutions that replaced it: Marriage and Civil Justice.

Almost every gesture and almost every set of formal words in the <term>Legis Actio Sacramenti</term> symbolizes something that, in one part of the world or another, or in one Aryan society or another, has developed into an important institution. The claimant places his hand on the slave or other subject of dispute. This grasping of the object claimed is reproduced in the corresponding procedures of the ancient Germans and was continued by them, in various modified forms, well into the Middle Ages; it is an early example of that "demand before action" upon which all civilized legal systems insist. The wand, which the claimant held in his hand, is stated by <term>Gaius</term> to have represented a spear. The spear, the emblem of the armed strongman, served as the symbol of property held absolutely and against the world, not
