<header>LECT. IX. SACRAMENTAL ACTION</header>

only in the Roman but in several other Western societies. The proceedings included a series of assertions and reassertions of rights by the parties, and this formal dialogue was the parent of the Art of Pleading. The quarrel between the plaintiff and the defendant, which was a mere pretense among the Romans, long remained a reality in other societies; and, though its theory was altered, it survived in the <term>Wager of Battle</term> which, as an English institution, was only finally abolished in our fathers' day. The intervention of the <term>Praetor</term> and the acceptance of his mediation expanded into the Administration of Justice in the Roman State—one of the most powerful instruments in the historical transformation of the civilized world. The disputants staked a sum of money—the *<term>Sacramentum</term>*, from which the proceedings took their name—on the merits of their quarrel, and the stake went into the public treasury. The money wagered in this way, which appears in a remarkably large number of archaic legal systems, is the earliest representative of those court fees which have been a more significant power in legal history than legal historians are generally inclined to admit. The very spirit in which a *<term>Legis Actio</term>* was conducted was that which, in the eyes of laymen, has been most characteristic of lawyers throughout history. If, says <term>Gaius</term>, you sued by *<term>Legis Actio</term>* for damage to your vines, and called them *vines*, you would fail; you

That is, they laid a formal wager (distinct from the stake called <term>Sacramentum</term>) on the justice of their respective contentions. The sum thus staked, which was always equal to a third of the amount in dispute, went in the end to the successful litigant, and not, like the <term>Sacramentum</term>, to the State. Lawyers wondered, <term>Gaius</term> tells us, why such an action should be needed when property could have been recovered by the older and unmodified procedure. Many technical answers to this question have been given by modern commentators on <term>Roman law</term>, but we will see whether a better explanation of it cannot be obtained by approaching it from another side.

<term>Gaius</term>, leaving the <term>Condictio</term>, proceeds to discuss two of the <term>Legis Actiones</term>, the <term>Manus Injectio</term> and the <term>Pignoris Capio</term>, which cannot be made to square in any way with our modern conception of an action. The <term>Manus Injectio</term> is expressly stated to have been originally the Roman mode of execution against the person of a judgment debtor. <header>THE PIGNORIS CAPIO. LECT. IX.</header>

Commonwealth. The <term>Pignoris Capio</term> was also, possibly under a slightly different name, a method of execution in later times against property after a court decree; however, this was not its original purpose as a <term>Legis Actio</term>. It was initially a completely extra-judicial proceeding. In certain cases, the person who used this procedure seized the goods of a fellow citizen against whom he had a claim, even though he had not started a formal lawsuit. This power of seizure could be exercised by soldiers against public officials who were required to provide them with pay, horses, or fodder; it could also be used by someone who sold an animal for a sacrifice against a buyer who failed to pay. It was therefore limited to claims of great urgency or those involving highly sacred obligations, but it was later expanded to include demands for overdue unpaid taxes. I am grateful to Mr. Poste for the observation that the ideal institutions in Plato’s *Laws* include something very similar to the Roman <term>Pignoris Capio</term>; there, too, it serves as a remedy for the violation of public duties related to military service or religious practices.

I will use the <term>Pignoris Capio</term> as the direct starting point for everything I am about to say on the subject of Ancient Civil Procedure. First of all, let us ask whether <term>Gaius</term> himself gives us any hint regarding its meaning and significance in the primitive Roman system. <header>LECT. IX. ANTIQUITY OF WAGERING.</header>

Let us return for a moment to the parent <term>Legis Actio</term>—the <term>L. A. Sacramenti</term>. Its ancient forms assume a quarrel and illustrate the method of settling it. The <term>Prætor</term> mimics the intervention of a passing arbitrator. But suppose there is no arbitrator nearby. What method remains for avoiding bloodshed, and is any such method reflected in that ancient procedure which, by its very existence, implies that bloodshed has somehow been prevented?

I suspect that, at first, I will seem to be making a trivial remark when I say that one way to achieve this goal is to place a wager. Even now, this is one of the most common ways to delay a dispute regarding a matter of fact, and the truth is that the tendency to bet on outcomes lies very deep in human nature and has developed alongside it from its earliest beginnings. Not everyone, when their anger is provoked, will agree to have a quarrel referred to a third person who is present, let alone one who is absent; however, they will often do so if they place a wager on it, and if, in addition to being proven right, they have a chance of receiving the amount staked. The <term>Legis Actio Sacramenti</term> assumes that the quarrel is immediately referred to an arbitrator who is present; the <term>Condictio</term> assumes that the reference is to the decision of an arbitrator after a thirty-day interval, but in the meantime, the parties have entered into a separate wager regarding the merits of their dispute. We know that the liability for an independent penalty applied to the person suing by <term>Condictio</term> even when it had become one of the most important Roman legal actions, and that it was still enforced in the age of <term>Cicero</term>.

There is yet another primitive method by which, in the absence of an arbitrator on the spot, a quarrel may be prevented from ending in bloodshed. The claimant who is willing to go to arbitration may, in the absence of his opponent—or, if he is the stronger, in his presence—take forcible possession of the opponent's movable property and keep it until the opponent also submits. I believe this to have been the true primitive function of the <term>Pignoris Capio</term>, though the full evidence for my opinion will not be presented to you until I have tracked the same institution through the early history of other legal systems. <header>LECT. IX. DISTRAINT OF GOODS.</header>

This was a consequence of the exceptionally rapid development of Roman law and procedure, and of the exceptionally early date at which the Roman tribunals became the instruments of national sovereignty. You will see later how much reason there is to think that the progress of most societies toward a complete administration of justice was slow and gradual, and that the Commonwealth at first interfered through its various branches more to keep order and ensure fair play in quarrels than to take them into its own hands, as it now does always and everywhere. The peculiar rules that survived along with the <term>Pignoris Capio</term>, which provided for its exercise out of court and during the judicial vacation, pointed back to this period that the Romans had long forgotten.

I turn to Teutonic societies for traces of a practice similar to what the Romans called <term>Pignoris Capio</term>. These traces seem quite unmistakable in that portion of our own English law concerned with the power of <term>Distraint</term> or <term>Distress</term> and with the related legal remedy known as <term>Replevin</term>. The examples of the right to distrain another person's property that are most familiar to you are, I imagine, the landlord's right to seize a tenant's goods for unpaid rent, and the right of a lawful landowner to take and impound stray animals.

<header>DISTRESS FOR RENT. LECT. IX.</header>

which are damaging his crops or soil. The process by which the latter right is made effective retains far more of the ancient institution than does distress for rent. For the landlord's peculiar power to distrain for rent, while it remains an extrajudicial remedy, has been converted into a complete remedy of its kind by a series of relatively modern statutes. It has always, however, been the theory of the most learned English lawyers that distress is in principle an incomplete remedy; its primary object is to compel the person against whom it is properly employed to provide satisfaction. But goods distrained for rent are nowadays not merely held as security for the landlord's claim; they are ultimately put up for sale with certain prescribed formalities, the landlord is paid out of the proceeds, and the surplus is returned to the tenant. Thus the proceeding has become merely a special method by which payment of rent, and certain other payments which are placed on the same footing, are enforced without the help of a Court of Justice. But the distraint of cattle for damage still retains a variety of archaic features. It is not a complete remedy. The taker merely keeps the cattle until satisfaction is made to him for the injury, or until they are returned by him on an engagement to contest the right to distrain in an action of <term>Replevin</term>.

<header>LECT. IX. COURSE OF A DISTRESS.</header>

There is reason to believe that in ancient times it was used in many more cases than our oldest Common-law authorities recognize; but around the reign of Henry III, when it was limited to certain specific claims and wrongs, the procedure was as follows: The person who believed himself to be aggrieved seized the goods (which in ancient times were almost always cattle) of the person who he believed had injured him or failed in his duty toward him. He drove the animals to a pound, an enclosed piece of land reserved for the purpose, and generally open to the sky. Let me observe in passing that there is no more ancient institution in the country than the Village Pound. It is far older than the King’s Bench, and probably older than the Kingdom itself. While the cattle were on their way to the pound, the owner had a limited right of rescue which the law recognized, but which he ran a great risk in exercising. Once lodged within the enclosure, the impounded animals, when the pound was uncovered, had to be fed by the owner and not by the <term>distrainor</term>; nor was this rule altered until the present reign. The <term>distrainor</term>’s part in the proceedings ended, in fact, with the impounding; and we have to consider what courses were then open to the person whose cattle had been seized. Of course

<header>COURSE OF A DISTRESS. LECT. IX</header>

It might happen, however, that he completely denied the <term>distrainor’s</term> right to <term>distrain</term>, or that the latter, after being offered security for the settlement of his claim, refused to release the cattle. In either of these cases, the cattle owner (at least at the time we are discussing) could either apply to the King’s Chancery for a writ commanding the Sheriff to ‘make <term>replevin</term>,’ or he could complain verbally to the Sheriff, who would then proceed at once to ‘replevy.’ The process represented by this ancient phrase consisted of several stages. The Sheriff first of all demanded to see the impounded cattle; if this was refused, he treated the <term>distrainor</term> as having committed a violent breach of the King’s peace and raised the <term>hue and cry</term> after him. If the cattle (as was undoubtedly often the case) had been driven far away and out of his jurisdiction, the Sheriff searched for the <term>distrainor’s</term> own cattle and seized them to double the value of the animals that were missing—the ‘taking in <term>withernam</term>’ of old English law. In more peaceful times, however, and among law-abiding people, the Crown’s deputy was allowed to see the cattle, which he immediately returned to their original owner upon a pledge to follow the decision of a Court of Justice. <header>LECT. IX. ANTIQUITY OF PROCEDURE IN DISTRESS.</header>

A great deal of technical learning has clustered around it, but for our purposes it is enough to say that the plaintiff in the action was the owner of the <term>distrained</term> cattle and the defendant was the <term>distrainor</term>.

The comparative antiquity of the various steps in the procedure is not, I think, difficult to detect. Nothing can be more archaic than the picture presented by its more venerable details. The seizure of the cattle, the rescue, and the counter-seizure belong to the oldest practices of mankind. We were carried back, by the <term>Legis Actio Sacramenti</term> of the Romans, to a sudden fight over disputed property barely stopped by a casual passer-by. Here, not in a city community, but among the ancient legal forms of a half-pastoral, half-agricultural people, we come upon plain traces of a foray. 

But the foray which survives in the old <term>Law of Distress</term> is not, like the combat of the ancient Roman Action, a mere dramatic representation. Up to a certain point, it is a reality, and the most probable account of its origin is that it is a genuinely disorderly proceeding which the law steps in to regulate. <page-num>266</page-num>
-># PRIMITIVE OBJECTS OF DISTRESS. LECT. IX.<-

violence consisted neither in completely forbidding it nor in taking active jurisdiction over the dispute that provoked it, but in limiting it, prescribing rules for it, or directing it toward new purposes. Thus, the next series of events in the practice of <term>distraint</term>—the impounding, the emphasis placed on a pledge or security, and the acknowledgment of continued ownership implied by the requirement for the person whose goods were distrained to feed the cattle, and the rule that the distrainor must not work them—belong to a more recent set of ideas that governed the first attempts to moderate retaliation and regulate revenge for wrongs. Distress now becomes a semi-orderly mechanism for demanding satisfaction. Many remnants of this ancient function still exist. It has been noted by Blackstone and others that the partial exemption of certain types of goods from distraint—plow-oxen, for example, and tools of one's trade—was not originally intended in the least as an act of kindness toward the owner. It was necessitated by the very nature of the entire process, since without the tools for farming or trade, the debtor could never pay his debt. A passage in the *Dialogus de Scaccario* (ii. 14), which sets the order in which the goods of the King’s debtors are to be sold, strongly supports this view.

<header>LECT. IX. PECULIARITIES OF ACTION OF REPLEVIN.</header>

His administrative deputy, the Sheriff, upon a complaint made by their owner, follows the cattle, demands to see them, raises the hue and cry if it is refused, and seizes twice their number if the animals have been driven away. Even when he obtains a view of them, he can do nothing unless the cattle owner—denying the right of his adversary to <term>distrain</term>—is prepared with security that he will try the case between them in a court of justice. Thus, that power which according to our modern notions should have appeared on the scene long ago makes its appearance tardily: the judicial power of the Commonwealth. Its jurisdiction is obviously acquired through the act of the Sheriff in restoring the cattle once a pledge has been given. The distrainor has lost his material security, the cattle. The owner of the cattle has become personally bound. And thus, both are placed under a compulsion which eventually drives them to a <term>judicial arbitration</term>.

Nearly six hundred years ago, the contrast between the ancient proceedings in <term>Replevin</term> and suits conducted on what were then modern principles was already striking. The second chapter of the <term>Statute of Westminster the Second</term> is aimed at certain methods by which tenants managed to defeat the lord’s remedy by <term>distress</term>; and, in giving the King’s Justices jurisdiction in such cases, it goes on to say that such a provision does not conflict with the principle of

<header>THE ACTION OF REPLEVIN LECT. IX.</header>

the <term>Common Law</term> which forbids the removal of suits to the Justices on the petition of a defendant. ‘For,’ it adds, ‘although at first sight the tenant may seem to be the plaintiff and the lord the defendant, yet in reality, having regard for the fact that the lord <term>distrains</term> and sues for overdue services and dues, he is rather the plaintiff or complainant than the defendant.’ The <term>Action of Replevin</term> is in fact an excellent illustration of the difference between ancient and modern juridical principles. According to ideas now established in us, the person who sets a Court of Justice in motion is the person who complains of a wrong. In the case supposed, this is not the man distrained upon but the man who <term>distrains</term>. It is he who has suffered an injury for which he made reprisals on his adversary’s property. Yet it is his adversary who has to start the legal procedure and to establish himself as the plaintiff in the <term>Action of Replevin</term>. The reason why a modern Court of Justice would insist on taking the whole dispute into its own hands, and dealing with it in its own way from the very beginning, is that, having always the full command of the public force, it is sure of being able to compel the submission of the defendant to its jurisdiction and of coercing him in the end until he does justice, however long the coercion may be delayed. But at the era to which the procedure in <term>distress</term> originally belonged, the Court had no such assurance of power; and hence the person assumed to

<header>LECT. IX. THE LEGES BARBARORUM.</header>

The information furnished to us respecting this primitive procedure by the various bodies of Continental Teutonic law known collectively as the <term>Leges Barbarorum</term> is of a very interesting kind. Almost all of them contain references to <term>Pignoratio</term> or <term>distraint</term> of goods. The <term>Visigothic law</term> expressly prohibits it; and, at the other end of the scale, the <term>Lombardic law</term> has a trace of that license of <term>distress</term> which has survived in the <term>English Common-law</term> and permits it after a simple demand for payment. But the <term>Salic law</term>, which the most learned Germans now believe to have been drawn up at some period between the time at which Tacitus wrote and the time at which the Franks broke into the Empire, contains a series of very peculiar and instructive provisions on the subject, which have been for the first time fully interpreted by Sohm. Under this system, <term>Distress</term> is not yet a judicial remedy; it is still an extrajudicial mode of redress, but it has been incorporated with a regular and highly complex procedure. A succession of notices has to be given in solemn form by the complainant to the person against whom he complains, and whose property he proposes to seize. Nor can he

It seems quite clear that, before the Conquest, attempts were made in England to limit the freedom of distraint by the same class of restrictions found in the <term>Salic Law</term> and the related Teutonic customs. These provisions have their close counterpart in the ordinance of <term>Canute</term> that no man is to take *nams* unless he has demanded justice three times in the <term>Hundred</term>; if he obtains no justice the third time, he is to go to the <term>Shire-gemot</term>; the shire is to appoint him a fourth time, and, if that fails, he may take the distress.

It should be noted that the process of the <term>Salic Law</term> which corresponds to our distress is especially a remedy in certain cases of breach of contract. Distraint, the seizing of *nams*, was certainly used to enforce a similar class of demands under old English law before the Conquest; and the practice seems to have been known in <term>Bracton's</term> day, though the brevity of his notice does not permit us to understand its course and character fully. It should be understood that the <term>Frankish procedure</term> was completely at the disposal of the complainant. It is not a strictly judicial procedure, but rather a procedure regulating <term>extrajudicial redress</term>. If the complainant follows the proper forms, the role of the Court in authorizing seizure is purely passive. Even after the exhaustive examination which this part of the <term>Salic Law</term> has undergone from <term>Professor Sohm</term>, it is very difficult to say whether at any point of the procedure the defendant had the opportunity to present a substantial defense; but it seems certain that, whenever he could do this, he

<header>ANCIENT VIEW OF PLAINTIFF AND DEFENDANT. LECT. IX.</header>
appeared essentially as a plaintiff, much like the <term>distrainee</term> in our <term>Action of Replevin</term>; and there is no doubt that, if he submitted or was unsuccessful in challenging the proceedings of the other side, he paid not only the original debt but also various additional penalties resulting from his neglect to comply with previous notices to discharge it. Such a procedure seems to us to be based on the now monstrous assumption that plaintiffs are always in the right and defendants are always in the wrong. Yet this assumption would perhaps not have struck the earliest authors of legal reform as altogether monstrous, nor could they have quite understood the modern principle which requires the complainant to establish, at all events, a *<term>primâ facie</term> case*. To them, the person most likely to be in the right would appear to be the man who faced the many risks attending the effort to obtain redress—the man who complained to the <term>Popular Assembly</term>, the man who cried for justice to the King sitting in the gate. It is only when violent wrongdoing has ceased to be widespread, when the dangers of challenging the oppression of powerful men have become insignificant, and when the law has been long and regularly administered according to technical procedure, that unjust claims are seen to be hardly less common than unjust refusals to satisfy them. In one particular case, the complaint of the King, the old assumption that complainants are presumably in the right was long maintained

<header>LECT. IX. GAIUS AND BLACKSTONE.</header>

Gaius, speaking of the <term>Legis Actiones</term> generally, observes that “they fell into discredit because, through the excessive subtlety of the ancient lawyers, things came to such a pass that whoever committed the smallest error failed altogether.”

Blackstone, many centuries later, makes the following remark on the English <term>Law of Distress</term>: “The many details involved in taking a distress used to make it a hazardous kind of proceeding; for, if any single irregularity was committed, it invalidated the whole.”

I quote these passages not only because of the curious similarity of language between two writers—the later of whom could not possibly have read the earlier—but because the excessive technicality of ancient law, which they both notice, goes some way toward explaining the severity and one-sidedness of the old Teutonic procedure. The power of seizing a man’s property extrajudicially in satisfaction of a demand was, as Professor Sohm justly remarks, a kind of two-edged sword. You might bring your adversary down with it, but you were extremely likely to injure yourself. For, unless the complainant who sought to <term>distrain</term> went through all the acts and words required by the law with the most

<page-num>274</page-num>
<header>REASONS FOR THE SURVIVAL OF DISTRESS. LECT. IX.</header>

The difficulty of putting the procedure into operation thus immediately made disputants cautious in resorting to it and seemed to people in general to compensate for its inherent unfairness. This consideration, however—though it explains in part how the harsh ancient law reconciled itself to the sense of right—is not by itself sufficient to account for the form it assumed in the <term>Teutonic Codes</term>, or for the survival of a portion of it within our own institutions.

I cannot doubt that the practice I have called by the general name of <term>Distress</term> kept its place in ancient Teutonic law partly as a mere "survival." I have already insisted that one great characteristic of primitive ages was the scarcity of human ideas. Societies just emerging from a savage state had been used to associating the redress of wrong with the seizure of a wrongdoer’s goods, and they were mentally unable to completely disconnect the two even when they began to regulate the practice. They did not, therefore, supersede <term>distress</term> with a completely new system, but grafted it onto a later procedure, which occasionally took the form so curiously preserved in its main features to our own day by <term>English Common Law</term>, but which at a relatively later date and

<page-num>275</page-num>
<header>LECT. IX. JUDICIAL PROCEDURE OF SALIC LAW.</header>

more generally, it may be believed to have shaped itself on the model of the rules observed by the <term>Salian Franks</term>.

It is not possible to explain all survivals by some convenience they incidentally serve. Some have undoubtedly been continued by superstition, and some by mere habit. But those relics of ancient thought and conduct that have been kept alive longest have generally had a usefulness of their own. Here, the <term>private redress</term> of wrongs, incorporated into legal procedure, served to compel the appearance of the defendant and his submission to <term>jurisdiction</term> at a time when <term>judicial authority</term> was still in its infancy, and when Courts of Justice could not yet completely and regularly command the aid of <term>sovereign power</term>. Gradually, as the public force—the arm of the State—was increasingly placed at the disposal of tribunals, they were better able to do without extrajudicial assistance. In the state of <term>Teutonic law</term> represented by the <term>Frankish Code</term>, we find a specific class of cases tried entirely judicially (in our modern sense of the word) from the initial stage to the judgment; but the judgment is not operative by its own force. If the defendant has expressly promised to obey it, the <term>Count</term> or royal deputy, upon being properly summoned, will execute it; but if no such promise has been made, the plaintiff has no remedy except an application to the King in person.

<page-num>276</page-num>
-><header>LATER HISTORY OF DISTRESS. LECT. IX</header><-

Not long after the <term>Franks</term> settled within the Empire, however, we find that another step was taken toward the administration of justice on modern principles; now, the royal deputy executes the judgment even if there has been no promise to submit to it. At this point, <term>Distress</term> is completely taken out of the hands of private litigants, and <term>extrajudicial seizure</term> becomes <term>judicial seizure</term>. This change is obviously a result of the growing vigor of the Courts, which in our own country was largely due to the development of <term>royal justice</term> at the expense of <term>popular justice</term>. Still, English judicial proceedings long retained the character of these old practices. Every student of our ancient English legal procedures will remember how, on very little apparent provocation, the King constantly took the lands of the defendant into his hands or seized his goods, simply to compel or finalize his submission to the <term>royal jurisdiction</term>. It seems probable that <term>Distress</term> was gradually lost and absorbed by <term>Attachment</term> and <term>Distringas</term>. The theory of <term>Attachment</term> now is that it is the taking of property into the actual or constructive possession of the judicial power. The later course of change—under which it has faded into an occasional and exceptional proceeding requiring justification by special reasons—corresponds with the growing confidence of Courts of Justice in their possession of the irresistible power granted to them by the sovereign. <page-num>277</page-num>
<header>LECT. IX. MODERN THEORY OF DISTRESS.</header>

...of that fragment of the primitive institution which remains in our law, I imagine that **Distress** would at most have become a mere survival, confined perhaps to the impounding of stray cattle, if several statutory innovations had not turned it into a convenient extra-judicial remedy for landlords by giving the distrainor a power of sale which, in old English law, was limited to a few very special demands. The modern theory of **Distress** is that a landlord is allowed to distrain because, by the nature of the case, he is always compelled to give his tenant credit, and that he can distrain without notice because every man is supposed to know when his rent is due. But this theory, though it explains the continuance of **Distress** to our day, does not at all fit in with the most ancient ideas on the subject, and could not indeed be easily made to square with the practice of distraint even at a date as relatively late as that at which <term>Bracton</term> wrote. How accidental the association of **Distress** is with the powers of landlords may be seen from the fact that, although there are plentiful traces of the institution in ancient <term>Scottish law</term>, the same practical results which the English system produces by allowing landlords to distrain for rent are chiefly attained in Scotland by applying the <term>Romanized Law of Hypothec</term> <note>A legal right over a debtor's property as security for a debt, similar to a lien or mortgage.</note> to landlord and tenant.

<page-num>278</page-num>
<header>DEVELOPMENT OF REMEDIES. LECT. IX.</header>

systems, the following conclusions regarding the <unclear>h</unclear>istorical development of the remedies which grew out of the savage practice of violently seizing property in redress for a supposed wrong. Nascent law adopted two alternative expedients. One of these consisted of tolerating distraint up to a certain point; it was overlooked so far as it served to compel the submission of defendants to the jurisdiction of the courts, but in all other cases, it was treated as a willful breach of the peace. The other was the incorporation of distraint into a regular procedure. The complainant must observe a great number of forms at his own risk; but if he observes them, he can distrain in the end. In a still more advanced stage of legal ideas, the tribunals take the seizure of land or goods into their own hands, using it freely to coerce defendants into submission. Finally, courts of justice resort to coercion before judgment only on the rarest occasions, certain as they at last are of the effectiveness of their process, and of the power which they hold in trust from the sovereign commonwealth.

<summary>The author traces the evolution of legal remedies from private property seizure to a regulated judicial system, identifying stages where distraint was either tolerated to force court appearance or formalized into strict legal procedures. In mature legal systems, the courts take direct control of property seizure and rely on their established authority rather than pre-judgment coercion.</summary>

<page-num>279</page-num>
<header>LECT. X. IRISH LAW OF DISTRESS.</header>

## LECTURE X.

### THE PRIMITIVE FORMS OF LEGAL REMEDIES.

->II.<-

I move from the early law of procedure in Roman and Teutonic societies to the corresponding branch of another ancient legal system which has only recently been revealed to us. So far as its existence was even suspected, it was supposed until recently to be separated by particularly sharp distinctions from all Germanic bodies of custom.

Rather more than half of the Senchus Mor is devoted to the Law of Distress. The Senchus Mor, as I told you, claims to be a Code of Irish law, and indeed to be that very Code which was prepared under the influence of St. Patrick upon the introduction of Christianity into Ireland. I added that, in the present state of our knowledge, no theory regarding the date of this Brehon compendium can be proposed with much confidence. It may be that some such revision of the pre-Christian law did take place; it may be that the Brehon lawyers only conjectured that it must have taken place;

<page-num>280</page-num>
<header>DISTRESS ACCORDING TO SENCHUS MOR. LECT. X.</header>

It may be that a document of unusual dimensions, and proportionately valued by the Brehon law school which happened to possess it, gradually came to be associated with a name held in pre-eminent honor or regarded as pre-eminently sacred—a process believed to have several examples in the history of Eastern jurisprudence. However, these doubts regarding the true date of the Senchus Mor do not diminish the significance and instructiveness of the fact that in a volume of great antiquity, of undoubted genuineness, and evidently thought by its owners to contain all that was important in the law, the Law of Distress—now an extremely subordinate branch of our legal system—occupies an extraordinarily large amount of space.

I borrow from the Editor of the first volume of "Ancient Laws of Ireland" the following summary of the old Irish law of distress as laid down in the Senchus Mor:

> "The plaintiff or creditor, after first giving the proper notice, proceeded to distrain in the case of a defendant or debtor who was not of the rank of a chieftain. If the defendant or debtor was a person of chieftain rank, it was necessary not only to give notice, but also to 'fast upon him.' The act of fasting upon him consisted of going to his residence and waiting there for a certain time without food. If the plaintiff did not receive satisfaction for his claim within a certain time, or a"

<page-num>281</page-num>
<header>LECT. X. DISTRESS ACCORDING TO SENCHUS MOR.</header>

pledge for it, he immediately, accompanied by a law agent, witnesses, and others, seized his distress. When seized, the distress was in certain cases subject to a Stay, which was a period varying according to fixed rules. During this time, the debtor received back the distress and kept it in his own possession, while the creditor held a lien upon it. Such a distress is a "distress with time"; however, under certain circumstances and in particular cases, an "immediate distress" was made. The unique feature of this was that during the fixed period of the Stay, the distress was not allowed to remain in the debtor’s possession, but was held by the creditor, or kept in one of the recognized greens or pounds.

If the debt was not paid by the end of the Stay, the creditor took away the distress and placed it in a pound. He then served notice of the distress on the debtor whose property he had seized, letting him know where the distrained property was impounded. The distress remained in the pound for a certain period, fixed according to its nature (<term>*dithim*</term>, translated as "delay in pound," is the name of this period). At the end of the delay in pound, the Forfeiting Time began to run, during which the distress became forfeited at the rate of three "<term>seds</term>" per day, until it was entirely forfeited. If the total value of the distress forfeited in this way was exactly equal to the original debt and the subsequent expenses, the debt was cleared; if it

<header>IRISH AND GERMANIC DISTRESS. LECT. X.</header>

was less than this, a second distress was taken for the difference; and, if it was more, the surplus was returned. All of these proceedings were managed by the party himself, or his law-agent, along with the several witnesses of the various steps, and other necessary parties.

> ‘But if, instead of allowing his cattle to go to the pound, the debtor gave a sufficient pledge—for example, his son, or some article of value—to the creditor, promising that he would within a certain time try the right to the distress by law, the creditor was bound to receive such a pledge. If he did not go to law, as he had undertaken, the pledge became forfeited for the original debt. At any time, up to the end of the “<term>dithim</term>,” the debtor could recover his cattle by paying the debt and such expenses as had been incurred. But, if he neglected to redeem them until the “<term>dithim</term>” had expired, then he could only redeem such as were still unforfeited.’

The very existence in ancient Ireland of the law thus summarized is almost enough by itself to destroy those reckless theories of race which assert an original, inherent difference of idea and usage between <term>Teuton</term> and <term>Celt</term>. The Irish system of Distress is obviously, in all essential features, the Germanic system. It wears, on its face, a very strong general resemblance to the corresponding branch of our Common Law; and I have seen some very ingenious attempts to account for the differences between the two by suggestions that the primitive contour of the

<page-num>283</page-num>
<header>LECT. X. IRISH AND ENGLISH DISTRESS.</header>

English law of <term>Distress</term> has been impaired. The goal of such speculations is to argue for the direct derivation of the English set of rules from the Celtic; but it does not seem necessary to me to resort to a hypothesis which has great and special difficulties of its own. The virtual identity of the Irish law of <term>Distress</term> with the Teutonic law is best demonstrated by comparing it with the Teutonic systems of procedure as a whole. Thus, the Distress of the <term>Senchus Mor</term> is not, like the Distress of the English <term>Common Law</term>, a remedy confined in the main to the demands of a lord on his tenants; as in the <term>Salic</term> and other Continental <term>Germanic Codes</term>, it extends to breaches of contract, and indeed, so far as the <term>Brehon law</term> is already known, it would appear to be the universal method of prosecuting claims of all kinds. The Notice, again, to the person whose goods are to be distrained—which it strongly insists upon—though not found in the surviving English <term>Common Law</term>, occupies an important place, as I stated, in other Teutonic collections of rules. So too, the attendance of witnesses is required by the Continental Codes; and, although the presence of the <term>Brehon law</term> agent is unique to the Irish system and very characteristic of it, certain persons having much the same duties are required by some of the Teutonic systems to be present during the process of <term>distraint</term>. Further, the <term>Stay of proceedings</term>, which has been compared to an Attach-

<header>IRISH AND TEUTONIC DISTRESS. LECT. X.</header>

Thereupon, the seizure is interrupted and an opportunity is given for inquiring into the regularity of the proceedings and, probably also, into the justice of the claim. The <term>Lien</term> or charge upon the distrained property, which the <term>Irish law</term> confers on the creditor during the currency of the <term>Stay</term>, is not found in the Continental <term>Teutonic law</term> in this exact shape; but, at a particular stage of the Salic proceedings, the creditor has the power of forbidding the debtor from selling or mortgaging any part of his property until the debt has been satisfied. On the other hand, several features of the Irish system, which are wholly absent from the Continental Teutonic procedure, or very faintly marked in it, belong conspicuously to the <term>English law</term>. Among these may be placed the impounding, and the ‘*taking in <term>withernam</term>*,’ but the greatest resemblance of all, and the common point of dissimilarity from the most ancient of the <term>Leges Barbarorum</term>, lies in the fact that the Irish procedure, like the English, requires neither assistance nor permission from any <term>Court of Justice</term>. <page-num>285</page-num>
<header>LECT. X. DIFFICULTIES AS TO IRISH DISTRESS.</header>

In all the Teutonic bodies of custom except the English and the Lombardic, even when the greatest latitude of seizure is allowed to litigants outside of Court, some judicial person or body must be applied to before they proceed to extremities. With us, however, the entire seizure is completed before authority is called in; and the Irish law has exactly the same peculiarity. Not only so, but the Irish law corresponds to the English law of <term>Distress</term> at a very advanced stage of development. It does not employ the seizure of cattle merely as a method of extorting satisfaction. It provides, as you have seen, for their forfeiture in discharge of the demand for which they were taken; and thus is distinguished by an improvement which was only added to the English law by statute after the lapse of several centuries.

The true difficulty in estimating the place of this Irish procedure in the historical development of law arises from doubts as to the part really played by the legal proceeding in which it terminated. The English process of <term>Distress</term>, wherever it was felt to be unjust, led up to, and ended in, the action of <term>Replevin</term>, and the Court, which ultimately tried the action, practically acquired its jurisdiction through the interposition of the <term>Sheriff</term> in restoring the cattle upon security given. No such interference with a high hand as that of the <term>Sheriff</term> appears to be contemplated by the Irish law; but the <term>Brehon</term> lawyer who ought properly

<summary>The author explains that both Irish and English law allow for the seizure of property before seeking judicial authority, a rarity among Teutonic customs. What authority did the Irish Courts have at any time when the <term>Brehon</term> law was respected? What were these Courts? To what extent did they control the public force of the sovereign State? Was there any sovereign power established in any part of Ireland at any time that could give effective jurisdiction to Courts of Justice and effective force to the law? All these questions—of which the last ones are truly the great problems of ancient Irish history—must be answered to some degree before we can have a confident opinion on the actual operation of the Law of Distress described at such length in the *Senchus Mor*.

The learned editors of the various introductions included in the official publications of *Ancient Irish Law* are clearly of the opinion that whatever jurisdiction the Irish Courts possessed was "voluntary," to use the technical phrase. In this view, the Law of Distress was clearly enough conceived by the <term>Brehon</term> lawyers, but it depended for practical obedience on the support of public opinion and popular respect for a professional caste. Its goal was to force disputants to submit to what was more an arbitration than a legal action, held before a <term>Brehon</term> selected

Dr. Sullivan, in his Introduction, admits that the information which has come down to us on these subjects is very fragmentary, and so obscure that it will be impossible to give a satisfactory account of them until the whole of the law-fragments in Irish <term>MSS.</term> are published or at least made accessible to scholars; but he nevertheless believes in the historical reality of this organization, and he speaks (Introduction, pp. cclii. cclxii.) of the Irish Courts in language of an extremely modern tone. Such tribunals might also be highly developed, and yet their jurisdiction might be only voluntary. Sohm appears to me to have proven that the Frankish Popular Courts did not enforce their own decrees; if the defendant had promised to submit to an award, the local deputy of the King might be required to enforce it, but, if there had been no such promise, the plaintiff was forced to petition the King in person.

There is much reason, in fact, for thinking that in the earliest times—and before the full development of that royal authority which has lent so much vigor to the arm of the law in most <term>Aryan</term> communities, but which was virtually denied to the Irish—Courts of Justice existed less for the purpose of doing right universally than for the purpose of supplying an alternative to the violent redress of wrongs. Even then, if we suppose that the Ireland which is said to have enjoyed an elaborate judicial organization was much ruder and wilder than Irish patriots would probably allow it to have been, there is no such inconsistency between the prevalence of disorder and the frequency of litigation that they would exclude one another. <header>LECT. X. LITIGATION IN INDIA.</header>

petual fighting and perpetual litigation may go on side by side, and that a highly technical procedure may be scrupulously followed at a time when homicide is an everyday occurrence. The fact seems to be that contention in Court takes the place of contention in arms, but only gradually takes its place; and it is a tenable theory that many of the strange peculiarities of ancient law—the technical snares, traps, and pitfalls with which it abounds—really represent and carry on the feints, strategies, and ambushes of actual armed strife between man and man, and between tribe and tribe. Even in our own day, when a wild province is annexed to the British Indian Empire, there is a most curious and instructive rush of litigants to the Courts which are immediately established. The arm of the law summarily suppresses violence, and the men who can no longer fight go to law instead, in numbers which sometimes make Indian officials believe that there must be something harmful in the law and procedure which tempt men into Court who never saw a Court before. The simple explanation is that the same natural impulse is gratified in a new way; hasty appeals to a judge succeed hurried quarrels, and hereditary lawsuits take the place of ancestral blood-feuds. If the transition from one state of society to another in modern India were not sudden but gradual and slow, as it universally was in the old <term>Aryan</term> world, we should see the battle with

When, however, we consider the place of the old <term>Irish Law of Distress</term> in legal history, the point we must attend to is not so much the mere existence of <term>Courts of Justice</term> as the effectiveness of their process—or in other words, the degree to which they command the public force of the <term>Commonwealth</term>. I think I have shown it to be probable that, as courts grow stronger, they first take under their control the barbarous practice of making reprisals on a wrongdoer by seizing his property, and ultimately they absorb it into their own procedure. Now, the <term>Irish Law of Distress</term> belongs in one respect to a very early stage in this course of development, since it is even more completely <term>extrajudicial</term> than that fragment of the primitive, barbarous remedy which has survived among ourselves. On the other hand, there are several particulars in which it is not more, but distinctly less, archaic than the <term>English Common law</term>. The ‘Notice’ to the defendant for which it provides—the ‘Stay,’ or temporary retention of the goods by the owner, subject to a <term>lien</term>—the witnesses who must be present, and the skilled legal adviser who must attend throughout the proceedings—belong to a range of ideas much more advanced than those under which all these precautions are dispensed

# LECT. X. RELATIVE ANTIQUITY OF IRISH DISTRESS.

Even stronger evidence of maturity is provided by the almost inconceivable multitude of rules and distinctions which the <term>Senchus Mor</term> applies to every part of the proceedings; and our own experience shows that the most remarkable feature of the old <term>Irish Law of Distress</term>—the forfeiture of the property taken in distress when the original debt and the expenses of custody reach its full value—has its place among the latest improvements in jurisprudence.

Whatever, then, is the truth regarding the Ireland of the golden age, these characteristics of the <term>Irish Law of Distress</term> leave on my mind a very distinct impression that it was brought to the shape in which we find it within a society in which the action of <term>Courts of Justice</term> was feeble and intermittent. It speaks well for the spirit of equity and reasonableness which animated the <term>Brehon lawyers</term> who gave it its form, and much also for their ingenuity, but it suggests that they relied little on the assistance of Courts and directed their efforts toward making the most of a remedy which was almost entirely <term>extrajudicial</term>. The comparison of the <term>Teutonic laws</term> shows that they had a basis of <term>Aryan custom</term> to work upon; but, while in other communities the superstructure on this foundation was the work of Courts constantly feeling themselves stronger, in Ireland it seems to have been the work of lawyers dependent in the main for the usefulness of their labors on popular respect for their order. It may also be that the law laid down in the <term>Senchus Mor</term> is of a much later date than the compilers of that tract claim, and that it therefore received its shape in times of disturbance and confusion. But I cannot believe that it ever coincided with a period of judicial activity and efficiency.

From what I have said, I think you will have gathered the chief points of difference between the <term>Irish Law of Distress</term>, as laid down in the <term>Senchus Mor</term>, and the <term>English Common Law of Distress</term>, as declared by the earliest authorities which our Courts recognize. Both had the same origin, but the Irish <term>distraint</term> was a universal, highly developed proceeding employed in enforcing all kinds of demands, while the corresponding English remedy—though much less carefully guarded by express rules—was confined to a very limited and special class of cases. <header>LECT. X. SPENSER'S CRITICISM ON LAW OF DISTRESS.</header>

wrongful distraining of any man's goods against the form of Common Law a felony. These statutes certainly seem to have been at first meant for the good of the realm and for restraining a foul abuse which was then common among those people, and yet is not altogether laid to rest: that when anyone was indebted to another, he would first demand his debt, and if he were not paid, he would immediately go and take a distress of his goods and cattle wherever he could find them to the value of the debt, which he would keep until he was satisfied. And the simple churl (as they call him) still commonly does this through ignorance of his wrongdoing, or because of an evil custom that has long been established among them. But while this is certainly most unlawful, it seems to me too harsh to make it a capital offense, since the party has no intention to steal the other's goods or to conceal the distress; rather, he usually does it openly before witnesses. Furthermore, these same statutes are so loosely written (besides one being so senselessly contrived that it scarcely contains any reason) that they are often and very easily twisted to the detriment of the subject. For example, if someone goes to distrain upon his own land or tenement, which he may lawfully do, yet if in doing so he transgresses the slightest point of the Common Law, he immediately commits a felony. Or if someone on any other occasion takes anything from another, such as when boys sometimes snatch one another's caps, it is immediately a felony. This is a very harsh law.

<page-num>294</page-num>
<header>WRONGFUL DISTRESS A CAPITAL FELONY. LECT. X</header>

Spenser continues, in a passage that I do not need to quote in full, to explain these statutes by a special provision in the charters of most Anglo-Irish corporate towns. He tells us that English law was not in use beyond the walls, and the burgesses had the power granted to them to seize the goods of any Irishman staying in or passing through the town for any debt whatsoever. He suggests that the Irish population living outside the walls was led by this practice to assume it was lawful to distrain the property of the townspeople. This explanation, if true, would be sad enough, but we know that it cannot represent the entire truth, and the real story is even sadder. The Irish used the remedy of distress because they knew no other remedy, and the English made it a capital felony for an Irishman to follow the only law with which he was acquainted. Indeed, those very subtleties of old English law which, as Blackstone says, made the act of seizure "a hazardous sort of proceeding" for the civil distrainor, could lead an Irishman to the gallows if he fell into the slightest mistake while conscientiously attempting to carry out the foreign law. It is some small consolation to be able, as one result of the inquiries we have been conducting, to dismiss as worthless the easy justification of those who disregard these cruelties as part of the inevitable struggle between men of different races. <header>LECT. X. THEORIES CONCERNING DISTRESS.</header>

Among the writers who have recognized the strong affinities connecting the English and Irish Law of Distress, I find it difficult to distinguish between those who believe in the direct derivation of English law from pre-existing Celtic customs common to Britain and Ireland, and those who see a sufficient explanation for the resemblances between the two sets of rules in their common parentage. I am not at all prepared to deny that recent research, and particularly studies into old French customary law, make it easier to believe than it once was that portions of primitive or original custom survive even the most devastating conquests. But I need hardly say that the hypothesis of the direct descent of any significant branch of English law from British usage is beset by extraordinary difficulties, not the least of which is the curiously strong case that may also be made for the purely Roman origin of a many institutions and rules which we are accustomed to consider purely English and Germanic. On this last point, a very interesting little volume which has attracted too little notice, Mr. Coote’s *Neglected Fact in English History*, may be read

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of the Law of Distress, moving from the possibility of Roman or British origins to the theory of a shared Aryan heritage.</meta>

<page-num>296</page-num>
<header>DISTRESS AN ARYAN CUSTOM LECT. X.</header>

with advantage, and should be compared with the reply to its arguments—on the whole a successful one—which Mr. Freeman published in *Macmillan’s Magazine* in July 1870. The true rival to all these theories about the derivation of one body of custom from another is, of course, the theory of the common descent of all from an original basis of usage which we must, at least provisionally, call Aryan. Limiting ourselves to the practice we have been investigating—the remedy for a supposed wrong through distress—if there could be any doubt of its being a legacy from primitive Aryan usages, it would be removed by the remarkable detail that connects Irish law with Hindu law. The Irish rules of distraint very strongly resemble the English rules and, less strongly, the continental Teutonic rules; but they include one rule not found in any Teutonic code—one almost unintelligible within the Irish system, but known to govern conduct even at this hour all over the East, where its meaning is perfectly clear. This is the rule that a creditor who requires payment from a debtor of higher rank than himself shall "fast upon him." What possible explanation could cover all these facts except that the primitive Aryans bequeathed the remedy of distress to the communities that sprang from them, and that variations in detail have been produced by what Dr. Sullivan, in his *Introduction*, has aptly called "dynamical influences"?

<summary>The author argues that the practice of distress originates from a common Aryan ancestor, citing a specific shared custom of "fasting upon" a debtor found in both Irish and Hindu law as definitive evidence.</summary>

<page-num>297</page-num>
<header>LECTURE X. INDIAN FORM OF DISTRESS.</header>

Here is the leading provision of the *Senchus Mor* on the subject (i. 113):—

> ‘Notice precedes every distress in the case of the inferior ranks, unless it is by persons of distinction or upon persons of distinction. Fasting precedes distress in their case. He who does not give a pledge in response to fasting is an evader of all; he who disregards all things shall be paid by neither God nor man.’

Mr. Whitley Stokes was the first, I believe, to point out that the institution referred to here was identical with a practice spread across the entire East, called by the Hindus ‘sitting dharna.’ I will soon read you a passage in which the proceeding is described as it was found in India before the British Government, which has always regarded it as an abuse, had made much progress in its efforts to suppress it. But perhaps the most striking examples of the ancient custom are to be found at this day in Persia, where (I am told) a man intending to enforce payment of a demand by fasting begins by sowing some barley at his debtor’s door and sitting down in the middle. The symbolism is clear enough. The creditor means that he will stay where he is without food, either until he is paid or until the barley seeds grow up and provide him with bread to eat.

The corresponding Indian practice is known, as I stated before, as ‘sitting dharna’—dharna, according to the prevailing opinion, being exactly equivalent to the

<header>SITTING DHARNA. LECTURE X.</header>

Among the methods of enforcing payment of a debt described in the collection of rules attributed to the semi-divine legislator, Manu (viii. 49), is one which Sir William Jones translates as "the mediation of friends"; however, more recent Sanskrit scholars assert that the expression in the original text signifies "dharna." And in the *Vyavahara Mayukha*, a Brahmanical law book of great authority, Brihaspiti—a legal writer sometimes grouped with Manu—is cited as listing, among the lawful modes of compulsion by which a debtor can be forced to pay, "confining his wife, his son, or his cattle, or watching constantly at his door." This remarkable passage not only connects Hindu law with Irish law through the reference to "watching constantly at the door," but it also connects it with Teutonic traditions—and among them with English bodies of custom—by speaking of the distraint of cattle as a method of enforcing a demand. In the Western world, as far as I am aware, we have no examples of such a severe form of <term>distress</term> <note>legal seizure of property</note> as seizing a man’s wife or children, but it is somewhat curious that we have evidence of it having been common in ancient Ireland to give a son as a pledge to a creditor for the purpose of releasing distrained property.

<header>LECT. X. SHORE'S ACCOUNT OF DHARNA.</header>

the "watching constantly at the door" mentioned by Brihaspiti had assumed in British India before the end of the last century: "The inviolability of the Brahmin is a fixed principle among the Hindus, and to deprive him of life, either by direct violence or by causing his death in any way, is a crime that admits of no atonement. To this principle may be traced the practice called *<term>dharna</term>*, which may be translated as seizure or arrest. It is used by the Brahmins to achieve a goal that cannot be accomplished by any other means, and the process is as follows: The Brahmin who adopts this method for the purpose mentioned goes to the door or house of the person against whom it is directed, or wherever he may most conveniently arrest him; he then sits down in *<term>dharna</term>* with poison, a dagger, or some other instrument of suicide in his hand, and threatening to use it if his adversary should attempt to bother or pass him, he thus completely arrests him. In this situation the Brahmin fasts, and by the strictness of the custom, the unfortunate object of his arrest is also expected to fast; and thus they both remain until the initiator of the dharna obtains satisfaction. In this, as he seldom makes the attempt without the resolution to persevere, he rarely fails; for if the party thus arrested were to allow the Brahmin sitting in dharna to die of hunger, the sin would lie upon his head forever. You will observe that the ancient Brahminical writer merely speaks of confining a man to his house by "watching constantly at the door" as one among several modes of extorting satisfaction. He classifies it with forms of <term>distraint</term> more intelligible to us—the seizure of the debtor's cattle, of his wife, or of his child. Although the ancient rule has not come down to us along with its original context, we need not doubt that even in the earliest times it was enforced by a supernatural sanction, since every violation of the Brahminical Code was regarded by its authors not only as a civil offense but as a sin. Thus a Brahmin might quite well be imagined saying, like the writer in the *<term>Senchus Mor</term>*, "He who does not give a pledge to fasting is an evader of all; he who disregards all things shall not be paid by God or man." Many centuries then pass, which it would be pointless to calculate, and almost in our own day we find the ancient usage practiced in India, but with modifications corresponding to a great deal of change which is suspected to have occurred in Hindu theology. The vague supernatural penalty has become the definite supernatural penalty incurred by destroying life, and particularly human life. The creditor does not

When English law first attempted to suppress "sitting dharna," it was understood to be a specific type of oppression practiced by Brahmins for a monetary fee. This is the view held by the Indian Penal Code, which condemns it in the following terms (Section 508):

> "Anyone who voluntarily causes... any person to do something that the person is not legally bound to do... by inducing... that person to believe that he... will become, through some act of the offender, an object of Divine displeasure if he does not do what the offender intends to make him do... shall be punished with imprisonment, etc."

It seems to me that a reasonable explanation can be provided for the origin of these practices, which appear so strange today. We should not forget that all forms of <term>distress</term>—the seizure of a wife, child, or cattle—even when completely unregulated by law, were improvements on older customs. This . . . is the signal for gathering all the available adult male residents. These accordingly assemble and also sit down within conversing distance.’ After a long silence a conversation ensues, and the proceeding, which is a perfectly peaceful one, is continued by a long series of technical formalities and intricate pleadings. <header>LECTURE 10. FEUDAL LAW OF ALFRED.</header>

the action taken by the attacking party is an early form of notice, which is itself one of the most valuable of institutions; and connected with it is another primitive method: confining a man in his house until he provides satisfaction, instead of attacking him immediately. A very striking illustration of this is found in a law of Alfred, familiar to historical scholars (Kemble, ‘*Saxons*,’ i. 272; Thorpe, ‘*Ancient Laws*,’ i. 91):

> ‘Let the man who knows his enemy to be staying at home not fight before he has demanded justice from him. If he has the power to surround his enemy and besiege him in his house, let him keep him there for seven days but not attack him if he remains indoors. If then, after seven days, he is willing to surrender and give up his weapons, let him be kept safe for thirty days, and let notice be given to his kinsmen and friends. But if the plaintiff does not have the power of his own, let him ride to the Ealdorman, and, if the Ealdorman will not aid him, let him ride to the King before he fights.’

The passage ends with a provision whose spirit, strange to say, survives in the modern code making the loudest claim to civilized principles, the **Code Napoléon** (*Code Pénal, section 324*), to the effect that if the man who is staying at home is actually shut up in his house with the complainant's wife, daughter, or sister, he may be attacked and killed without ceremony.

<header>SETTLED PROPERTY OF MARRIED WOMEN. LECT. XI.</header>

# LECTURE XI.

## THE EARLY HISTORY OF THE SETTLED PROPERTY OF MARRIED WOMEN.

The subject on which I am about to speak may perhaps convey one lesson. It may serve as a warning against the careless use of the words "ancient" and "modern." I suppose there are few people who, approaching the topic of the Settled Property of Married Women without previous knowledge of its history, would not call it one of the most modern of subjects. It has given rise to intense controversy in our own time; some of the questions it suggests are not yet solved, and I dare say there are many here who believe they remember the first emergence of sound ideas on these issues. Yet, as a matter of fact, the discussion of the settled property of married women is a very old one. I do not say, considering the vast antiquity now claimed for the human race, that our earliest ancestors troubled themselves about the matter; but nothing can be more certain than that very soon after those divisions of mankind destined for ultimate greatness

<header>LECT. XI. ROMAN AND HINDU LAW.</header>

This assertion, I may observe, is less incredible to a Frenchman, or indeed to a citizen of any Continental State, than it possibly is to an Englishman. The law of the Continent on the property relations of husband and wife is in the main Roman law, very slightly transformed; and through the institutions of the Romans, the history of this branch of law may be traced to the earliest institutions of that portion of the human race which has proved capable of civilization.

The Roman and <term>Hindu</term> systems of law, from which I propose to illustrate my subject, are very far indeed from being the only sources from which information can be gathered concerning the infancy of mankind, or even concerning the <term>Aryan</term> race of men. But the evidence supplied by each of them is highly authentic, and, while both of them go back to what may fairly be called a vast antiquity, they both assume at their starting point the existence of the institution—by no means apparently universal among savage men—out of which, as I said, all civilization has grown: the Family. I need scarcely add that, even for historical purposes, their value is very unequal.

<header>ROMAN LAW. LECT. XI.</header>

There is no history so long, so continuous, and so authentic as that of the <term>Roman Law</term>; and yet it is quite remarkable that until about half a century ago it was systematically treated, except by a small minority of <term>jurists</term>, as if it had no history at all. This was a consequence of its great legal perfection. Let me pause to observe that, considering the time and effort spent in learning the <term>Latin</term> language, it is much to be regretted that so little is known of the chief branch of Latin literature. For it is really so expressed, and so composed, as to deserve the name of literature. Moreover, it was the only literature of the Romans which has any claim to originality; it was the only part of their literature in which the Romans themselves took any strong interest; and it is the one part which has profoundly influenced modern thought. One result, however, of its symmetry and clarity was that it was long regarded as a product of pure intellect, produced, so to speak, in a single effort. Those who attempted to construct a history for it were few and not highly regarded. But it happened that in 1816, the great German historian, <term>Niebuhr</term>, traveling in Italy, had his attention attracted at <term>Verona</term> to a <term>manuscript</term> of one of the Church Fathers, under the letters of which ancient writing appeared. This manuscript, when deciphered, proved to be a nearly perfect copy of an educational work, written in the second

<header>THE PATRIARCHAL FAMILY. LECT. XI</header>

seen actually flourishing in <term>India</term>, under the protection of <term>English Courts of Justice</term>.

The two societies, <term>Roman</term> and <term>Hindu</term>, which I take up for examination with the view of determining some of their earliest ideas concerning the property of women, are seen to be formed at what is, for practical purposes, the earliest stage of their history, by the multiplication of a particular unit or group: the <term>Patriarchal Family</term>. 

There has been much speculation lately among writers belonging to the school of so-called pre-historic inquiry concerning the place in the history of human society that this unique group, the <term>Patriarchal Family</term>, holds. Whether it has existed universally from all time—whether it has existed from all time only in certain races—or whether, in the races among whose institutions it appears, it has been formed by slow and gradual development—it has the same character and composition everywhere we find it. The group consists of animate and inanimate property—of wife, children, slaves, land, and goods—all held together by subjection to the despotic authority of the eldest male of the eldest ascending line: the father, grandfather, or even more remote ancestor. The force which binds the group together is Power. A child adopted into the <term>Patriarchal Family</term> belongs to it as perfectly as a child naturally born into it, and a child who severs his connection with it is lost to it altogether.

<summary>The author defines the Patriarchal Family as the primary unit of early Roman and Hindu societies, characterized by the absolute authority of the eldest male ancestor over all members and property. He notes that this structure remains identical across different cultures, held together by the concept of Power and including both biological and adopted members.</summary>

But when we first examine the <term>Patriarchal Family</term> through perfectly reliable evidence, it is already in a state of decay. The <term>emancipation</term> or legal liberation of male children from <term>parental power</term> by the father’s voluntary act has become a recognized custom; this is one of several practices that demonstrate a loosening of the stricter ideas from a more distant antiquity. Focusing our attention on women, we find that they have begun to inherit a share of family property alongside their male relatives. However, several signs suggest that their share was smaller, and they were still restricted in both the use and the disposal of it.

Here, however, we find the first trace of a distinction that runs through all legal history. Unmarried women, who were originally in no different position than married women, initially acquired a much higher degree of <term>proprietary independence</term>. The Family was based less upon actual relationship than upon power, and the husband acquired over his wife the same <term>despotic power</term> which the father had over his children. There can be no question that, in strict accordance with this conception of <term>Roman marriage</term>, all the wife’s property passed at first absolutely to the husband and became fused with the domain of the new family; and at this point begins, in any reasonable sense of the words, the early history of the <term>property of married women</term>.

The term, a famous one in legal history, is <term>*manus*</term>, the Latin word for ‘hand,’ and the wife was said <term>*convenire in manum*</term>, to come under the hand of her husband. I have elsewhere expressed a conjectural opinion that this word <term>*manus*</term> or hand, was at first the sole general term for <term>patriarchal power</term> among the Romans, and that it became confined to one form of that power by a process of <term>specialization</term> easily observable in the history of language. The allotment of particular names to special ideas which gradually disengage themselves from a general idea is apparently determined by accident. We cannot give a reason, other than mere chance, why power over a wife should have retained the name of <term>*manus*</term>, why power over a child should have obtained another name, <term>*potestas*</term>, why power over slaves and inanimate property should in later times be called <term>*dominium*</term>. But, although the transformation of meanings is capricious, the process of <term>specialization</term> is a permanent phenomenon, in the highest degree important and worthy of observation. When once this <term>specialization</term> has in any case been effected, I venture to say that there can be no accurate historical vision for him who will not, in mental contemplation, recombine the separated elements. Taking the conceptions which have their root in the <term>family relation</term>—what we call property, what we call marital right, what we call parental authority,

<header>PRIMITIVE BLENDING OF NOTIONS. LECT. XI.</header>

If, leaving the Family, we pass on to the group which stands next above it in the primitive organization of society—that combination of families, in a larger aggregate, for which at present I have no better name than <term>Village Community</term>—we find it impossible to understand the existing examples of it, unless we recognize that, in the infancy of ideas, legislative, judicial, executive, and administrative power are not distinguished, but considered as one and the same. There is no distinction drawn in the mind between passing a law, affirming a rule, trying an offender, carrying out a sentence, or prescribing a set of directions to a communal official. All these are regarded as exercises of an identical power held by some authority or body of authorities. When these communities become blended into the larger groups which are conveniently called political, the recombination of ideas originally blended becomes infinitely more difficult, and, when successfully achieved, is among the greatest accomplishments of historical insight. <header>LECT. XI. USURPATION AND MARRIAGE.</header>

primitive customs than by conquering them—or to the marvelously complex societies to which we belong, and in which the influence of primitive family and village notions still makes itself felt amid the mass of modern thought—I still venture to say that one great secret for understanding these groups of men is the mental reconstruction of ancient, general, and blended ideas by recombining the modern specialized ideas that are their offshoots.

The next stage in the legal history of Roman civil marriage is marked by a mechanism, very familiar to students of Roman law, by which the process of "coming under the hand" was avoided, and the wife no longer legally became her husband's daughter. From very early times, it seems to have been possible to contract a legal marriage simply by establishing the existence of a conjugal society. But in old Roman law, the effect of continuous conjugal society on the wife was precisely the same as the effect of continuous servile occupation on a man in a Roman household. The institution called <term>Usucapion</term>, or (in modern times) <term>Prescription</term>—the acquisition of ownership through continuous possession—lay at the root of ancient Roman law, whether concerning persons or things; and, in the former case, the woman became the daughter of the head of the house; in the latter case, the man became his slave. The legal

He describes the later legal shift where this process was bypassed, allowing a wife to remain outside her husband’s legal "hand."</summary>
<keywords>Roman law, Usucapion, Prescription, marriage, conjugal society, servile occupation, legal history, primitive customs, manus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the Roman legal concept of Usucapion was applied to marriage, specifically how continuous possession of a wife led to the husband gaining legal authority over her, and how that process could be interrupted.</meta>

<page-num>316</page-num>
<header>METHOD OF DEFEATING USUCAPION. LECT. XI.</header>

The result was not the same in the two cases only because the degrees of power had now been distinguished, and <term>paternal power</term> had become different from the lordship of the master over the slave. In order, however, that acquisition by <term>Usucapion</term> might be completed, the possession had to be continuous; there was no <term>Usucapion</term> where the possession had been interrupted—where, to use the technical phrase (which has had a rather distinguished history), there had been <term>usurpation</term>, the breaking of *usus* or enjoyment. It was possible, therefore, for the wife, by absenting herself for a specific period from her husband's home, to protect herself from his acquisition of <term>paternal power</term> over her person and property. The exact duration of the absence necessary to defeat the <term>Usucapion</term>—three days and three nights—is provided for in the ancient <term>Roman Code</term>, the <term>Twelve Tables</term>, and certainly the appearance of such a rule in so early a monument of legislation is quite remarkable. It is extremely likely, as several writers on the ancient <term>Roman law</term> speculated, that the purpose of the provision was to clear up a doubt and to declare with certainty what period of absence was necessary to legalize an existing practice. But it would never do to suppose that the practice was common, or rapidly became common. In this, as in several other cases, it is probable that the lack of qualification in the clause of the <term>Twelve Tables</term> is to be explained by

It details how the Twelve Tables allowed a wife to interrupt this process by absenting herself for three days and nights, thereby preventing the husband from acquiring paternal power over her.</summary>
