<header>LECT. XI. NEWER ROMAN MARRIAGE.</header>

the reliance of the legislator on custom, opinion, or religious feeling to prevent the abuse of his legislation. The wife who saved herself from coming under marital authority no doubt had the legal status of wife, but the Latin antiquaries evidently believed that her position was not at first held to be respectable. By the time of Gaius, however, any association of imperfect respectability with the newer form of marriage was decaying or had perished; and, in fact, we know that marriage, 'without coming under the hand,' became the ordinary Roman marriage, and that the relation of husband and wife became a voluntary conjugal society, terminable at the pleasure of either party by divorce. It was with the state of conjugal relations thus produced that the growing Christianity of the Roman world waged a war ever increasing in fierceness; yet it remained to the last the basis of the Roman legal conception of marriage, and to a certain extent it even colors the Canon law, founded though it be, on the whole, on the sacramental view of marriage.

For our present purpose it is necessary to regard this newer marriage just when it had superseded the ancient and stricter usages of wedlock, and just before it began to be modified by the modern and much severer principles of the Christian community. For at this point in the history of marriage we come upon the beginnings of that system of settling the property

<page-num>318</page-num>
<header>PROPERTY OF MARRIED WOMEN. LECT. XI.</header>

of married women which has supplied the greatest part of Continental Europe with its law of marriage settlement. It appears to be an immediate consequence of thoroughly established legal principles that, as soon as the wife ceased to pass by marriage into her husband's family and to become his daughter in the eyes of the law, her property was no longer transferred to him.

In the earlier period of Roman law, this property, both present and prospective, would have remained with her own family; and, if she were no longer under direct parental authority, it would have been administered by her guardians for the benefit of her male relatives. As we know, however, and as I stated before, the power of guardians was gradually reduced to a shadow. The legal result would seem to have been that the woman was placed in the same position as a French wife today under what the French Code calls the *régime of biens séparés*, or as an English wife whose property has been secured for her separate use by an appropriate marriage settlement or by the operation of the new Married Women's Property Act.

But, although this was the legal consequence, it would be a social anachronism to assume that it followed rapidly or generally in practice. The original object of marriage "without coming under the hand" was undoubtedly to prevent the husband from acquiring excessive proprietary power, not to deprive him of all such power, and

<page-num>310</page-num>
<header>LECT. XI. THE DOTAL ESTATE</header>

indeed the legal result of this marriage, unless practically qualified in some way, would unquestionably have been far in advance of social feeling. Here, then, we encounter an institution which, of all purely artificial institutions, has perhaps had the longest and the most important history. This is the *dos*, or dotal estate, something very different from our "dower." It has become the *dot* of French law and is the favorite form of settling the property of married women all over the continent of Europe. It is a contribution by the wife’s family, or by the wife herself, intended to assist the husband in bearing the expenses of the marriage household. Only the revenue belonged to the husband, and many detailed rules, which need not be specified here, prevented him from spending it on objects unrelated to the purpose of the settlement. The *corpus* or capital of the settled property was, among the Romans (as it is now in France), incapable of alienation, unless with the permission of a court of justice. If any part of the wife’s property was not settled on her as *dos*, it became her *parapherna*. *Parapherna* means something very different from our "paraphernalia," and is the *biens séparés* of French law. It was that portion of a wife’s property which was held by her under the strict law applicable to a woman marrying without "coming under the hand" <note>This refers to the Roman legal state of *manus*, where a wife was under her husband's absolute legal power</note>. It is only quite recently, under the Married Women’s Property Act, that we have arrived at a similar institution, since money settled to a wife’s separate use—though practically the same thing—required a settlement to create it.

I have now summarized a very long and, in some parts, very intricate history. Roman law began by giving all the wife’s property to the husband, because she was assumed to be, in law, his daughter. It ended by having as its general rule that all the wife’s property was under her own control, except when a part of it had been converted by settlement into a fund for contributing to the expenses of the conjugal household. But, no doubt, the exception to the general rule was the ordinary practice. In all respectable households, as is currently the case on the Continent, there was a settlement by way of *dos*. We should not suppose there was among the Romans any such form of contract as we are accustomed to under the name of a **Marriage Settlement**. The mechanism was infinitely simpler. A few words on paper would suffice to bring any part of the wife’s property under the well-established rules supplied by the written law for **dotal settlements**, and nothing more than these words would be needed, unless the

This simple, but most admirable, arrangement of having, so to speak, model settlements set forth ready-made in the law, which may be adopted or not at pleasure, characterizes the French Code Napoléon, and it was inherited by the French from the Romans.

Warning you that the account I have given you of the transitions through which the Roman law of settled property passed is, by necessity, fragmentary, I move to the evidence of early ideas on our subject which is contained in the Hindu law. The settled property of a married woman, which cannot be alienated by her husband, is well known to the Hindus under the name of *Stridhan*. It is certainly a remarkable fact that the institution seems to have been developed among the Hindus at a period relatively much earlier than among the Romans. But instead of being matured and improved, as it was in Western society, there is reason to think that in the East, under various influences which may be partially traced, it has gradually been reduced to dimensions and importance far inferior to those which at one time belonged to it.

<header>THE STRIDHAN. LECT. XI.</header>

Up to this point, the doctrine has the concurrence of all the schools of Hindu law, but the compiler of the *Mitakshara* adds a proposition not found elsewhere:

> 'also property which she may have acquired by inheritance, purchase, partition, seizure, or finding, is designated by Manu and the others "woman's property."'

(Mitakshara, xi. 2.) These words, attributed, you see, to the mythical legislator, Manu, have excited the most vehement controversies among later Brahminical commentators, and have caused considerable perplexity to Anglo-Indian Judges, bound as they are to elicit consistent doctrine from the Hindu legal texts.

> 'All the property which a woman may have acquired by inheritance, purchase, partition, seizure, or finding,'

is a comprehensive description of all the forms of property as defined by the modes of acquisition, and, if all this is *Stridhan*, it follows that the ancient Hindu law secured to married women, in theory at least, an even greater degree of proprietary independence than that given to them by the modern English Married Women's Property Act. No doubt there is much difficulty in understanding this. ### LECT. XI. STRIDHAN IN THE ANGLO-INDIAN COURTS.

Among the Aryan sub-races, the Hindus may be as confidently asserted as the Romans to have had their society organized as a collection of patriarchally governed families. If, then, at any early period, the married woman among the Hindus had her property altogether freed from her husband's control, it is not easy to give a reason why the obligations of family despotism were relaxed in this one particular. In fact, there is no clue to the mystery as long as we focus our attention on Hindu law, and no course is open to a judge except to take a stand on the one ancient authority I have quoted or to follow the great majority of modern authorities who reject the doctrine of the *Mitakshara* on this point. The Anglo-Indian courts have now substantially decided that Hindu law (with the possible exception of that current in Western India) limits the *Stridhan* to property given to the woman at her marriage either by her family or by her husband (*'Madras High Court Reports,'* iii. 312). I think, however, that if we extend our examination to other bodies of Aryan custom, we may partly understand the breadth which the *Mitakshara*, one of the most archaic of Hindu compendiums, assigns to the *Stridhan*. ->PRE-HISTORIC ORIGIN OF STRIDHAN.   LECT. XI.<-

Among the <term>Aryan</term> communities as a whole, we find the earliest traces of women's separate property in the widely practiced ancient institution known as the <term>Bride-Price</term>. Part of this price, which was paid by the bridegroom either at the wedding or the day after, went to the bride’s father as compensation for the patriarchal or family authority transferred to the husband. However, another part went to the bride herself and was generally enjoyed by her separately and kept apart from her husband’s property. It further appears that under several <term>Aryan</term> customs, other types of property rights that women slowly acquired were modeled after their rights to their portion of the <term>Bride-Price</term>, likely because it was the only existing form of women's property. The exact extent of the separate ownership allowed to married women under ancient Irish law is still uncertain, but they undoubtedly had some power to manage their own property without their husbands' consent; this was one of the institutions that judges expressly declared illegal at the beginning of the seventeenth century.

The abandonment of all practices which a scholar could show to be relatively modern would dismantle the entire Hindu system. These inquiries, pushed much further, have shown that Hindu laws, both religious and civil, have for centuries been undergoing transformation, development, and, in some respects, corruption at the hands of successive Brahminical commentators, and that no rules have been so consistently changed—as we would say, for the worse—as those affecting the legal status of women.

It will probably be agreed by all who have paid any attention to our subject, that the civilized societies of the West, in steadily expanding the personal and property-related independence of women, and even in granting them political privileges, are only continuing to follow a law of development which they have been obeying for many centuries. # LECT. XI. DEVELOPMENT OF HINDU LAW.

In addition to many other objections which may be raised against the common claim that the legal disabilities of women are merely part of the tyranny of one sex over another, this claim is historically and philosophically worthless—as, indeed, are most propositions concerning classes as large as sexes. What actually existed was the despotism of groups over the members who composed them. What is currently being relaxed is the severity of this despotism. Whether this relaxation is destined to end in complete dissolution—or whether, on the other hand, under the influence of either voluntary agreement or mandatory law, society is destined to take on new forms—are questions which it is not now relevant to explore, even if there were any hope of solving them. All we need to note at present is that the so-called enfranchisement of women is merely one phase of a process that has affected many other classes: the replacement of compact groups of human beings with individual human beings as the basic units of society. Now, it is true that in the legal institutions of the Hindus (I hardly need to say they have had no political institutions for many centuries), the despotism of the family group over the men and women composing it is maintained more completely than in any other society of similar civilization and culture. Yet there is abundant evidence that the emancipation of the individual

# PER STIRPES AND PER CAPITA. LECT. XI.

from the family had progressed somewhat, even before the country came under Western influence through British rule. To provide full proof of this, I would need to guide you through much of the detail of Hindu law. I will mention one indicator of it, because few realize that this specific characteristic serves as a test by which we can distinguish very ancient or undeveloped law from law that is relatively mature and developed.

All beginners in law have heard of the difference between distributing an inheritance *per stirpes* and distributing it *per capita*. Suppose a man has two sons, one of whom has eight children and the other two. The grandfather dies, his two sons having predeceased him, and the grandfather's property must be divided among the grandchildren. If the division is *per stirpes* [by the lineage], the lineages of the two sons will be kept separate: one half of the inheritance will be distributed among the eight grandchildren, and the other half between the two. If the division is *per capita* [by the head], the property will be divided equally among all ten grandchildren, share and share alike. Now, the tendency of mature and developed law is to give a clear preference to distribution *per stirpes*; it is only with distant classes of relatives that it abandons the distinctions between lineages and distributes the property *per capita*. But in this, as in several

<page-num>329</page-num>
<header>LECT. XI. HINDU PRESERVATION OF THE STOCKS.</header>

In other details, very ancient and undeveloped law reverses the ideas of the modern jurist and consistently prefers distribution *per capita*—exactly equal division among all the surviving members of the family. This is apparently based on the principle that, since everyone was impartially subject to a despotism that recognized no ranks, everyone ought to share equally upon the dissolution of the community by the death of its chief. A preference for division *per stirpes*—a careful attention to the preservation of lineages—is, in fact, very strong evidence of the growth of a respect for individual interests within the family, distinct from the interests of the family group as a whole. This is why the importance granted to distribution *per stirpes* shows that a particular system of law has undergone development; and it so happens that this importance is very significant in Hindu law, which is extremely careful regarding the distinction between lineages and maintains them through long lines of succession.

Let us now turn to the causes which, in Hindu law and in the other great Aryan system, Roman law, have respectively led to the separation of the individual from the group. Regarding Roman institutions, we know that among the most powerful dissolving influences were certain philosophical theories of Greek origin, which had a deep effect on the minds of the jurists who guided the development of the law. <header>ROMAN LEGISLATION. LECT. XI.</header>

For one reason alone, the Roman Empire must be placed in a totally different class from the Oriental despotisms, both ancient and modern, and even from the famous Athenian Empire. All of these latter examples were tax-taking empires, which exercised little or no interference in the customs of village communities or tribes. But the Roman Empire, while it was tax-taking, was also a legislating empire. It suppressed local customs and substituted institutions of its own in their place. Through its legislation alone, it caused such a significant interruption in the history of a large part of mankind that it has had no parallel—except for the modern British Empire in India, though the comparison is very imperfect. There is no reason to suppose that philosophical theory had any serious influence on the jurisprudence of the Hindus. I speak with caution on the subject, but I believe that none of the remarkable philosophical theories produced by the genius of that race are founded on a conception of the individual as distinct from the group into which he is born. <header>LECT. XI. EFFECTS OF RELIGION ON LEGAL DOCTRINE.</header>

What, then, was the influence (for some influence there certainly was) which, operating on the minds of the <term>Brahminical jurists</term>, led them to assign to the individual rights distinct from those which would have belonged to him through mere membership in the family group? I believe that it was the influence of Religion. Wherever among any part of <term>Hindu society</term> there prevailed the conviction of responsibility after death—whether that responsibility was to be enforced by direct rewards and punishments, or through the stages of <term>metempsychosis</term>—the conception of the individual, who was to suffer separately and enjoy separately, was necessarily realized with extreme clarity.

The portions of the race strongly affected by religious belief of this kind were exactly those for whom the <term>Brahminical jurists</term> legislated, and at first they probably legislated for these alone. But with the notion of responsibility after death, the notion of expiation was always associated. Building upon this last notion, the Brahminical commentators gradually transformed the whole law until it became an illustration of what <term>Indian lawyers</term> call the doctrine of <term>Spiritual Benefit</term>. Because the condition of the dead could be improved by proper expiatory rites, the property descending or passing to a man came to be regarded by these writers partly as a fund for

<header>RELIGIOUS OBJECTS OF PROPERTY. LECT. XI.</header>

paying the expenses of the ceremony by which the soul of the person from whom the inheritance came could be redeemed from suffering or degradation, and partly as a reward for the proper performance of the sacrifices. There should be nothing to surprise us in the growth of such a doctrine, since it is only distinguished by its logical completeness from one which had a great influence on Western jurisprudence. The interest which the Church claimed from very early times in the movable or personal property of deceased persons is best explained by its teaching that the first and best destination of a dead man’s goods was to purchase masses for his soul; from this view of the proper objects of wealth, the entire testamentary and intestate jurisdiction of the Ecclesiastical Courts appears to have grown. But in India, the law constructed on these principles became extremely unfavorable to the ownership of property by women, apparently because its priestly authors thought that women—due to their physical weakness and their seclusion (which was doubtless regarded as unavoidable)—would have much greater difficulty than men, within a society that was always more or less disturbed, in applying a proper share of the property to the funeral ceremonies of the person who had transmitted it. The reasoning on the subject current even in comparatively ancient times is given this way in the <term>Mitakshara</term>: "The wealth of a

In India, this logic led to laws restricting women's inheritance due to perceived difficulties in their performing these essential funeral duties.</summary>

The compiler of the <term>Mitakshara</term>, who preserved the liberal rule regarding <term>Stridhan</term> that I mentioned earlier, opposes this doctrine. He does this, however, not by asserting that women are capable of performing sacrifices, but by denying that all property is intended for religious purposes, and by pointing out that certain actions a female owner can perform are of a semi-religious nature; for example, she may dig tanks. (<term>Mitakshara</term>, ii. 1, 22, 23, 24.) Setting him aside, the Brahminical commentators who follow one another in the Hindu legal schools show a clearly growing desire to link all property with the performance of sacrificial duties, and the reluctance to place property in the hands of women is somehow connected to this desire.

On the whole, successive generations of Hindu lawyers show an increasing hostility toward the institution of <term>Stridhan</term>, not by abolishing it, but by limiting as much as possible the circumstances under which it can occur. Minute distinctions are drawn between the various ways in which property may pass to a woman, and the conditions under which such property may become <term>Stridhan</term> are made rare and exceptional. Not only does the woman have exceptionally full power in dealing with <term>Stridhan</term>—not only is the husband barred from interfering with it, except in cases of extreme distress—but when the female owner dies, there is a special order of succession to her property, which is clearly intended to give a preference, whenever it is possible, to female relatives over males.

<header>LECT. XI. SUTTEE IN BENGAL.</header>

If there are male children, they succeed at once; but if there are none, the widow inherits for her life before the collateral relatives. At the present moment, as marriages among the upper classes of Hindus are very commonly infertile, a considerable portion of the land in the wealthiest Indian province is in the hands of childless widows as tenants for life. But it was exactly in Bengal Proper that the English, upon entering India, found <term>Suttee</term>, or widow-burning, to be not merely an occasional practice, but a constant and almost universal one among the wealthier classes; and, as a rule, it was only the childless widow, and never the widow with minor children, who burned herself on her husband's funeral pyre. There is no question that there was the closest connection between the law and the religious custom, and the widow was made to sacrifice herself so that her life tenancy might be removed. The anxiety of her family for the rite to be performed, which seemed so striking to the first English observers of the practice, was, in fact, explained by the crudest motives; but the <term>Brahmins</term> who urged her to the sacrifice were undoubtedly influenced by a purely professional dislike of her enjoyment of property. The ancient rule of the civil law, which made her a life tenant, could not be eliminated, but it was combated by the modern institution

<header>ROMAN COMPULSORY DOTATION. LECT. XI.</header>

which made it her duty to devote herself to a frightful death.

If the <term>Stridhan</term> of the Hindus is a form of married women’s separate property, which has been disliked and distorted by the professional classes who had the power to change it, the institution which was first the *dos* of the Romans, and is now the *dot* of Continental Europe, has received a remarkable amount of artificial encouragement. I have tried to describe to you how it originated, but I have yet to state that it entered into one of the most famous social experiments of the Roman Empire. A well-known law of the Emperor Augustus—celebrated by Horace in an official ode as the prince’s greatest legislative achievement—aimed to encourage and regulate marriage and to impose penalties on celibacy. Among the main provisions of this ‘Lex Julia et Papia Poppæa’—to give its full title—was a clause requiring wealthy parents to provide portions, or *dotes*, for their marriageable daughters. This provision of a statute, which profoundly affected Roman law in many ways, must have met with general approval, for at a later date we find the same principle applied to the *donatio propter nuptias*, or a settlement on the married couple from the husband’s side. In the mature Roman law, therefore, as strange as it may seem to us, parents were under a legal obligation to make settlements on their children.

<summary>The author compares Hindu property rights with Roman dowry laws, noting that while the former were suppressed, Roman laws under Augustus actually mandated that parents provide marriage settlements. This legal requirement for dowries eventually expanded to include settlements from the husband's side as well.</summary>

<page-num>337</page-num>
<header>LECT. XI. THE CHURCH AND THE INTERESTS OF WIDOWS.</header>

It has been somewhat fashionable to speak of these experiments of the Roman Emperors regarding public morality as if they totally failed—I suppose, based on some idea that the failure added to the credit of the moral regeneration brought about by Christianity. But, as a matter of fact, the Christian Church granted few civil benefits of greater importance to several generations of mankind than by keeping alive the traditions of Roman legislation regarding settled property, and by vigorously exerting itself to extend and apply the principles of these disciplinary laws. There can be no serious question that, in its final result, the collapse of the Roman Empire was very unfavorable to the personal and proprietary liberty of women. I deliberately say, "in its final result," in order to avoid a learned controversy regarding their position under purely Teutonic customs. It is very possible that the last stages of the process, which is difficult to call anything other than feudalization, were more unfavorable to women than the earlier changes, which were exclusively due to the introduction of Germanic usage; but, at any rate, the status of women under the new system, when fully organized, was worse than it was under Roman law, and would have been much worse if not for the efforts of the Church. One lasting monument of these efforts we have constantly before us in the husband’s promise in the Marriage service: "With all my worldly goods, I

The words have, indeed, been used occasionally in English legal treatises as the subject of a detailed study on the distinction between the Roman *dos*, to which they are supposed to refer, and the *doarium*, which is the "dower" of lands known to English law. The fact is, however, that the tradition the Church was carrying on was the general tradition of the Roman *dos*. The practical object was to secure for the wife a provision that the husband could not recklessly deprive her of, and which would remain hers after his death. The systems of customary law that were established across Europe were, in all matters of basic principle, under ecclesiastical influences; however, the particular applications of a principle, once accepted, were extremely varied. The dower of lands in English law—of which hardly a shadow remains today, but under which a wife surviving her husband took a third of the rents and profits of his estates for life—belonged to a category of institutions widespread across Western Europe. These were very similar in general character and often designated as *doarium*, but they differed considerably in detail. They undoubtedly originated in the efforts of the Church to revive the Roman institution of the compulsory *dos*, which, in this sense, produced the *doarium*, even

The assertion, then, that there is a relation between civilization and the proprietary capacities of women is only a form of the truth that every one of those conquests, the sum of which we call civilization, is the result of curbing some of the strongest, because they are the primary, impulses of human nature. If we were asked why the two societies with which we have been concerned—the Hindus on the one hand, and the Romans and all the races to which they have bequeathed their institutions on the other—have had so widely different a history, no reply can be very confidently given, so difficult is it, among the vast variety of influences acting on great assemblages of men, to single out any one or any definite number of them, and to be sure that these have operated more powerfully than the rest. <header>JURISPRUDENCE IN ENGLAND. LECT. XII.</header>

# LECTURE XII.

## SOVEREIGNTY.

THE historical theories commonly accepted among English lawyers have done so much harm not only to the study of law but to the study of history, that an account of the origin and growth of our legal system, founded on the examination of new materials and the re-examination of old ones, is perhaps the most urgently needed of all additions to English knowledge. But next to a new history of law, what we most require is a new philosophy of law. If our country ever gives birth to such a philosophy, we shall probably owe it to two advantages. The first of them is our possession of a legal system which for many purposes may be considered indigenous. Our national pride, which has sometimes retarded or limited our advance in juridical inquiry, has kept our law singularly pure from mixture with the stream of legal rules flowing from the great fountain of the Roman <term>Corpus Juris</term>, and thus, when we place it in juxtaposition with any other European legal system,

<header>LECT. XII. THE ANALYTICAL JURISTS.</header>

I believe the second advantage consists in the growing familiarity of Englishmen with the investigations of the so-called <term>Analytical Jurists</term>, of whom the most significant are Jeremy Bentham and John Austin. We have a monopoly on this advantage. Bentham seems to be known in France and Germany exclusively as the author of an unpopular system of morals. Austin is apparently not known at all. Yet the world is indebted to Bentham, and even more so to Austin, for the only existing attempt to construct a system of jurisprudence through a strict scientific process and to found it, not on *a priori* assumption, but on the observation, comparison, and analysis of various legal concepts. There is no need to accept all the conclusions of these great writers with unquestioning deference, but it is extremely necessary to know what those conclusions are. They are indispensable, if for no other reason than to clear one's mind.

An important distinction between Bentham and Austin is not recognized as often as it should be. Bentham is primarily a writer on legislation. Austin is primarily a writer on jurisprudence. Bentham is chiefly concerned with law as it might be

<header>->BENTHAM AND AUSTIN. LECT. XII.<-</header>

and ought to be. Austin is chiefly concerned with law as it is. Each occasionally trespasses on the domain of the other. Unless Bentham had written the treatise called the *Fragment on Government*, Austin’s *Province of Jurisprudence Determined*, which sets forth the basis of his system, would probably never have been composed. On the other hand, Austin, in his unique discussion of the theory of utility as an index to the Law of God, has entered into an investigation of the kind followed by Bentham. Still, the description I have given of their objectives is sufficiently correct as a general description, and those objectives are widely different. Bentham aims at the improvement of the law to be achieved by the application of the principles now indissolubly associated with his name. Almost all of his more important suggestions have been adopted by the English Legislature, but the process of grafting onto the law what each successive generation considers to be improvements is in itself of indefinite duration, and may continue, and possibly will continue, as long as the human race lasts. Austin’s undertaking is more modest. It would be completed if a Code were produced that was perfectly logical in its order of arrangement and perfectly clear in its statement of rules. Jurisprudence, the science of positive law, is sometimes spoken of nowadays as if it would bring the substance of the law into a state of indefinite perfection. It would

# LECT. XII. AUSTIN'S PROVINCE OF JURISPRUDENCE.

would doubtless, if it were carried far, lead indirectly to great legal reforms by clearing up obscurities and removing delusions. However, the investigation of the principles on which the direct improvement of substantive legal rules should be conducted belongs not to the legal theorist (the theorist on jurisprudence) but to the legislative theorist.

The portion of Austin’s *Lectures* which establishes the basis of his system, and which was published several years ago as *The Province of Jurisprudence Determined*, has long been one of the advanced textbooks in this University; and, taken together with the other lectures more recently published (though unfortunately in a fragmentary form), it must always, or for a long time to come, be the mainstay of the studies pursued in this department. While fully acknowledging the value of the book, I find it impossible not to recognize the scale of the difficulties it presents to the beginner. Some difficulties originate in peculiarities of style, which seem to be attributable to the constant intellectual exchange the writer maintained with his predecessors, Bentham and Hobbes. I find these practically less serious than difficulties of another sort, which arise from the mental repulsion created by the way Austin’s analysis presents the concepts of law, right, and duty. Of course, to the extent that this distaste is caused by unpalatable

A very frequent effect of forcing a system or subject that is for some reason or other repugnant to them upon students of active minds and industrious habits is to make them regard it as mere dogma—as something resting on the personal authority of the writer with whose name it happens to be associated. Now, nothing could be more unfortunate for the philosophy of law than for the system of *The Province of Jurisprudence Determined* to come to be regarded simply as "Austin's system"—as standing alongside Blackstone's, Hegel's, or any other system—as interchangeable with or equivalent to them. For, once certain assumptions or postulates have been made, I am fully convinced that the great majority of Austin's positions follow naturally and by ordinary logical processes. This Lecture is an attempt to show what several of these assumptions or postulates are; in the lecture that follows it, I try to show how these assumptions are affected by some conclusions we reached in previous Lectures during our investigation of the early history of society. (*See above*, Lectures 1 to 11.) I think it is best for my purpose to begin by drawing attention to the definition of Sovereignty. Beyond all doubt, this is the logical order for the discussion undertaken by Austin, and I find it difficult to understand—except on one hypothesis—why, abandoning the arrangement of Hobbes, he began the discussion of this part of his subject with the analysis of Law, Right, and Duty, and ended it with an account of Sovereignty, which it seems to me should have come first. I imagine, however, that Blackstone influenced him—as he did Bentham—by repulsion, so to speak. <header>AUSTIN'S DEFINITIONS. LECT. XII.</header>

The latter seems to me to have thought that the propositions he disputed would be most effectively dealt with if they were contradicted in the order given to them by their author. However that may be, the branch of my subject that I shall first enter may be described as an inquiry into the probable way Austin's analysis would have been affected if he had begun in his first lecture with the examination of the nature of Sovereignty. He placed this examination in the sixth lecture, which, as far as the "Province of Jurisprudence Determined" is concerned, is the last of his lectures.

I believe I may assume that most of my listeners are familiar with the general nature of the investigation carried out by Austin in the treatise I have mentioned; however, since his definitions are not easily remembered in their complete form, I will provide his descriptions of an Independent Political Society and of Sovereignty—the two concepts being interdependent and inseparable from each other.

> "If," he says, "a determinate human superior, not in the habit of obedience to a similar superior, receives habitual obedience from the bulk of a given society, that determinate superior is Sovereign in that society, and the society, including the superior, is a political and independent society."

<summary>The author explains that Austin's structural choices were likely a reaction to the authors he was refuting and introduces Austin's specific definitions of Sovereignty and Independent Political Society.</summary>

--- Page 356 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of John Austin's legal theories, specifically focusing on his definitions of sovereignty and the relationship between a superior authority and the members of a political society.</meta>

<page-num>349</page-num>
<header>LECT. XII.   INDEPENDENT POLITICAL COMMUNITIES.</header>

He then proceeds: "To that determinate superior, the other members of the society are subject; or on that determinate superior, the other members of the society are dependent. The position of its other members toward that determinate superior is a state of subjection or a state of dependence. The mutual relation that exists between that superior and them may be called the relation of Sovereign and Subject, or the relation of Sovereignty and Subjection."

I may perhaps avoid the necessity for part of the elaboration and explanation of these definitions contained in the chapter in which they occur if I state Austin's doctrine of Sovereignty in another way—more popularly, though without, I think, any substantial inaccuracy. It is as follows: There is, in every independent political community—that is, in every political community not in the habit of obedience to a superior above itself—some single person or some combination of persons which has the power of compelling the other members of the community to do exactly as it pleases. This single person or group—this individual or this <term>collegiate Sovereign</term> (to use Austin's phrase)—may be found in every independent political community as certainly as the center of gravity in a mass of matter. If the community is violently or voluntarily divided into a number of separate fragments, then, as soon as

<page-num>351</page-num>
->LECT. XII. THE SOVEREIGN DETERMINATE.<-

...or it is discovered, he may not fit any recognized designation, but where there is an independent political society not in a state of anarchy, the Sovereign is certainly there. The question of determining his character is, you will understand, always a question of fact. It is never a question of law or morals. Whoever, when a particular person or group is asserted to constitute the Sovereign in a given community, denies the proposition on the ground that such Sovereignty is a usurpation or a violation of constitutional principle, has completely missed Austin’s point of view.

The definitions which I read from the Sixth Lecture furnish Austin’s tests for discovering the seat of Sovereignty in independent states. I will again refer to a few of the most important of them, though very briefly.

First, the Sovereign is a *determinate* human superior. He is not necessarily a single person; in the modern Western world, he is very rarely so; but he must possess enough attributes of a single person to be determinate. If he is not a single person, he must be a number of persons capable of acting in a corporate or collegiate capacity. This part of the definition is absolutely necessary, since the Sovereign must effect his exertions of power—must issue his orders—by a definite exercise of his will. The possession of physical power, which is one characteristic

<summary>This page explains that identifying a sovereign is a matter of factual observation rather than legal or moral legitimacy. It defines Austin's sovereign as a "determinate human superior" that must be capable of acting with a single, corporate will.</summary>
<keywords>John Austin, Sovereignty, determinate human superior, political society, constitutional principle, legal theory, corporate capacity</keywords>

--- Page 359 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of John Austin’s definition of sovereignty, focusing on the requirement that a sovereign must be a "determinate" human superior and the necessity of obedience from the majority of a society.</meta>

<page-num>352</page-num>
<header>THE OBEDIENCE OF SUBJECTS. LECT. XII</header>

Sovereignty has, as a matter of historical fact, repeatedly been for a time in the hands of a number of persons who are *not* determinate—not so connected together as to be capable of exercising a single will. Austin would call such a state of things anarchy, even though it might not have all the usually recognized symptoms of a revolutionary interval. At the same time, the limitation of sovereignty to determinate groups, when the sovereign is not an individual, is extremely important; it qualifies the notion of sovereignty by making it subject to the various methods by which an exercise of will is drawn from a corporate body. As familiar as the practice of taking a majority opinion as the opinion of an entire group is to us, and as natural as it seems, nothing can be more artificial.

Again, the bulk of society must obey the superior who is to be called the sovereign. Not the whole of society—for in that case sovereignty would be impossible—but the bulk, the large majority, must obey. After the House of Hanover took the British throne, a certain number of Jacobites and a considerable portion of the Scottish Highlanders habitually disobeyed or disregarded the commands of the British Crown and Parliament, but the bulk of the nation, including no doubt the bulk of the Jacobites themselves, gave these commands a practical obedience. According to Austin’s principles, there—

It also clarifies that sovereignty exists when the bulk of society—rather than every single individual—obeys the superior power.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of John Austin’s definition of sovereignty, focusing on the requirement that the majority of a society must give habitual obedience to a determinate superior.</meta>

<page-num>353</page-num>
<header>LECT. XII. HABITUAL OBEDIENCE.</header>

Therefore, there is not the slightest ground for questioning the sovereignty of George I and II and of the Parliaments elected at their summons. The Jacobite view—that the Hanoverian kings were exclusively sovereign in Hanover—would be immediately cast aside by Austin, as it does not raise that question of fact which is the only point of dispute under his system.

Next, the sovereign must receive habitual obedience from the bulk of the community. In European societies professing the Roman Catholic faith, the great majority of the population receives a variety of directions on points of personal conduct, either indirectly or directly, from the See of Rome. But, compared with the number of times it submits itself to the laws of the country it inhabits, its obedience to these external commands is only occasional and not habitual. At the same time, a vague appreciation of the principles brought to light by Austin may be detected in several famous ecclesiastical controversies; these sometimes tend to become disputes over whether the obedience actually paid to the See of Rome is or is not frequent enough to fall under the description of "habitual."

A further characteristic of sovereignty is immunity from the control of every other human superior. This limitation is obviously necessary; otherwise, the Governor-General of India in Council would be sovereign, and indeed would exhibit a closer correspondence—

<header>HOBBES. LECT. XII.</header>

pondence with the more salient features of Sovereignty than almost any other potentate on the face of the globe.

Those who have observed the slowness with which definite concepts are developed in the fields of history and politics will be prepared to hear that this entire view of the nature of Sovereignty is older than Austin's work. But, so far as my own knowledge extends, I do not think that any material portion of it is older than Hobbes. On the other hand, in the *Leviathan* of Hobbes and in the Chapter *De Cive* in his treatise first published in Latin, called the *Elementa Philosophiæ*, the analysis of Government and Society and the determination of Sovereignty are so nearly completed that little could be added to them by Bentham and Austin. The originality of these later writers, and more particularly of Austin, lies in their much fuller examination of the concepts dependent on the notion of Sovereignty—positive law, positive duty, sanction, and right—in setting forth the relations of these concepts to others that superficially resemble them, in combating objections to the theory by which the entire group of notions are connected together, and in applying this theory to certain complex states of fact which had arisen since Hobbes wrote. There is, however, one great difference between Hobbes and his latest successor. The process of Hobbes was scientific, but

<header>LECT. XII. POLITICAL OPINIONS OF HOBBES.</header>

When, with a sharpness of intuition and clarity of statement that have never been rivaled, he has established a case for the universal theoretical existence of Sovereignty, it becomes clear that he has, at the very least, a strong preference for monarchies over aristocracies and democracies, or (to use the terminology of the school which he founded) for individual over corporate Sovereignty. Those of his intellectual followers who would have rejected his politics have often asserted that he has been misunderstood, and no doubt some superficial readers have supposed that he was pointing toward despotism when he was really referring to the essentially unqualified power of the Sovereign, whatever the form of the Sovereignty. But I do not think it can in fairness be denied that his strong dislike of the Long Parliament and of the English Common Law, as the great instrument of resistance to the Stuart Kings, has occasionally influenced the language which he uses in examining the nature of Sovereignty, Law, and Anarchy; nor is it a matter for surprise that he should have been charged during his life with having devised his system with the secret intention of making his peace with the Protector, though the accusation itself is sufficiently refuted by dates. But Austin's objective is strictly scientific. If he has fallen into errors, he has been led into them by his philosophy, and his language

<header>HOBBES ON THE ORIGIN OF SOCIETY. LECT. XII</header>

scarcely ever reveals the color of his political opinions.

Another significant difference is this. It is well known that Hobbes speculated on the origin of Government and Sovereignty. This is the one fact that some people seem to have learned about him, and they appear to think his philosophy is sufficiently condemned by it. But Austin barely enters into this inquiry; indeed, he occasionally, though perhaps unintentionally, uses language that almost seems to imply that Sovereignty and the concepts dependent on it have an *a priori* existence. Now, on this matter, I personally hold that Hobbes's method was correct. It is true that nothing could be more worthless in itself than Hobbes’s conjectural account of the origin of society and government. He asserts that mankind was originally in a state of war. They then made a compact under which every man abandoned his powers of aggression, and the result was Sovereignty, and through Sovereignty came law, peace, and order. The theory is open to every sort of objection. There is no evidence for any stage of this supposed history, and the little we know of primitive man contradicts it. The universal disorder of the race in its infancy may be true regarding the conflicts of tribe with tribe and of family with family; but it is not true of the relations of individual man with individual man, whom we, on the contrary, first discern

<header>LECT. XII. ASSERTIONS OF ANALYTICAL JURISTS.</header>

But I still think that Hobbes acted correctly in addressing himself to the problem, even though he did little to solve it. The duty of inquiring—if not how Sovereignty arose, then at least through what stages it has passed—is, in my judgment, indispensable. It is only in this way that we can satisfy ourselves as to how well the results of the Austinian analysis align with the facts.

There is, in truth, nothing more important for the student of jurisprudence than to carefully consider how far the observed facts of human nature and society support the assertions which are made, or seem to be made, about Sovereignty by the Analytical Jurists. To begin with, these assertions must be separated from one another. The first of them is that, in every independent community of men, there resides the power of acting with irresistible force on the individual members of that community. This may be accepted as an actual fact. The next assertion is that, in every independent *political community*—that is, in every independent community neither in a state of nature on the one hand nor in a state of anarchy on the other—the power of using or directing the irresistible force stored up in the society resides in some person or combination of persons who belong to the society themselves. The truth of this assertion is strongly suggested by a certain class of facts, particularly by the political facts of the Western and Modern world; but all the relevant facts, it must be remembered, have not been fully observed. The whole world—of which theorists on human nature are extremely likely to forget considerably more than half—and the entire history of the whole world, would have to be examined before we could be quite certain of the facts. If this were done, it may be that a great number of the facts would not so strongly suggest this conclusion; or, as I myself think, the assertion which we are considering would not be shown to be false so much as to be only verbally true, and therefore without the value which it possesses in societies of the type to which our own belongs. <page-num>359</page-num>
<header>LECT. XII. SOVEREIGNTY AN ABSTRACTION.</header>

Analytical Jurists cannot be charged with making—though some of their disciples come very close to suggesting—that the Sovereign person or group actually wields the stored-up force of society through an uncontrolled exercise of will, is certainly never in accordance with fact. A despot with a disordered mind is the only conceivable example of such Sovereignty. The vast mass of influences, which we may call "moral" for brevity's sake, perpetually shapes, limits, or forbids the actual direction of the forces of society by its Sovereign. This is the point which, above all others, is practically most necessary for the student to bear in mind, because it does the most to show what the Austinian view of Sovereignty really is—that it is the result of Abstraction. It is arrived at by throwing aside all the characteristics and attributes of Government and Society except one, and by connecting all forms of political superiority together through their common possession of force. The elements neglected in the process are always important, and sometimes of extreme importance, for they consist of all the influences controlling human action except force directly applied or directly perceived; but the operation of throwing them aside for purposes of classification is, I need hardly say, perfectly legitimate philosophically, and is only the application of a method in ordinary scientific use.

To put the same thing in another way, that which

<summary>The author explains that the Austinian theory of Sovereignty is an abstraction that isolates the element of force from other social and moral influences. While these ignored moral influences are important in reality, setting them aside for the sake of scientific classification is a legitimate philosophical method.</summary>
<keywords>Analytical Jurists, Sovereignty, Abstraction, Austinian view, Government, Society, force, moral influences, political science methodology</keywords>

--- Page 367 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 359: "To put the same thing in another way, that which..."</meta>

<page-num>360</page-num>
<header>ELIMINATION OF HISTORICAL INFLUENCES. LECT. XII.</header>

we reject in the process of abstraction by which the conception of Sovereignty is reached is the entire history of each community. First of all, it is the history—the whole historical background of each society—that has determined where, and in which person or group, the power of using social force resides. The theory of Sovereignty ignores the way in which this result was achieved, and thus it is able to group together the coercive authority of the great King of Persia, the Athenian Demos, the later Roman Emperors, the Russian Czar, and the Crown and Parliament of Great Britain. Next, it is history—the entire mass of historical background—which in each community determines how the Sovereign shall exercise or refrain from exercising their irresistible coercive power. All that makes this up—the whole enormous collection of opinions, sentiments, beliefs, superstitions, and prejudices; of ideas of all kinds, inherited and acquired, some produced by institutions and some by the nature of humanity—is rejected by the Analytical Jurists. And thus it is that, so far as the restrictions contained in their definition of Sovereignty are concerned, the Queen and Parliament of our own country might order all weak children to be put to death or establish a system of *lettres de cachet* <note>Orders directly from a monarch, often used to imprison individuals without trial.</note>.

The procedure of the Analytical Jurists is closely

<header>LECT. XII. ABSTRACT SCIENCES.</header>
<page-num>361</page-num>

analogous to the method followed in mathematics and political economy. It is strictly philosophical, but the practical value of all sciences founded on abstractions depends on the relative importance of the elements discarded and the elements retained during the process of abstraction. Measured by this test, mathematical science is of much greater value than political economy, and both are more valuable than jurisprudence as conceived by the writers I am criticizing. Similarly, the misconceptions that arise from the Austinian analysis are very similar to those that might be imagined as troubling the student of mixed mathematics <note>An older term for applied mathematics, such as mechanics or optics, which apply mathematical principles to physical objects.</note>, and which actually do trouble the student of political economy. Just as it is possible to forget the existence of friction in nature and the reality of social motives other than the desire to grow rich, the student of Austin may be tempted to forget that there is more to actual Sovereignty than just force. They may also forget that there is more in laws—which are the commands of sovereigns—than can be understood by merely considering them as regulated force. I am not prepared to deny that Austin occasionally, and Hobbes frequently, express themselves as if their system were not restricted throughout by the limitation that lies at its foundation. In fact, all the great masters of Abstraction are occasionally led into speaking or writing as if the materials discarded in the purely mental process were actually worthless.

<summary>The author argues that while the abstract methods of John Austin and Thomas Hobbes are philosophically sound, they risk being impractical if the student forgets the real-world factors—like social motives and morality—that the theory intentionally ignores. He compares this to a physicist forgetting about friction or an economist assuming people only care about wealth.</summary>

<header>LAW DEPENDENT ON SOVEREIGNTY. LECT. XII.</header>
<page-num>362</page-num>

However, once it is recognized that in Austin's system the definition of Sovereignty must precede the definition of Law; when it is understood that the Austinian concept of Sovereignty was reached by mentally grouping all forms of government together and stripping them of every attribute except coercive force; and when it is steadily kept in mind that deductions from an abstract principle are never, by their very nature, perfectly reflected in actual facts; then, it seems to me, the main difficulties felt by students of Austin disappear. Not only that, but some of the claims he makes—which beginners are most likely to struggle with—begin to seem like self-evident propositions. I expect you are familiar enough with his treatise for me to mention some of these propositions without the detailed explanations needed for a perfectly accurate statement. Jurisprudence is the science of Positive Law. Positive Laws are commands, addressed by Sovereigns to their Subjects, imposing a Duty (or a condition of being obliged or under obligation) on those subjects, and threatening a Sanction, or penalty, if the command is disobeyed. A Right is the faculty or power granted by the Sovereign to certain members of the community to invoke the sanction against a fellow subject who violates a Duty. Now, all these concepts of Law, Right,

<summary>The author explains that Austin’s legal definitions become clear once it is understood that they are based on an abstraction of sovereignty as pure coercive force. He defines Austin's core concepts of positive law, duties, and rights as a system of sovereign commands backed by penalties.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of John Austin’s legal theory, specifically how the concepts of law, right, and duty flow from his particular definition of sovereignty as coercive force.</meta>

<header>LECT. XII. CUSTOMARY LAW.</header>
<page-num>363</page-num>

Duty and Punishment depend upon the primary concept of Sovereignty, just as the lower links of a hanging chain depend upon the highest link. But Sovereignty, for the purposes of Austin’s system, possesses no attribute other than force; consequently, the view taken here of ‘law,’ ‘obligation,’ and ‘right’ is a view of them regarded exclusively as products of coercive force. The ‘sanction’ thus becomes the primary and most important part of this series of concepts, influencing the character of all the others. Probably no one has ever found it difficult to accept that laws have the character Austin attributes to them, as long as those laws have originated from formal Legislatures. However, many people—including some very brilliant thinkers—have resisted the position that the vast body of legal rules which have never been prescribed by the state body conventionally known as the Legislature are actually commands of the Sovereign. The customary law of all countries that have not included their laws in Codes, and especially the English <term>Common law</term>, has often been claimed to have an origin independent of the Sovereign. Various theories have been proposed on this subject, which Austin rejects as mysterious and unintelligible. The way in which Hobbes and he incorporate such bodies of rules as the <term>Common law</term> into their system is by insisting on a maxim that is of vital importance to it: ‘Whatever the Sovereign permits, he commands.’

<summary>The author explains that in Austin’s system, all legal concepts stem from sovereignty viewed as coercive force, making the "sanction" the central element. Austin and Hobbes account for customary law and Common Law by arguing that any rule the Sovereign allows to exist is effectively a command.</summary>
<keywords>Sovereignty, John Austin, Thomas Hobbes, Customary Law, English Common Law, Sanctions, Coercive Force, Legislature</keywords>

--- Page 371 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of John Austin’s legal theory, specifically addressing how customs and the Common Law are integrated into his definition of sovereignty as a command backed by force.</meta>

-># PERMISSION AND COMMAND. LECT. XII.<-
<page-num>364</page-num>

Until customs are enforced by courts of justice, they are merely "positive morality"—rules enforced by public opinion. However, as soon as courts of justice enforce them, they become commands of the Sovereign, conveyed through the judges who are his delegates or deputies. A better response to this theory than Austin might have admitted is that it is founded on a mere figure of speech, and that it assumes courts of justice act in a way and from motives of which they are completely unaware. But when it is clearly understood that, in this system, there are no associations with the Sovereign except for force or power, the position that what Sovereigns permit they command becomes more easily understood. They command because—assuming they possess uncontrollable force—they could make changes without limit at any moment. The <term>Common law</term> consists of their commands because they can repeal, alter, or restate it as they please. The theory is perfectly defensible as a theory, but its practical value and the degree to which it approaches the truth differ greatly in different eras and countries. There have been independent political communities—and indeed, some would still be found if the world were thoroughly searched—in which the Sovereign, though possessing irresistible power, never dreams of making changes, and believes the persons or groups by whom laws are declared and applied to be just as much

<summary>The author explains Austin's view that customs become law only when enforced by the Sovereign's courts, based on the principle that permission equals command. He critiques this as a linguistic artifice that may not reflect the reality of societies where Sovereigns do not seek to innovate or change established laws.</summary>
<keywords>John Austin, Sovereign, positive morality, Common Law, customary law, legal theory, command, innovation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the critique of John Austin's theory of sovereignty, specifically addressing how the theory can be forced to fit historical examples only by straining the meaning of language.</meta>

<header>### LECT. XII. LIMITATIONS OF AUSTINIAN THEORY.</header>

<page-num>385</page-num>

part of the necessary constitution of society as he is himself. There have again been independent political societies in which the Sovereign has enjoyed irresistible coercive power and has carried innovation to the farthest point, but in which every single association connected with law would be violated if laws were regarded as his commands. The Tyrant of a Greek city often satisfied every one of Austin's tests of Sovereignty; yet it was part of the accepted definition of a Tyrant that "he subverted the laws." Let it be understood that it is quite possible to make the theory fit such cases, but the process is a mere straining of language. It is carried on by taking words and propositions altogether out of the sphere of the ideas habitually associated with them.

Before proceeding to speak at some length in my next Lecture regarding these historical limitations on the practical value of Austin's theories, let me repeat my opinion: if the method of discussion which seems correct to me had been followed in his treatise, and if the examination of Sovereignty had preceded the examination of the conceptions dependent on it, a considerable number of the statements which he has made respecting these latter conceptions would have appeared not merely innocent but self-evident. <header>AUSTIN ON MORALITY. LECT. XII.</header>

<page-num>388</page-num>

The one doctrine of this school of jurists which is offensive to lawyers would lose its air of paradox if an assumption were made which, in itself theoretically unobjectionable, clearly approaches practical truth as the course of history proceeds—the assumption that what the Sovereign might change, but does not change, he commands. The same arrangement would have a further advantage, as it seems to me, through the modifications it would require in Austin’s manner of discussing Morality, though the subject is not one which can be treated completely here. The position at which many readers have stumbled—I do not pretend to do more than state it in popular language—is that the sanction of moral rules, as such, is the disapproval which one’s fellow men show at their violation. It is sometimes interpreted to mean that the only motive for obeying moral rules is the fear of such disapproval. Such a construction of Austin’s language is an entire misunderstanding of his meaning; but, if the order of discussion which I advocate had been followed, I do not think it could ever possibly occur to any mind. Let us suppose Austin to have completed his analysis of Sovereignty and of the concepts immediately dependent on it: law, legal right, and legal obligation. He would then have to examine that great mass of rules which men in fact obey, which have some of the characteristics of laws,

<header>LECT. XII. THEORY OF MORALITY.</header>
<page-num>367</page-num>

but which are not (as such) imposed by sovereigns on subjects, and which are not (as such) enforced by the sanction supplied by sovereign power. It would be, of course, the duty of the philosophical jurist to examine these rules, because sovereigns—being by his hypothesis *human* superiors—are, as human beings, subject to them. Austin has, in fact, examined them from this point of view in some of his most interesting passages. While insisting that sovereignty is, by the nature of the case, incapable of legal limitation, he fully admits that sovereigns are restrained from issuing some commands and compelled to issue others by rules which, though they are not laws, are highly compelling. The Crown and Parliament of Great Britain are in his view sovereign—a sovereign aristocracy, as he would call it—but, though this aristocracy could, for the sake of argument, do anything it pleased, it would defy all experience to assert that it actually does so. That great body of rules which is embodied in constitutional principles keeps it from doing some things; that great body of rules which in ordinary usage are called moral keeps it from doing others. What common characteristics does this collection of rules have, which operates on men and on sovereigns just like other men? Austin, as you know, names it ‘positive morality,’ and says that its sanction is opinion, or the disapproval of the majority of the community following its violation—

<header>AUSTIN'S ETHICAL CREED. LECT. XII.</header>
<page-num>388</page-num>

Properly understood, this last point is an obviously true proposition; what is meant is that public disapproval is the one <term>sanction</term> which all these rules have in common. The rule which keeps the Crown and Parliament from declaring murder legal, and the rule which keeps them from allowing the Queen to govern without Ministers, are connected together through the penalty that follows a breach of them: the strong disapproval of a majority of the English people. The fact that they have a <term>sanction</term> of some kind is what principally connects both rules with laws proper. But, although fear of public opinion may be a motive for obeying both rules, it does not follow at all that the sole motive for obeying both rules is fear of public opinion. Most people would admit this fear is the chief, if not the exclusive, motive for obeying <term>constitutional rules</term>; however, such an admission does not involve any necessary assertion regarding the complete <term>sanction</term> of <term>moral rules</term>. The truth is that Austin's system is consistent with *any* ethical theory; if Austin seems to claim otherwise, I think the reason should be found in his firm conviction in the truth of his own ethical creed, which, I need not say, was *<term>Utilitarianism</term>* in its earlier form. Indeed, I do not intend for a moment to deny that a careful study of Austin would probably modify a student's view of morals. The discussion of ethics, like many

<header>LECT XIII. FORCE AND ORDER.</header>
<page-num>371</page-num>

# LECTURE XIII.

## SOVEREIGNTY AND EMPIRE.

THE word ‘law’ has come down to us in close association with two notions: the notion of *order* and the notion of *force*. This association is of considerable antiquity and is revealed by a wide variety of languages. The problem has repeatedly suggested itself: which of the two notions thus linked together is entitled to precedence over the other, and which of them is first in terms of mental conception?

The answer, before the Analytical Jurists wrote, would on the whole have been that ‘law,’ before all things, implied order. “Law, in its most general and comprehensive sense, signifies a rule of action, and is applied indiscriminately to all kinds of action, whether animate or inanimate, rational or irrational. Thus we say, the laws of motion, of gravitation, of optics or mechanics, as well as the laws of nature and of nations.” With these words Blackstone begins that Chapter on “the Nature of Laws in General,” which may almost be said to have made Bentham and

It references Blackstone’s broad definition of law as a general rule of action applicable to both the natural world and human society.</summary>

<header>PRIORITY OF ORDER OR FORCE. LECT. XIII</header>
<page-num>372</page-num>

The <term>Analytical Jurists</term>, on the other hand, unhesitatingly assert that the concept of force takes priority over the concept of order. They say that a true law—the command of an irresistible <term>Sovereign</term>—requires a class of acts or a class of omissions from either an individual subject or a number of subjects, all of whom are placed equally and without distinction under a legal obligation by that command. The characteristic that actually applies to most true laws—binding a number of persons, chosen without distinction, to a number of acts or omissions defined generally—has caused the term ‘law’ to be extended metaphorically to all uniform patterns or constant sequences in the physical world, in the workings of the mind, or in the actions of mankind. Law, when used in such expressions as the <term>Law of Gravity</term>, the <term>Law of Mental Association</term>, or the <term>Law of Rent</term>, is treated by the <term>Analytical Jurists</term> as a word distorted from its true meaning by an inaccurate metaphorical expansion, and the kind of disrespect with which they speak of it is quite remarkable. But I believe that, if dignity and importance can properly be attributed to a word, there are in our day few words more dignified and more important than Law, in the sense of the constant sequence of phenomena—whether physical, mental, or even political-economic. With this meaning, ‘law’ enters into much of modern thought and has almost become the condi-

The statement that, in all languages, Law primarily means the command of a <term>Sovereign</term>, and has been applied derivatively to the orderly sequences of Nature is extremely difficult to verify; and it may be doubted whether its value, if it were true, would repay the labor of establishing its truth. The order of Nature has unquestionably been regarded as determined by a Sovereign command. Many persons to whom the pedigree of much of modern thought is traceable, conceived the particles of matter which make up the universe as obeying the commands of a personal God just as literally as subjects obey the commands of a sovereign through fear of a <term>penal sanction</term>. <header>LAW IN A JURIDICAL SENSE. LECT. XIII.</header>

Order in the external world has strongly influenced the view of laws proper held by much of the civilized part of mankind. The Roman theory of <term>Natural Law</term> has affected the entire history of law, and this famous theory is, in fact, composed of two elements: one provided by an early perception—Greek in origin—of a certain order and regularity in physical nature, and the other attributable to an early perception—Roman in origin—of a certain order and uniformity among the customs of the human race. I do not need to repeat the proof of this here, which I attempted to provide in a volume published some years ago. No one is free to criticize individuals or communities for using words in any sense they please, or with as many meanings as they like; however, the duty of the scientific inquirer is to distinguish the various meanings of an important word from one another, to select the meaning appropriate to his own purposes, and to consistently use the word in that sense and no other during his investigations. The laws that concern the student of <term>Jurisprudence</term> today are undoubtedly either the actual commands of <term>Sovereigns</term>—understood as the portion of the community endowed with irresistible coercive force—or else they are the practices of humanity brought under the formula "a law is a command" with the help of the principle, "whatever the Sovereign permits, is his command." The law that prescribes only a single act is not a true law, but is instead distinguished as an "occasional" or "particular" command. Law, thus defined and limited, is the subject matter of <term>Jurisprudence</term> as conceived by the <term>Analytical Jurists</term>. At present, we are only concerned with the foundations of their system; and the questions I wish to raise in the present Lecture are these: has the force that compels obedience to a law always been of such a nature that it can reasonably be identified with the coercive force of the <term>Sovereign</term>, and have laws always been characterized by that generality which, it is said, alone connects them with physical laws or general formulas describing the facts of nature? These inquiries may seem to lead us far afield, but I trust you will perceive in the end that they have interest and importance, and that they throw light on the limits which must be assigned in certain cases—not to the theoretical soundness, but to the practical value—of the speculations we have been discussing.

This is among the most interesting parts of his writings, and his wisdom and originality are nowhere more strikingly demonstrated. The problem had become much more complex than it was when Hobbes wrote, and even more than at the time of Bentham’s earlier publications. Hobbes, a partisan in England, was a keen scientific observer of the political phenomena of the Continent, and there the political conditions open to his observation were (setting England aside) practically limited to despotism and anarchy. But, by the time Austin wrote, England—which Hobbes probably considered the ground on which the battle for his principles was to be fought out—had long since become a "limited monarchy," an expression disliked by Hobbes’s successors almost as much as the *thing* was by Hobbes himself; and moreover, the influences of the first French Revolution were beginning to take effect. France had lately become a limited monarchy, and almost all the other continental states had given signs of becoming so. The complex political mechanism of the United States had arisen on the other side of the Atlantic, and the even more complicated systems of the German and Swiss Confederations had emerged in continental Europe. And, in speaking in this last sentence of groups not numerous enough to be political, I have introduced the most remarkable exception allowed by Austin to the rule that Sovereignty is universal among mankind. Now, since it is not a part of another and larger community, the society formed by the parents and children is clearly an independent society; and, since the rest of its members habitually obey its chief, this independent society would form a political society, if the number of its members were not extremely small. But, since the number of its members is extremely small, it would, I believe, be considered a society in a state of nature; that is, a society consisting of persons not in a state of subjection. The effect of this passage, then, is that a society may be too small to allow for the application of the theory. The use of his terminology, Austin says, would be ridiculous in such a case. I believe I shall be able to point out to you the significance of this appeal to our sense of absurdity, which is generally a most dangerous criterion; but at present, I merely ask you to note the seriousness of the admission. This is because the form of authority to which it refers—the authority of the Patriarch or <term>Paterfamilias</term> over his family—is, at least according to one modern theory, the element or germ out of which all permanent power of man over man has been gradually developed.

<header>THE PUNJAUB. LECT. XIII.</header>

but because it happens to be the most modern relevant precedent. My example is the Indian province called the Punjab, the Country of the Five Rivers, as it existed for about a quarter of a century before its annexation to the British Indian Empire. After passing through every imaginable phase of anarchy and dormant anarchy, it fell under the reasonably consolidated rule of a half-military, half-religious oligarchy known as the Sikhs. The Sikhs themselves were later subjected to the rule of a single chieftain belonging to their order, Runjeet Singh. At first sight, there could be no more perfect embodiment of sovereignty, as Austin conceived it, than Runjeet Singh. He was absolutely despotic. Except occasionally on his wild frontier, he maintained perfect order. He could have commanded anything; the slightest disobedience to his commands would have been followed by death or mutilation, and the vast majority of his subjects knew this perfectly well. Yet I doubt whether even once in his entire life he issued a command that Austin would call a law. He took a massive share of the land's produce as his revenue. He raided villages that resisted his demands, and he executed a great many men. He raised large armies; he possessed all the instruments of power and exercised them in various ways. But he never made a law. The rules which regulated the life of his subjects were

<header>LECT. XIII. RUNJEET SINGH.</header>

I do not for a moment assert that the existence of such a state of political society falsifies Austin’s theory, as a theory. The great maxim by which objections to it are dismissed is, as I have so often said before, ‘What the Sovereign permits, he commands.’ The Sikh despot permitted heads of households and village elders to prescribe rules; therefore, these rules were his commands and true laws. Now we can see that an answer of this kind might have some force if it were made to an English lawyer who denied that the Sovereign in England had ever commanded the Common law. The Crown and Parliament command it because the Crown and Parliament permit it; and the proof that they permit it is that they could change it. As a matter of fact, since the objection was first advanced, the Common law has been largely encroached upon by Act of Parliament, and, in our own day, it is possible that it may come to owe the whole of its binding force to statute. But my Oriental example shows that the difficulty felt by the old lawyers about the Common law may have once deserved more respect than it received from Hobbes
