<header>RUNJEET SINGH. LECT. XIII.</header>

and his successors. Runjeet Singh never dreamed, nor could he have dreamed, of changing the civil rules under which his subjects lived. He was probably as strong a believer in the independent, binding force of such rules as the elders themselves who applied them. An Eastern or Indian legal theorist, if told that Runjeet Singh commanded these rules, would find the idea just as absurd as those instances where Austin admits that an appeal to absurdity is legitimate. The theory remains true in such a case, but the truth is merely a matter of words.

You must not suppose that I have been indulging in a merely curious speculation about a few extreme cases where the theory of Sovereignty, and of Law founded on it, cannot be applied without straining the language. In the first place, the Punjab under Runjeet Singh may be taken as a model for all Eastern communities in their native state during their rare intervals of peace and order. They have always been despotisms, and the commands of the despots at their head—however harsh and cruel they might be—have always been implicitly obeyed. However, these commands, except when they served to organize the administrative machinery for the collection of revenue, have not been true laws; they have belonged to the class Austin calls occasional or particular commands. The reality is that the one thing that dissolves local and domestic customs in those parts of the world of which we have any real

<header>LECT. XIII. ANCIENT STATE OF THE WORLD.</header>

It is important to observe that, for the purposes of the present inquiry, the state of political society which I have described as Indian or Oriental is a far more trustworthy clue to the former condition of the greater part of the world than the modern social organization of Western Europe, as we see it before our eyes. It is perhaps not an unreasonable impression that Sovereignty was simpler and more easily identified in the ancient world than in the modern world. The critic of Hobbes and Austin, whom I quoted before, writes:

> 'In every state of which we read, whether Greek, Phoenician, Italian, or Asiatic, there was a Sovereign of some sort whose authority was absolute while it lasted;'

and he adds that:

> 'if Hobbes had tried to write an imaginary history of mankind, he could not have constructed one better suited to his purpose than the history of the foundation and establishment of the Roman Empire.'

<header>ANCIENT EMPIRES. LECT. XIII.</header>

We know something of the Assyrian and Babylonian Empires from Jewish records, and something of the Median and Persian Empires from Greek records. We learn from these that they were in the main tax-taking empires. We know that they raised enormous revenues from their subjects. We know that, for occasional wars of conquest, they levied vast armies from populations spread over immense areas. We know that they exacted the most implicit obedience to their occasional commands, or punished disobedience with the utmost cruelty. We know that the monarchs at their head were constantly dethroning petty kings and even transplanting whole communities. But amid all this, it is clear that in the main they interfered but little with the everyday religious or civil life of the groups to which their subjects belonged. They did not legislate. The 'royal statute' and 'firm decree' which has been preserved to us as a sample of the 'law of the Medes and Persians which does not change' is not a law at all in the modern juridical acceptation of the term. It is what Austin would call a 'particular command'—a sudden, spasmodic, and temporary interference with ancient, diverse customs left in general undisturbed. <page-num>385</page-num>
<header>LECT. XIII. LIMITS OF ANALYTICAL SYSTEM.</header>

Athenian Empire belonged to the same class of sovereignties as the Empire of the Great King. The Athenian Assembly made true laws for residents on Attic territory, but the dominion of Athens over its subject cities and islands was clearly a tax-collecting empire, as distinguished from a legislating one.

The difficulty of employing Austin’s terminology for these great governments is obvious enough. How can it contribute to clear thinking to speak of Jewish law as being commanded at one period by the Great King at Susa? The cardinal rule of the Analytical Jurists—"what the Sovereign permits, he commands"—remains verbally true; however, against its application in such a case, there lies an appeal to a higher tribunal whose jurisdiction Austin allows: our sense of the ridiculous.

I have now reached the point at which I can conveniently state my own opinion of the practical limitations which must be applied to the system of the Analytical Jurists, in order that it may possess—I will not say theoretical truth, but—practical value. The Western world, to which they limited their attention, must be understood as having undergone two sets of changes. The states of modern Europe must be conceived as having been formed in a manner different from the great empires of antiquity (except one), and from the modern empires and kingdoms of the East, and a new order of ideas on the subject of

<header>ORIGINAL COMMUNITIES. LECT. XIII.</header>

Unless these changes had taken place, I do not believe that the system would ever have been conceived in the minds of its authors. Wherever these changes have not occurred, I do not believe the application of the system has any value.

The most nearly universal fact that can be asserted regarding the origin of the political communities called States is that they were formed by the merging of groups, with the original group having been, in no case, smaller than the patriarchal family. But in the communities which came into existence before the Roman Empire, and in those which have been slightly affected by it or not at all, this merging was soon halted. There are some traces of the process everywhere. The hamlets of Attica merged to form the Athenian State; and the primitive Roman State was formed by the merging of the tiny communities on the original hills. In very many Indian village-communities, there are signs of smaller elements combining to make them up. But this earlier merging soon stops. # LECT. XIII. FORMATION OF MODERN STATES. 387

ing great masses of population. But, independent of the Roman Empire and its influence, the separate local life of the small societies included in these great states was not extinguished or even much weakened. They continued just as the Indian village-community has continued; indeed, even in their most glorious forms, they belonged essentially to that type of society. But the process of change by which the states of the modern world were formed has been materially different from this. The smaller groups have been much more completely broken up and absorbed into the larger ones; these larger groups have again been swallowed up into even wider ones, and those into still wider areas. It is true that local life and village customs have not decayed everywhere to the same degree. There is much more of them in Russia than in Germany; more of them in Germany than in England; and more of them in England than in France. But on the whole, whenever the modern state is formed, it is an assembly of fragments considerably smaller than those which made up empires of the earlier type, and they are considerably more similar to one another.

It would be rash to state confidently which is the cause and which is the consequence, but unquestionably this more complete breaking down <note>The original text uses "trituration," referring to the process of grinding or reducing something to fine particles.</note> in modern societies of the groups that once lived with an independent life has proceeded alongside much greater activity in legislation. <header>THE VILLAGE COUNCIL. LECT. XIII.</header>

dition of an <term>Aryan race</term> reveals itself either through historical records or through the survival of its ancient institutions, the organ which in the elementary group corresponds to what we call the <term>legislature</term>, is everywhere discernible. It is the Village Council, sometimes owning a responsibility to the entire body of villagers, sometimes disclaiming it, sometimes overshadowed by the authority of a hereditary chief, but never altogether obscured. From this embryo have sprung all the most famous legislatures of the world: the Athenian <term>Ekklesia</term>, the Roman <term>Comitia</term>, Senate and Prince, and our own <term>Parliament</term>—the type and parent of all the "collegiate sovereignties" (as <term>Austin</term> would call them) of the modern world, or in other words, of all governments in which sovereign power is exercised by the people or shared between the people and the King. Yet, if we examine the undeveloped form of this organ of <term>State</term>, its legislative faculty is its least distinct and least energetic faculty. In point of fact, as I have observed elsewhere, the various shades of the power lodged with the Village Council, under the influence of the ideas proper to it, are not distinguished from one another, nor does the mind see a clear difference between making a law, declaring a law, and punishing an offender against a law. If the powers of this body must be described by modern names, that which lies most in the background is the legislative power.

<page-num>390</page-num>
<header>THE LAW OF THE KING LECT. XIII.</header>

A long series of earlier conquerors have swept over the village communities, but after including them in a nominal empire, they have imposed no permanent obligation beyond the payment of tax or tribute. If on some rare occasions they have attempted the forced religious conversion of subjugated populations, the temples and the rites have been, at most, changed in the villages, while the civil institutions have been left untouched. Here in England, the struggle between the central and the local power has followed a very different course. We can see plainly that the King's law and the King's courts have been perpetually contending against the local law and the local courts, and the victory of the King's law has brought with it the long series of Acts of Parliament founded on its principles. The whole process can only be called legislation—ever increasing in energy—until the ancient, diverse law of the country has been all but completely abolished, and the old usages of the independent communities have degenerated into the customs of manors or into mere habits having no legal sanction.

There is much reason to believe that the Roman Empire was the source of the influences which have led, immediately or ultimately, to the formation of highly centralized, actively legislating states. It was the first great dominion which did not merely tax, but legislated as well. The process was spread

<header>LECT. XIII. ROMAN LEGISLATION.</header>

If I had to determine the eras of its beginning and end, I would place them roughly at the issuance of the first *Edictum Provinciale* and the extension of Roman citizenship to all subjects of the empire; however, the foundations of the change were undoubtedly laid long before the first period and continued in some ways long after the last. But, in the result, a vast and varied mass of customary law was broken up and replaced by new institutions. Seen in this light, the Roman Empire is accurately described in the Prophecy of Daniel: it devoured, broke in pieces, and trampled the residue with its feet.

The invasion of the barbarian races into the Empire spread many of the primitive tribal and village ideas throughout the communities included within it—ideas which those communities had previously lost. Nevertheless, no society directly or indirectly influenced by the Empire has been entirely like the societies formed on that more ancient system which the unchanging nature of the East has preserved until we can actually observe it. In all commonwealths of the first kind, Sovereignty is more or less distinctly associated with legislative power, and the direction in which this power was to be exercised was, in a considerable number of countries, clearly mapped out by the jurisprudence which the Empire left behind. <header>THE FORCE OF LAW. LECT. XIII.</header>

expelled, was obviously the great dissolver of local usage everywhere. There are thus two types of organized political society. In the more ancient of these, the great majority of men derive their rules of life from the customs of their village or city, but they occasionally—though most implicitly—obey the commands of an absolute ruler who takes taxes from them but never legislates. In the other, and the one with which we are most familiar, the Sovereign is increasingly active in legislating on his own principles, while local custom and ideas are constantly decaying. It seems to me that in the transition from one of these political systems to another, laws have distinctly changed their character. The force, for example, which supports the law, can only be called the same by a significant stretch of language. Customary law—a subject on which all of Austin's <note>John Austin, a prominent legal philosopher known for legal positivism.</note> remarks seem to me relatively unproductive—is not obeyed in the same way that enacted law is obeyed. When it exists over small areas and within small natural groups, the penal sanctions on which it depends are partly public opinion and partly superstition, but to a far greater extent, an instinct almost as blind and unconscious as that which produces the involuntary movements of our bodies. The actual force required to ensure conformity with custom is incredibly small. When, however, the rules that must be obeyed emanate from an authority external to the small

<header>LECT. XIII. LAW AND ORDER.</header>

natural group and forming no part of it, they take on a character completely different from that of a customary rule. They lose the support of superstition, probably that of opinion, and certainly that of spontaneous impulse. The force behind the law becomes, therefore, purely coercive force to an extent quite unknown in societies of a more primitive type. Moreover, in many communities, this force must operate at a very great distance from the majority of the people subject to it; thus, the Sovereign who wields it must deal with broad classes of acts and with broad classes of persons, rather than with isolated acts and individuals. Among the consequences of this necessity are many of the characteristics sometimes supposed to be inseparable from laws: their indifference, their inexorability, and their generality.

And as the concept of Force associated with laws has changed, so also, I think, has the concept of Order. In the basic social groups formed by men of the Aryan race, nothing can be more monotonous than the routine of village custom. Nevertheless, inside the households which together make up the village community, the tyranny of usage is replaced by the tyranny of paternal authority. Outside each doorway is ancient custom blindly obeyed; inside is the <term>Patria Potestas</term> exercised by a half-civilized man over his wife, child, and slave. To the extent then that laws are commands,

The Force, then, which is at the back of laws was not always the same. The Order which goes with them was not always the same. They have only gradually attracted to themselves the attributes which seem essential to them, not only in the popular view but to the penetrating eye of the <term>Analytical Jurist</term>. Their generality and their dependence on the coercive force of a <term>Sovereign</term> are the result of the great territorial area of modern States, of the fragmentation <note>comminution</note> of the sub-groups which compose them, and above all of the example and influence of the Roman Commonwealth under Assembly, Senate, and Prince, which from very early times was distinguished from all other dominations and powers in that it broke up more thoroughly that which it devoured.

<header>LECT. XIII. HOBBES AND BENTHAM.</header>

Hobbes's great doctrine is clearly the result of a generalization that he had unrivaled opportunities to make in that day; during his intellectual prime, he spent as much time on the Continent as in England, first as a traveling tutor and later as an exile fleeing from civil unrest. Independent of English affairs—which he certainly viewed as a strong partisan—the phenomena he observed were governments rapidly centralizing themselves, local privileges and jurisdictions in extreme decay, and old historical bodies, such as the French Parliaments, tending at the time to become hotbeds of anarchy. In his view, the only hope for order was found in royal power. These were among the obvious results of the wars that ended in the Peace of Westphalia. The old, diverse local activity of feudal or quasi-feudal society was everywhere weakened or destroyed; if it had continued, the system of this great thinker would almost certainly never have seen the light of day. We have heard of a "village Hampden," <note>A reference to John Hampden, symbolizing a local hero who resists petty tyranny.</note> but a "village Hobbes" is inconceivable. By the time Bentham wrote, and throughout his career, the conditions that suggest the *Analytical System of Jurisprudence* presented themselves even more distinctly. <page-num>396</page-num>
### ANALYSIS AND HISTORY. LECT. XIII.

France. There had never before in the modern world been so striking an example of the proposition that what the <term>Sovereign</term> permits, he commands. This is because he could at any time replace his tacit permission with an express command. Nor had there been so impressive a lesson in the far-reaching and, on the whole, most beneficial results that might be expected from the increased activity of Sovereigns in actual legislation.

No geniuses of such a high rank so completely separated themselves from history as <term>Hobbes</term> and <term>Bentham</term>. To me, at least, they appear to be completely under the impression that the world had always been more or less as they saw it. <term>Bentham</term> could never escape the idea that imperfect or distorted applications of his principles had produced many things with which they had nothing whatever to do. I know of no more striking instance of a historical misconception (though at the time a very natural one) than <term>Hobbes</term>’s comparison of privileged corporations and organized local groups to the parasites which the physiology then becoming fashionable had shown to live in the internal membranes of the human body. We now know that, if we are forced to use a physiological illustration, these groups must instead be compared to the primary cells out of which the entire human body has been built.

But, even if the <term>Analytical Jurists</term> failed to see a great deal which can only be explained with the help of his-

He uses the centralization of French law as a prime example of the Sovereign's power to command through both action and permission.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Analytical Jurists, specifically how their lack of historical perspective did not prevent them from creating a highly effective and consistent system of law and sovereignty.</meta>

<page-num>397</page-num>
<header>LECT. XIII. INFLUENCE OF ANALYTICAL SYSTEM.</header>

tory, they saw a great deal which even in our day is imperfectly seen by those who, so to speak, let themselves drift with history. <term>Sovereignty</term> and <term>Law</term>, regarded as facts, had only gradually assumed a shape in which they answered to the conception of them formed by <term>Hobbes</term>, <term>Bentham</term>, and <term>Austin</term>, but the correspondence really did exist by their time, and was tending constantly to become more perfect. They were thus able to frame a juridical terminology which had for one virtue that it was rigidly consistent with itself, and for another that, if it did not completely express facts, the qualifications of its accuracy were never serious enough to deprive it of value and tended moreover to become less and less important as time went on. No conception of law and society has ever removed such a mass of undoubted delusion. The force at the disposal of <term>Sovereigns</term> did in fact act largely through laws as understood by these <term>Jurists</term>, but it acted confusedly, hesitatingly, with many mistakes and vast omissions. They for the first time saw all that it was capable of effecting, if it was applied boldly and consistently. All that has followed is a testimony to their sagacity. I do not know a single law-reform effected since <term>Bentham</term>’s day which cannot be traced to his influence; but a still more startling proof of the clearing of the brain produced by this <term>Analytical System</term>, even in an earlier stage, may be found in <term>Hobbes</term>. In his *Dialogue of the Common Laws*, he

<summary>This page explains that despite their historical oversights, the Analytical Jurists developed a consistent legal terminology that accurately reflected the growing power of the state. Their work cleared away legal delusions and laid the groundwork for modern law reform, as seen in the influence of Bentham and the early insights of Hobbes.</summary>

<keywords>Hobbes, Bentham, Austin, Sovereignty, Law, Jurists, Dialogue of the Common Laws, Analytical System, law reform, legal terminology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Hobbes’s foresight regarding legal reform and moves into the relationship between legislative activity and the analytical and utilitarian theories of law.</meta>

<page-num>398</page-num>
<header>LEGISLATION IN MODERN STATES. LECT. XIII.</header>

argues for a fusion of law and equity, a registration of titles to land, and a systematic penal code—three measures which we are on the eve of seeing carried out at this very moment.

The principal fact in the structure of modern States is the energy of legislatures. Until this fact existed, I do not believe, as I have said, that the system of <term>Hobbes</term>, <term>Bentham</term>, and <term>Austin</term> could have been conceived; wherever it shows itself imperfectly, I think that the system is never properly appreciated. The relative neglect with which German writers have treated it seems to me to be explained by the comparative newness of legislative activity in <term>Germany</term>. It is, however, impossible to remark upon the connection between legislation and the <term>analytical theory of law</term> without having the mind drawn to the famous addition which <term>Bentham</term> and <term>Austin</term> grafted onto the theories of <term>Hobbes</term>. This addition consisted in linking them with the doctrine or <term>theory of utility</term>—of the greatest happiness of the greatest number, considered as the basis of law and morals. What, then, is the connection, essential or historical, between the <term>utilitarian theory</term> and the <term>analytical theory of law</term>? I certainly do not pretend to be able, especially at the close of a lecture, to cover a subject of such extent and difficulty completely, but I have a few words to say about it. To me, the most interesting thing about the <term>theory of Utility</term> is

<summary>The author identifies the vigor of modern legislatures as the practical foundation for the legal theories of Hobbes, Bentham, and Austin, and introduces the connection between analytical jurisprudence and the utilitarian principle of the "greatest happiness."</summary>
<keywords>legislation, analytical theory of law, utilitarian theory, Hobbes, Bentham, Austin, Germany, penal code, law and equity, greatest happiness of the greatest number</keywords>

--- Page 406 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the theory of Utility, specifically addressing its reliance on the concept of equality as a practical legislative tool.</meta>

<page-num>399</page-num>
<header>LECT. XIII. THE UTILITARIAN PHILOSOPHY.</header>

that it presupposes the theory of <term>Equality</term>. The greatest number is the greatest number of men taken as units; "one shall only count for one," said <term>Bentham</term> emphatically and over and over again. In fact, the most conclusive objection to the doctrine would consist in denying this equality; and I have myself heard an Indian <term>Brahmin</term> dispute it on the grounds that, according to the clear teaching of his religion, a <term>Brahmin</term> was entitled to twenty times as much happiness as anybody else. Now, how did this fundamental assumption of equality—which (I may observe) broadly distinguishes <term>Bentham</term>’s theories from some systems with which it is supposed to share the reproach of having pure selfishness for its basis—how did it suggest itself to <term>Bentham</term>’s mind? He saw plainly—nobody more clearly—that men are not, as a matter of fact, equal; the proposition that men are by nature equal he expressly denounced as an <term>anarchical sophism</term>. Whence then came the equality which is a postulate of his famous doctrine about the <term>greatest happiness of the greatest number</term>? I venture to think that this doctrine is nothing more than a working rule of <term>legislation</term>, and that in this form it was originally conceived by <term>Bentham</term>. Assume a numerous and tolerably homogeneous community—assume a <term>Sovereign</term> whose commands take a legislative shape—assume great energy, actual or potential, in this legislature—the only possible, the

<summary>The author argues that Bentham's "greatest happiness" principle relies on a postulate of equality that Bentham viewed not as a natural fact, but as a practical working rule for legislation. This distinguishes Utilitarianism from other systems based on pure selfishness, despite Bentham's rejection of "natural rights" as sophistry.</summary>
<keywords>Jeremy Bentham, Utilitarianism, Equality, Legislation, Indian Brahmin, Anarchical Sophism, Greatest Happiness Principle, Sovereign</keywords>

--- Page 407 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that Bentham’s "greatest happiness" principle is primarily a practical rule for legislation, born from the distance between the state and the individual.</meta>

<page-num>400</page-num>
<header>BENTHAM AS A MORALIST. LECT. XIII.</header>

the only conceivable principle which can guide legislation on a large scale is the greatest happiness of the greatest number. It is, in fact, a condition of legislation which—like certain characteristics of laws—has grown out of the distance from which sovereign power acts upon subjects in modern political societies, and from the necessity under which it is thereby placed of neglecting differences, even real differences, between the units of which they are composed. <term>Bentham</term> was, in truth, neither a jurist nor a moralist in the proper sense of the word. He theorizes not on law but on legislation; when carefully examined, he may be seen to be a legislator even in morals. No doubt his language seems sometimes to imply that he is explaining moral phenomena; in reality, he wishes to alter or rearrange them according to a working rule gathered from his reflections on legislation. This transfer of his working rule from legislation to morality seems to me the true ground of the criticisms to which <term>Bentham</term> is justly open as an analyst of moral facts.

<summary>The author argues that Bentham's "greatest happiness" principle is a rule of legislation necessitated by the scale of modern states, and that Bentham incorrectly applied this legislative logic to the field of morality.</summary>
<keywords>Bentham, greatest happiness, legislation, moralist, jurist, sovereign power, morality, analyst of moral facts</keywords>
